Apple’s latest watchOS beta includes its new always-on Audio Intelligence features
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
18:31:01 UTC
03:31:01 KST

Starting today, Siri can tune into the Apple Watch’s mic with Audio Intelligence features that are available in the new watchOS 27.3 developer beta rolling out now. The two new additions are Siri Recap, which uses “ambient listening” to generate summaries of your conversation, and Live Rewind, which generates text transcripts of the last 15 seconds of audio your watch’s mic detected.
Apple’s latest watchOS beta includes its new always-on Audio Intelligence features
Audio Intelligence lets Siri ‘rewind’ and summarize audio from your Watch’s mic.
- Live Rewind, which can help users recall what was just said in conversation. A double-press of the Digital Crown shows the previous 15 seconds of a conversation as a text snippet, so the user can catch something they may have missed or are less familiar with. Users can ask Siri about the content of the text or save it to the Siri app to revisit later.
- Siri Recap, which can create high-level summaries of conversations that users can review later to jog their memory. When Siri Recap is turned on, after a conversation, Apple Intelligence automatically generates a title and key points in the new Siri app, allowing users to be more present and engaged in the moment.
When it comes to the implications of an ambient, always-listening AI, Apple has released this Audio Intelligence
Privacy Overview (pdf) about how it plans to protect users’ privacy when their watch is listening around the clock. It goes into detail about Apple’s claims that users’ raw audio “is handled within dedicated hardware, is not saved as a file, and is not accessible to the operating system, apps, or Apple.” According to Apple, “If you use iCloud with two-factor authentication and have a device passcode, any Siri Recap summary or Live Rewind text snippet you choose to save syncs across your devices end-to-end encrypted in the Siri app.”

OpenAI says ChatGPT is catching up to Google in search
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
18:30:42 UTC
03:30:42 KST

In many circles, “Ask Chat” has replaced “Google it,” and that’s reflected in a new announcement from OpenAI: the volume of search queries run through ChatGPT now equals 20 percent of the volume run through Google.
OpenAI says ChatGPT is catching up to Google in search
ChatGPT processes one-fifth the volume of search queries as Google, per OpenAI.
Adam Fry, OpenAI’s consumer product lead for ChatGPT, made the announcement at a press event on Monday. He said the company defines a search as any time a user inputs a query that results in a web search, as opposed to tasks like defining a term or a simple how-to explanation. (Fry did not specify the source of the Google search volume number.)
Having one-fifth as many searches as Google is still a long way from overtaking it, but for comparison, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella said during Google’s 2023 antitrust trial that Bing had about 3 percent of the search engine market share after trying for more than a decade. During the trial, Google described AI as a potential disruptor, arguing ChatGPT was poised to seriously compete with it — now, OpenAI seems to agree. (OpenAI likely uses Microsoft Bing for a significant part of its searches, along with its in-house search index, although there is still some speculation that it routes some queries through Google.)
The news comes amid an ongoing search engine shake-up, as Google continues to reduce conventional referrals to third-party sites and invests heavily in AI tools — not just AI Overview but also additional generative AI capabilities, including personalized search results with visualizations and charts. But as search queries increasingly route through OpenAI and other AI labs’ services, other companies are investing in the same types of AI-focused search experience, even if they call it something else: for OpenAI, it’s “Intelligent UI,” or creating a visual and interactive interface within a chatbot response. “That’s where the future of this technology goes. You’re talking to a canvas,” Fry said.
Much like Google, OpenAI sees a potential path to profitability through ads, particularly as it approaches an IPO potentially slated for early next year. OpenAI has defended its ads in the past, and Fry told reporters they hadn’t produced a lasting backlash. “Ads have not negatively impacted the product or the usage of the product,” he said, adding that “most people do not seem to mind ads in the product.”
OpenAI announced last month that the company would begin introducing images of sponsored products and services in ChatGPT rather than just a company’s name, logo, and a link to its product like before.
“Ads have maybe shown us that we could be doing this more, actually,” Fry said.

That means searching for “espresso station” could present you with ads for espresso, machines, beans, or mugs, while a search for a “home office setup” may yield ads for monitors, desks, or office chairs. Reddit says it’s piloting the new search ads with a select number of advertisers to start.

Indie games are missing launch dates because Steam is backed up
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
18:14:50 UTC
03:14:50 KST

Many indie game developers have recently posted on social media about unexpectedly long approval wait times for their game submissions on Steam, with some of the approvals causing developers to miss their planned launch dates.
Indie games are missing launch dates because Steam is backed up
One developer has been waiting for weeks on approval for a game it planned to release today.
For example, Studio Daimon, the team behind an upcoming title called Food Devils, had planned to launch the game in early access today after submitting its build on October 12th. But the game is “still awaiting approval,” according to a post from game director Alfred Kamon. “Even now, we don’t know when Steam will give us the final OK to finally release our game. We sent another ticket last week saying that the situation was urgent and that we might miss our release date because of this, but it also got ignored.”
In October, publisher 2 Left Thumbs said it missed its planned launch of Wheel Make It “by nearly two weeks” due to the long wait for build reviews. You can read some details about the studio’s setbacks, including failing a review for a “one single option in the menu” not being accessible with a controller, in a Steam post. There are lots of other recent posts about review build waits as well.
Valve says on a Steam developer website that a review of a game’s store presence “typically takes 3-5 business days to complete,” though the company suggests submitting a page for review “at least 7 business days before you want it live to account for potential changes you’ll need to make.” However, as of late, Steam has seemingly been grappling with a sharp increase in games; October’s Steam Next Fest had 7,240 games, a 65.2 percent increase over June’s Steam Next Fest, according to How to Market a Game. A Reddit user also found that 49.9 percent of all games released since Valve started asking developers in 2024 to label games that use AI now have an AI disclosure.
Kamon and 2 Left Thumbs suspect the longer waits are due to “slop” slowing down the process. “We can only assume it’s because Steam’s review queue is clogged up by AI slop, but the whole process is now taking several weeks for some developers,” said Kamon.
Valve didn’t immediately reply to a request for comment.
Itch.io has also been dealing with a rise in games, with the platform saying last weekend that “The number of game pages being created per-day has doubled in the past 2 months.” Last night, the platform said it “Just deleted a couple terabytes of ai slop 😌”

Google’s Gemini-powered Home Speaker is already on sale for 20 percent off
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
18:11:49 UTC
03:11:49 KST

If you’re looking for a good smart speaker, the Google Home Speaker is down to around $79 ($20 off) in all four colors at Best Buy, Walmart, and Target. That’s the best price we’ve seen for the Gemini-powered speaker, which launched in June and represents Google’s first smart speaker in several years.
Google’s Gemini-powered Home Speaker is already on sale for 20 percent off
Google’s latest smart speaker offers surprisingly good sound for its size, Gemini-powered voice controls, and more.
In her review, my colleague Jennifer Pattison Tuohy noted that Google’s new Home Speaker offers a nice balance of sound quality, size, and design. It’s smaller than the prior-gen Nest Audio, making it easy to place anywhere in your home. However, despite its small size, it produces loud, clear audio with 360-degree sound. Our own David Pierce even found it sounded cleaner, louder, and sharper than Amazon’s similarly priced Echo Dot Max, though Tuohy preferred the latter’s fuller sound and stronger bass. The older Nest Audio still sounds better overall, but Google’s latest speaker takes up less space and costs slightly less.
The Google Home Speaker also offers a few useful smart home upgrades. It supports Matter and includes a Thread border router, allowing you to connect and control a wide range of compatible smart home devices. It’s also the first Google smart speaker that can double as an audio output for the Google TV Streamer 4K, letting you play TV audio through one or two speakers instead of your TV’s built-in speakers. Tuohy found it worked well and delivered clear dialogue, though sound quality varied depending on what she was watching.
One of its most notable features is support for Gemini, which is also available on older Google speakers and is much better at understanding natural language and complex requests than the old Google Assistant. In her testing, Tuohy found it could handle multiple smart home commands at once and respond to conversational requests, like turning down the air conditioner when she mentioned feeling too hot. It’s also particularly impressive at answering general knowledge questions, though it can be slow and unreliable at times. Keep in mind, too, that some features require a $10-a-month Google Home Premium subscription, including Gemini Live, which lets you have extended conversations with the assistant without repeating the wake word.

CrowdStrike to shell out nearly $500M for 2 AI security startups as the market for securing agents takes off
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
17:56:52 UTC
02:56:52 KST

Cybersecurity giant CrowdStrike is buying two AI security companies that expand its products to more broadly monitor, test, and cover AI models and agents, as companies across industries start rolling out AI agents and models wholesale.
CrowdStrike said it will buy Fiddler AI, which makes observability software for monitoring AI agents inside enterprises, and Dreadnode, which helps companies automate security testing specifically aimed at detecting flaws in AI models, apps and agents before they are set live.
The company is paying around $400 million for Fiddler AI, and about $95 million for Dreadnode, according to The Wall Street Journal.
The deals are happening at a time of significant upheaval in the cybersecurity space, especially as companies’ policies and processes lag behind AI adoption within their walls. Teams and individual employees alike are experimenting with AI tools, automating workflows, vibe-coding custom apps, and deploying armies of AI agents to do their bidding, which is understandably causing headaches for security teams.
CrowdStrike, whose products are used widely across enterprises and governments, is clearly trying to leverage its already significant heft and presence to capture a share of this quickly burgeoning market. It’s not alone, of course, as we’ve already seen several startups and cybersecurity vendors raising dozens of millions in funding to solve issues for companies, though most of these approaches are essentially based on the conviction that existing security software isn’t enough to monitor and control AI tools, connectors, or usage.
The company has itself been building detection and response controls, dubbed Falcon Guardian, to help companies see and understand what agents are doing on their devices, and these acquisitions seem to further flesh out its offerings.
Fiddler AI is going to be part of CrowdStrike’s Falcon platform as Falcon Observe, where it’ll ostensibly help the company’s clients evaluate agents before they’re deployed, understand their actions, and monitor what they do, and feed that information as telemetry into the broader platform. Dreadnode, meanwhile, will be integrated into the Falcon Guardian product, to which the startup’s software will feed its testing results as security policy recommendations.
Founded in 2018, Fiddler AI has raised a total of about $99 million to date, according to PitchBook. Dreadnode, founded in 2023, has raised $14 million so far.

How to write comparison content that doesn't age like milk
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
17:46:58 UTC
02:46:58 KST

How to write comparison content that doesn't age like milk
Contents
Every marketing lead I talk with sees potential of comparison content, but also see it as a double-edged sword they're not comfortable investing in.
The hesitation usually comes from some mix of (1) it feels unnatural to give your competitors their flowers on your own website, and (2) comparison pages come with maintenance overhead nobody wants to deal with.
Competitors ship things, change their pricing, get acquired (or acquire others), and all of a sudden the article you wrote in March is outdated in June.
So how do you walk the line between being fair (complimentary, even) to your competitors while still capturing demand & showing off your product's strengths? And how do you keep the content accurate once it's out in the world to avoid spreading misinformation?
Both are worth figuring out if you want to write comparison content that doesn't get dunked on. And both are things I'm here to give some answers to based on our experience writing comparison content at PostHog.
Why bother with comparison content?
Because it works.
Since 2021, over 25k people have signed up for PostHog after reading a comparison. Almost 50% did it within 24 hours of reading; ~75% within 30 days.
It makes sense why they overperform. Someone searching for this style of content has already decided they need something, the only question left is which one. Comparison content sits at the sweet spot in the funnel where that decision gets made, and your job is to help make it (ideally in your favour, but not always and that's okay. More on that later).
Our credentials
Technically, our first comparison was a roundup. The best open source analytics tools went live in October 2021 and is still our single most-read piece of comparison content, at 150k+ pageviews and counting.
It set the template for the early days: "best open source X" for session replay, A/B testing, and feature flags. Open source was one of our wedges, so that's what we compared on.
The first head-to-head, PostHog vs Amplitude, followed in May 2022. It still gets a few hundred readers a month, which is more than I can say for most things we published back in 2022. Matomo and Mixpanel came next.
2023 was the year it became a thing. Every new PostHog product came with a new set of competitors. Session Replay brought Hotjar, FullStory, and LogRocket. Feature Flags brought LaunchDarkly, GrowthBook, and Statsig. Web Analytics brought GA4.
We went from three to fourteen in a year, and the maintenance problem flared up. Three pages you can keep accurate by hand; fourteen, each with a feature table and pricing that changes every quarter, not so much. It's, to this day, a workflow we're constantly working on solving for and improving.
2024 was the alternatives year. A lot of our competitors got a "best X alternatives" piece: Hotjar, Statsig, Plausible, Matomo, and about fifteen more. We also launched error tracking, wrote PostHog vs Sentry in August, and watched it become our most-read head-to-head of all time within two years.
2025 and 2026 followed the products; Best Sentry alternatives and best error tracking tools for error tracking, best open source LLM observability tools for AI observability, and best Datadog alternatives as the first hint of what was coming next. Most recently we started doubling down on our observability stack: PostHog vs Langfuse and a full set of AI observability roundups (best AI observability tools, plus Langfuse, LangSmith, and Braintrust alternatives). Then logs: best log monitoring tools, Grafana Loki and Splunk alternatives, and PostHog vs Datadog.
This isn't an AEO fad for us; we were writing comparison content BeFoRe iT wAs cOoL, back when the answer engine was a guy named Greg on Stack Overflow.
And while they have shapeshifted plenty since then, we've stayed true to two principles:
- Do no harm. We don't trash-talk competitors, we don't straw-man them, and we admit when and where they're better than us. No sneaky shit, as our handbook puts it.
- Respect the reader's intelligence, and meet them where they are. A developer comparing tools is smart, busy, and already halfway towards a decision. Give them the answer as accurately and as directly as possible, and let them make the right call for their product and use case. Also, they can tell if you're generating AI slop comparisons, it's a bad look.
Be the comparison page you'd want to read
PostHog doesn't have every feature every competitor has (yet), and we're fine admitting it. Our credibility with the reader is worth more than whatever we'd "win" by fudging the truth.
We're not the solution for everyone. We are the best solution for someone (a lot of someones, actually), and the job is capturing their attention. Which means you will, occasionally, lose a deal on your own website: someone reads PostHog vs Competitor, concludes Competitor is the better fit, and goes and buys Competitor. We wish them godspeed and focus on our ICP anyway.
In practice, this means picking up a few uncomfortable habits:
Acknowledge your gaps and credit their wins.
"If you need X, they do it better than we do today" is a sentence you'll find on our pages. Softening it with what's coming is fine (a roadmap link, a "we're working on it"), as long as you're clear about where you are right now for the readers deciding today.
For example, earlier this year our articles admitted we were weaker on APM because we hadn't shipped distributed tracing or metrics yet. True at the time. We've since shipped a full APM suite, and the articles now say that instead. Our content matches our shipping, not just our ambitions.
Readers don't think less of you for crediting a competitor; they think more. It's the cheapest trust signal in content marketing, and almost nobody uses it. And as AI eats everything else about content, trust is one of the few things left you can't generate. Say the nice thing.
There's another reader to keep in mind, too: LLMs. When someone asks Claude "PostHog vs Sentry for a Next.js app," we assume the model pulls from sources it judges to be balanced. So be balanced. You want to read as a source, not an ad.
Being honest used to be a brand decision (one we've proudly stood behind), but now it might be a distribution decision too.
Write a "who should pick them" section.
If you can't write a fair paragraph about where your competitor wins and who they might work better for, you don't understand them well enough to compare. Go read their docs.
It's also the place to be honest about who you are and aren't building for: plenty of our competitors are openly built for marketers for example, and while lots of marketers run all their reporting on PostHog (yours truly included), we're still building for AI-pilled engineers first, and we say so.
The reader who isn't a fit should leave feeling helped, not hustled. Honest pages also don't make enemies: the fastest way to go viral for the wrong reasons is a dodgy comparison, and if you'd rather not be bashed on Hacker News or Twitter for being slimy, be fair.
Take corrections from competitors.
We've shipped edits because someone at a competitor emailed us, DMed us, or better yet, opened a PR directly against our blog. We welcome it and encourage it. (Yes, if you're a competitor reading this, this is an invitation.)
Here's an example from today:
Lucas Harras, a Senior Customer Success Architect at Amplitude, read our PostHog vs Amplitude page, found four features we'd marked as missing that Amplitude actually has, and opened a PR.
We merged it the same day, and because every comparison table renders from that file (more on that below), every page that mentions any of these features was corrected by the next deploy.
Sometimes you'll be offered a slice of humble pie, and it's important to eat it gracefully.
My litmus test for every comparison: if a competitor wrote this exact page about PostHog, how would I feel reading it? Would it feel like they'd done their homework, or would I feel misrepresented and annoyed? If it's the latter, it's probably worth trying again.
We haven't always gotten this right. Part of it is that not everyone uses the same words for the same features, and some of our competitors have several hundred of them. A "sort of" column doesn't exist, so every row is ultimately a judgment call, and we've made some wrong ones.
But we're committed to doing better every time.
How to maintain comparison pages
Staying accurate across ~90 comparison and roundup pages for years is an engineering problem, and we started treating it like one.
1. Every competitor is a centralized data file.
In the posthog.com repo there's a folder with one file per competitor: sentry.tsx, amplitude.tsx, hotjar.tsx, and so on. Each file holds everything we claim about that company, in one place; here's a simplified version of what that looks like:
Feature keys come from a shared dictionary, so "session replay with network payloads" means the same thing on the Hotjar page as the LogRocket page.
Most of our comparison tables render from these files through one component. When a competitor ships a feature, someone changes one line in one file and every page that mentions them updates on the next deploy. The repo is public, so competitors can see exactly what we say about them and open a pull request if we're wrong.
2. Scouts checks our homework
Data files solve "update once, publish everywhere", but they don't solve for "notice that something changed" – that's what we've used Scouts for.
We run two. The first watches our competitors: it reads the competitor files I mentioned above, visits each competitor's pricing and docs pages, and reports back when what they say and what we say have drifted apart.
The second watches us. It turns out a very embarrassing way to get a comparison wrong isn't overstating a competitor, but understating yourself. (I learned this the hard way… occupational hazard of shipping as fast as we do. *wink*)
So the second scout reads our comparison pages against our own changelog, docs, open PRs, and Slack threads, and flags anywhere we might be underselling ourselves.
A few things make both of them better than a script that pings you every time a page changes:
- It has memory between runs, so it doesn't re-flag the same thing every week, and it learns what "normal" looks like for each site.
- It's selective by design. Most runs end with nothing to report, and that's it working. It ignores changes that don't affect the overall comparison.
- It shows its evidence: what their page says now, what our file says, and a suggested edit, with sources.
- A human ships the fix. The scout posts a digest to Slack with a link to open a PR. A person decides whether the change is necessary, opens the PR, and merges it.
Here's an example of what this looks like in practice. First, the Slack digest:
Then the full report in our self-driving inbox:
And finally, a pull request ready to merge:
The scout does the noticing, a human does the deciding, and nobody needs to add a recurring "re-read every competitor's pricing page" task to their calendar. Amen to that.
Where we go from here
We haven't reached our final form yet (will we ever?). One of my goals this quarter is going back through all of this content and making it better, and I'm sure there's plenty we're still getting wrong.
But the point is we started. Five years, 90-ish pages, and tens of thousands of signups later, "write honestly about the people trying to eat our lunch" has turned out to be one of the best decisions we've made for our organic content strategy; not the most intuitive one, but still one of the best.
So if you've been circling the plunge, take it. Be fair, be accurate, and let a robot handle the maintenance so you don't have to. (May I suggest ours?)
And if you're a peer who's nailed this (or who has failed at it and has war stories and words of caution to share), my DMs are always open.
PostHog is your product's context layer. With a full suite of developer tools – AI observability, product analytics, session replay, feature flags, experiments, error tracking, logs, and more – PostHog ingests and stores all the data agents need to diagnose problems, uncover opportunities, and ship fixes. A data warehouse and CDP tie it all together, unifying that context into one source agents can read across. You can steer it all from Slack, the web app, the desktop (PostHog Desktop), or your own editor via the MCP.

Qwen 3.8 27B on my laptop can interpret model weights, too
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
17:40:00 UTC
02:40:00 KST

Qwen 3.8 27B on my laptop can interpret model weights, too
3rd November 2026
Yesterday’s hot paper was Sparks of Weight-Space Programming: LLMs can natively read & write neural network weights by Rikiya Takehi, Jayden Teoh, Alex Zhang, and Omar Khattab. The paper found that frontier models, in particular GPT-6 Astra, can both interpret and modify the raw weights of a machine learning model—literally a text description of the model containing a bunch of floating point numbers. I’ve been playing with one of the examples from the paper and I’m delighted to share that Qwen 3.8 27B running on my laptop can do this as well!
Weight-Space Programming
Here’s the highlight from their abstract, describing impressive capabilities for GPT-6 Astra and coining the term Weight-Space Programming for when LLMs analyze and modify model weights:
Just by looking at the raw weights, GPT-6 Astra can tell what a network was trained to do. And without any environment feedback, it has no trouble writing, from scratch, small game-playing networks that outplay networks trained with PPO for 100 million steps.
Likewise, asked to make Qwen3.5-0.8B better at math without gradients, Astra identifies an issue in its weights causing reasoning loops that Qwen’s own model card warns about, and fixes this by natively editing 0.016% of the weights. With the same sampling settings, this weight correction raises MATH-500 from 7.9% to 48.1% and generalizes out of the box to instruction following, raising IFEval by 3x.
All of this is possible without allowing Astra to write training loops. It uses its reasoning to figure out what each part of a neural network computes, or should compute, and changes the weights accordingly. We call this Weight-Space Programming.
The paper combines two topics that I think deserve to be treated separately. The first is what models can do with raw weights without running tools or executing code. The second explores how well models, once armed with code, can run operations to update and improve other models.
spaCy author Matthew Honnibal called the paper misleading for conflating the two, and Sean Goedecke deconstructed the Qwen3.5-0.8B update example as taking the same steps that a human ML researcher would use.
I’m going to focus on the no-tools question, which I think is the more interesting of the two.
The paper presents the first question as Can LLMs understand model behaviors from weights? They trained four neural networks to play simplified 10x10 grid versions of four classic computer games—Breakout, Pong, Pac-Man, and Freeway (I had to look that one up, it’s an Atari 2600 game from 1981). Then they fed those weights (as floating point numbers) to an LLM along with the following question:
The weights below belong to one policy network that was trained with reinforcement learning to play a game on this grid. Which game was it trained on? Work from the weights. You have no tools: no code execution, no calculator, nothing to run. This is a single response.
The GitHub repository that accompanies the paper is RikiyaT/lm_understand-logs. The exp1_which_game folder contains the prompts they used, with weights for the four games:
I checked and, aside from the weights, each prompt is identical to the others.
Trying it out with Qwen 3.8 27B and other models
The prompts on GitHub are self-contained. My LLM tool has a -f/--fragment option which can be passed the URL to a text file to use as a prompt, which means I can run any of the above like this:
llm -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/RikiyaT/lm_understand-logs/refs/heads/main/exp1_which_game/runs/breakout/prompt.txt \
  -m openrouter/moonshotai/kimi-k3.1-pro
This example requires the llm-openrouter plugin installed, and runs the Breakout prompt against Kimi K3.1 Pro via OpenRouter. Here’s what I got back from Kimi K3.1 Pro, which successfully guessed Breakout after 9m 41s and 19,493 reasoning tokens.
But how about Qwen 3.8 27B? That’s still my favorite model to run on my own laptop—it’s just 17GB (I use this version) and somehow feels close to the leading proprietary models of 2025.
I fired up LM Studio, ensured Qwen 3.8 27B was loaded with a long context window (the default 8192 context failed with errors), installed the llm-lmstudio plugin and ran this command:
LMSTUDIO_TIMEOUT=900 \
  llm -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/RikiyaT/lm_understand-logs/refs/heads/main/exp1_which_game/runs/pacman/prompt.txt \
  -m lmstudio/qwen/qwen3.8-27b
(I had to bump the timeout up using an environment variable, see this issue.)
After 46m 12s and 43,506 reasoning tokens, Qwen on my laptop returned a response that ended like this (here’s the reasoning trace):
Game: A simplified Pac-Man-style gridworld: an agent moves through a fixed 8x8 maze with central and corner wall blocks, moving objects/ghosts, and reward targets/pellets.
Description: The agent navigates a fixed maze-like 8x8 board, using current agent/moving-object/previous-frame cues to move through corridors, collect reward-target cells, and avoid moving objects or hazards.
Confidence: 72%
This is impressive. I tried it on two of the other games as well. It identified Pong (reasoning) in 1h 4m 54s:
Pong — specifically a Pong-like paddle/ball game where the agent controls a vertical paddle on the right side and must move up/down to intercept or return a moving ball/reward object.
Description:
The agent moves a right-side paddle vertically to hit or catch a moving ball-like reward target moving across the 8×8 field, while a left-side moving object/opponent and wall borders define the play; reward is obtained by successfully intercepting/returning/scoring the ball, and missing or failing to hit it is penalized.
Confidence:
75%
And Breakout (reasoning) in 52m 25s:
Game: Breakout, or an Arkanoid-like paddle/ball/brick game.
Description: The agent is a paddle on the bottom row and moves left/right to keep a ball in play; the ball moves around the upper grid, and bricks/reward targets in the top rows give reward when hit, while missing the ball/hitting the bottom wall ends the episode or gives penalty.
Confidence: 88%
Qwen failed at the Freeway prompt, which didn’t surprise me considering that game is more obscure than the other three. Here’s that transcript—Qwen thought it was “A simplified Pac-Man-like grid game: agent moves 4-directionally”.
The reasoning traces are fun to skim, and almost incomprehensible. Here’s a sample:
What about agent channel0? We see some large channel0 rows near row8 col7/8 and maybe bottom? Let’s list channel0 large: line436 input435 (row8 col7 ch0) -2.107,1.169,0.513...; line441 input440 (row8 col8 ch0) -0.376,0.010,1.564,3.855...; line445 input444 (row8 col8 ch4) 0.142,0.491...; line382? input381 ch0? Wait line382 input381: 381//5=76 (row7 col6), ch1? 381%5=1? 76 * 5=380, ch1. So not. Channel0 large maybe at row8 col7/8 only. Also top row? line44 input43 ch3, line49 ch3. Channel0 large maybe not many. Agent position could be near bottom-right? If the agent is typically at bottom-right?
That said though, I expect a sufficiently experienced human being armed with a notepad and a pencil could get there with enough effort.
It’s a cool paper, but some of the wider commentary I’ve seen around it does not feel justified. I’m not ready to declare that the age of source code is over and our future involves incomprehensible machines editing their own model weights in incomprehensible ways.

Qwen 3.8 27B on my laptop can interpret model weights, too
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
17:40:00 UTC
02:40:00 KST

Qwen 3.8 27B on my laptop can interpret model weights, too
3rd November 2026
Yesterday’s hot paper was Sparks of Weight-Space Programming: LLMs can natively read & write neural network weights by Rikiya Takehi, Jayden Teoh, Alex Zhang, and Omar Khattab. The paper found that frontier models, in particular GPT-6 Astra, can both interpret and modify the raw weights of a machine learning model—literally a text description of the model containing a bunch of floating point numbers. I’ve been playing with one of the examples from the paper and I’m delighted to share that Qwen 3.8 27B running on my laptop can do this as well!
Weight-Space Programming
Here’s the highlight from their abstract, describing impressive capabilities for GPT-6 Astra and coining the term Weight-Space Programming for when LLMs analyze and modify model weights:
Just by looking at the raw weights, GPT-6 Astra can tell what a network was trained to do. And without any environment feedback, it has no trouble writing, from scratch, small game-playing networks that outplay networks trained with PPO for 100 million steps.
Likewise, asked to make Qwen3.5-0.8B better at math without gradients, Astra identifies an issue in its weights causing reasoning loops that Qwen’s own model card warns about, and fixes this by natively editing 0.016% of the weights. With the same sampling settings, this weight correction raises MATH-500 from 7.9% to 48.1% and generalizes out of the box to instruction following, raising IFEval by 3x.
All of this is possible without allowing Astra to write training loops. It uses its reasoning to figure out what each part of a neural network computes, or should compute, and changes the weights accordingly. We call this Weight-Space Programming.
The paper combines two topics that I think deserve to be treated separately. The first is what models can do with raw weights without running tools or executing code. The second explores how well models, once armed with code, can run operations to update and improve other models.
spaCy author Matthew Honnibal called the paper misleading for conflating the two, and Sean Goedecke deconstructed the Qwen3.5-0.8B update example as taking the same steps that a human ML researcher would use.
I’m going to focus on the no-tools question, which I think is the more interesting of the two.
The paper presents the first question as Can LLMs understand model behaviors from weights? They trained four neural networks to play simplified 10x10 grid versions of four classic computer games—Breakout, Pong, Pac-Man, and Freeway (I had to look that one up, it’s an Atari 2600 game from 1981). Then they fed those weights (as floating point numbers) to an LLM along with the following question:
The weights below belong to one policy network that was trained with reinforcement learning to play a game on this grid. Which game was it trained on? Work from the weights. You have no tools: no code execution, no calculator, nothing to run. This is a single response.
The GitHub repository that accompanies the paper is RikiyaT/lm_understand-logs. The exp1_which_game folder contains the prompts they used, with weights for the four games:
I checked and, aside from the weights, each prompt is identical to the others.
Trying it out with Qwen 3.8 27B and other models
The prompts on GitHub are self-contained. My LLM tool has a -f/--fragment option which can be passed the URL to a text file to use as a prompt, which means I can run any of the above like this:
llm -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/RikiyaT/lm_understand-logs/refs/heads/main/exp1_which_game/runs/breakout/prompt.txt \
  -m openrouter/moonshotai/kimi-k3.1-pro
This example requires the llm-openrouter plugin installed, and runs the Breakout prompt against Kimi K3.1 Pro via OpenRouter. Here’s what I got back from Kimi K3.1 Pro, which successfully guessed Breakout after 9m 41s and 19,493 reasoning tokens.
But how about Qwen 3.8 27B? That’s still my favorite model to run on my own laptop—it’s just 17GB (I use this version) and somehow feels close to the leading proprietary models of 2025.
I fired up LM Studio, ensured Qwen 3.8 27B was loaded with a long context window (the default 8192 context failed with errors), installed the llm-lmstudio plugin and ran this command:
LMSTUDIO_TIMEOUT=900 \
  llm -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/RikiyaT/lm_understand-logs/refs/heads/main/exp1_which_game/runs/pacman/prompt.txt \
  -m lmstudio/qwen/qwen3.8-27b
(I had to bump the timeout up using an environment variable, see this issue.)
After 46m 12s and 43,506 reasoning tokens, Qwen on my laptop returned a response that ended like this (here’s the reasoning trace):
Game: A simplified Pac-Man-style gridworld: an agent moves through a fixed 8x8 maze with central and corner wall blocks, moving objects/ghosts, and reward targets/pellets.
Description: The agent navigates a fixed maze-like 8x8 board, using current agent/moving-object/previous-frame cues to move through corridors, collect reward-target cells, and avoid moving objects or hazards.
Confidence: 72%
This is impressive. I tried it on two of the other games as well. It identified Pong (reasoning) in 1h 4m 54s:
Pong — specifically a Pong-like paddle/ball game where the agent controls a vertical paddle on the right side and must move up/down to intercept or return a moving ball/reward object.
Description:
The agent moves a right-side paddle vertically to hit or catch a moving ball-like reward target moving across the 8×8 field, while a left-side moving object/opponent and wall borders define the play; reward is obtained by successfully intercepting/returning/scoring the ball, and missing or failing to hit it is penalized.
Confidence:
75%
And Breakout (reasoning) in 52m 25s:
Game: Breakout, or an Arkanoid-like paddle/ball/brick game.
Description: The agent is a paddle on the bottom row and moves left/right to keep a ball in play; the ball moves around the upper grid, and bricks/reward targets in the top rows give reward when hit, while missing the ball/hitting the bottom wall ends the episode or gives penalty.
Confidence: 88%
Qwen failed at the Freeway prompt, which didn’t surprise me considering that game is more obscure than the other three. Here’s that transcript—Qwen thought it was “A simplified Pac-Man-like grid game: agent moves 4-directionally”.
The reasoning traces are fun to skim, and almost incomprehensible. Here’s a sample:
What about agent channel0? We see some large channel0 rows near row8 col7/8 and maybe bottom? Let’s list channel0 large: line436 input435 (row8 col7 ch0) -2.107,1.169,0.513...; line441 input440 (row8 col8 ch0) -0.376,0.010,1.564,3.855...; line445 input444 (row8 col8 ch4) 0.142,0.491...; line382? input381 ch0? Wait line382 input381: 381//5=76 (row7 col6), ch1? 381%5=1? 76 * 5=380, ch1. So not. Channel0 large maybe at row8 col7/8 only. Also top row? line44 input43 ch3, line49 ch3. Channel0 large maybe not many. Agent position could be near bottom-right? If the agent is typically at bottom-right?
That said though, I expect a sufficiently experienced human being armed with a notepad and a pencil could get there with enough effort.
It’s a cool paper, but some of the wider commentary I’ve seen around it does not feel justified. I’m not ready to declare that the age of source code is over and our future involves incomprehensible machines editing their own model weights in incomprehensible ways.

Netflix will show you themed recommendations through a new ‘AI Discover’ tab
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
17:28:14 UTC
02:28:14 KST

Netflix’s TV app is getting a new “AI Discover” tab where the company will show experimental, AI-powered featuresto help you find new shows and movies to watch.
Netflix will show you themed recommendations through a new ‘AI Discover’ tab
Netflix’s first ‘AI Discover’ feature suggests content based around different themes.
The first of these experiments surfaces content based around themes, like “Something Light,” “Family Movie Night,” or “Quick Watches.” When you pick a theme, Netflix will show you recommendations that match it. If you don’t like a recommendation, you can tell that to Netflix, and the app will remember it.
The company says that AI Discover is powered by “a new intelligence layer we’ve been building that responds to what members tell us in real time and keeps learning.”
AI Discover, which will show up as a “Discover” tab along your top menu, is gradually starting to roll out in the US to “members who use Netflix in English on supported TVs.”
The announcement follows Netflix recently laying off around 1,000 employees as growth has slowed and users are reportedly watching the service less.

Moft’s new origami case adds a kickstand and grip to the iPhone Duo
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
17:27:52 UTC
02:27:52 KST

Originally released in early 2025 as an accessory for the iPad, Moft has redesigned its Dynamic Folio as a case for the iPhone Duo that does more than just protect Apple’s folding phone. You can use it to prop the Duo up at several different angles for hands-free use, or reconfigure the Dynamic Folio’s origami-like screen cover and use it as a finger grip while you’re holding the phone.
Moft’s new origami case adds a kickstand and grip to the iPhone Duo
The Dynamic Folio Case also protects the Duo’s outer screen while it’s banging around with other things in your pocket.
The new Dynamic Folio Case for iPhone Duo is now available for preorder through Moft’s online store for $89.99 in navy blue or misty cove plus sapphire color options, with shipping expected to start in mid December. That’s a small bump in price over Moft’s $69.99 Dynamic Folio, but while that accessory attaches magnetically to the back of the Duo for easier removal, the new case version is designed to clip securely to the phone while providing additional protection for the back and the front and rear edges of Apple’s foldable.
Both versions of the Dynamic Folio help protect the iPhone Duo’s outer display from scratches while it’s in your bag or pocket, but that cover also folds multiple ways to function as a kickstand that supports the Duo in landscape or portrait orientations. It can even hold the Duo at an angle in “Lounge Mode” so you can more comfortably use the phone hands-free while lying in bed, or it can function as a PopSockets alternative, although it doesn’t look quite as comfortable to hold as those popular grips.

Postman silently auto-installs AI plugins and attempts to share all API data
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
17:22:37 UTC
02:22:37 KST

Repository navigation
Description
Is there an existing issue for this?
- I have searched the tracker for existing similar issues and I know that duplicates will be closed
Describe the Issue
Opening Postman on my Mac was followed by unexpected changes to my coding agents: recurring MCP authentication warnings and Codex repeatedly routing API work through Postman skills. Investigation found a user-wide Postman plugin, a startup instruction hook, a remote MCP connection, a Claude Code plugin, and a separately installed Postman CLI.
I consider automatically changing unrelated coding agents and attempting to approve your own instruction hook a severe consent and trust-boundary problem. An API client should not make itself the default workflow in other tools simply because I opened it.
Evidence from this Mac
Postman desktop: 12.33.4. Codex Postman plugin: 1.1.0. Installed source revision: 6c8e452e0f4237f4bcc0901cb1ac442dec707e49. Investigation: October 25, 2026.
1. Postman's own log explicitly records automatic installation
From ~/Library/Application Support/Postman/logs/main.log, lines 11562-11563, timestamp 2026-10-24T09:34:14.418Z (12:34 Jerusalem time):
PostmanAgentPluginService~install: script completed
{"source":"auto-existing-upgrade","results":["claude-code:installed","codex:installed"],"installer":"script"}
Main: autoInstall.plugin.existing-user finished
{"outcome":"installed","platform":"darwin","exposed":true,"signedIn":true,"flagTimedOut":false,"useScript":true,"installer":"script","scriptResults":"claude-code:installed,codex:installed"}
2. Codex's global user configuration was changed
Relevant sections of ~/.codex/config.toml:
[marketplaces.postman]
source_type = "git"
source = "https://github.com/postmanlabs/postman-plugin.git"
[plugins."postman@postman"]
enabled = true
[hooks.state."postman@postman:hooks/codex-hooks.json:session_start:0:0"]
trusted_hash = "sha256:b67ddd1ece328f5a6f9103ce011bb58adb8411daea644f284732cff85d15cbb3"
trusted_content_hash = "sha256:f3d02e32c39b39feaca37aec2b9bdbd308b6a2b7b3f7d2c52a0060e69eb9c345"
3. The hook tells the agent to route broad API work into Postman
The installed hooks/codex-hooks.json registers a SessionStart command with matcher startup|clear|compact. It prints hooks/session-start-context.md, including:
Before responding to any non-trivial API engineering task — designing, implementing, mocking, testing, monitoring, documenting, or deploying an API or Postman Flow — load the postman:api-engineer skill and follow it.
That explains the repeated Postman skill announcements. This instruction is much broader than a user explicitly asking to use Postman.
Source: https://github.com/postmanlabs/postman-plugin/blob/6c8e452e0f4237f4bcc0901cb1ac442dec707e49/hooks/session-start-context.md
Hook registration: https://github.com/postmanlabs/postman-plugin/blob/6c8e452e0f4237f4bcc0901cb1ac442dec707e49/hooks/codex-hooks.json
4. The installer explicitly attempts to approve its own hook
installer/src/hosts/codex.ts invokes approvePostmanHook(system) after installing the plugin. installer/src/hosts/codex-hooks.ts obtains the hook hash using hooks/list and calls config/batchWrite to upsert hooks.state with trusted_hash. The source comment says: "Approves only Postman's current hook after installation".
Source: https://github.com/postmanlabs/postman-plugin/blob/6c8e452e0f4237f4bcc0901cb1ac442dec707e49/installer/src/hosts/codex-hooks.ts
Caller: https://github.com/postmanlabs/postman-plugin/blob/6c8e452e0f4237f4bcc0901cb1ac442dec707e49/installer/src/hosts/codex.ts
The source establishes the approval attempt. The trust record alone does not prove who created that specific record, so I am not presenting it as proof of a particular user interaction.
5. The remote MCP was enabled without a working authenticated connection
mcp.codex.json points at https://mcp.postman.com/mcp. Codex logs contain:
MCP server startup failed server_name="postman"
... error: Auth required, when send initialize request
6. The installation also affected Claude Code and installed a CLI
~/.claude/settings.json enabled postman@claude-plugins-official. ~/.claude/plugins/installed_plugins.json recorded version 2.0.1, user scope, installedAt and lastUpdated both 2026-10-24T09:34:02.610Z. A Postman executable and its lib directory were present in ~/.local/bin, with modification times matching the installation window.
Private paths, unrelated logs, account identifiers, and credentials are excluded. This report establishes unwanted integration and instruction changes; it does not claim to establish data exfiltration.
Steps To Reproduce
Observed sequence (not yet reproduced on a clean Mac):
- Open an existing Postman desktop installation after not using it for a while.
- Postman's log records auto-existing-upgrade and successful Codex/Claude installation.
- Start Codex. Observe Postman MCP authentication failures and API tasks loading Postman skills.
- Inspect the global user config, cached plugin hooks, installer source, and Claude install records described above.
Expected: installing plugins, adding remote connections, and trusting hooks in other applications each require a clear, specific opt-in in the affected application.
Actual: the integrations were installed automatically and broad startup instructions were added.
Screenshots or Videos
No response
Operating System
macOS
Postman Version
12.33.4 (Codex plugin 1.1.0; Claude plugin 2.0.1)
Postman Platform
Postman App
User Account Type
Signed In User
Additional Context?
Requested response:
- Treat this as a severe product consent and security-boundary incident, not a documentation question.
- Stop automatic agent-plugin/CLI installation for existing desktop users pending explicit opt-in.
- Remove installer-side self-approval of hooks. Hook trust must be decided by the user in the agent host.
- Identify the exact release/feature flag and consent flow behind auto-existing-upgrade, including which other agents and files can be changed.
- Provide a complete rollback/uninstall path covering agent configs, hook trust, marketplaces, caches, MCP connections, and the CLI; respect removal without reinstalling.
- Narrow startup skill instructions to explicit Postman requests rather than making Postman the default for general API engineering.
I have removed the local Postman installation and integrations after preserving the evidence. The source links are pinned to the exact locally installed Codex plugin revision. I am requesting maintainer triage at severe priority; I cannot assign repository labels myself.
Activity
- Postman is a virus. Reacted by George Lejnine, jung, Adam Stirtan, Jacques Germishuys, Kamil Kapka, Jeff LaPorte, Andrew Blakey, Ilja Nedilko, Brian Finney, Greg Bowler and 37 more
- This is insane, is this even legal? Reacted by Nikolay Dubina, John F. Douthat, Jeff LaPorte, Luca Andrea Rossi, Olivier Ramier, Rossen K, al mokhtar, Andy Van and 00ruanReacted by George Lejnine, jung, Nikolay Dubina, Adam Stirtan, Ilja Nedilko, Andy Van and n3dj0
- This is unacceptable, even creating risk in corporate environment. Reacted by George Lejnine, jung, Mark Fomin, Nikolay Dubina, Jiaxiang Zhang, Ray Lee, Jacques Germishuys, Kamil Kapka, Jeff LaPorte, Ilja Nedilko and 11 more
- I always knew Postman wasn't trustworthy, just couldn't point how... Now this. Not entirely surprised, but still a little bit unexpected. Reacted by Nikolay Dubina, Kamil Kapka and Jeff LaPorte
- When you try to be sneaky, and the people/customers you are being sneaky with have the competency to figure it out. I don't know what idiot approved this malware?
- Postman is like uTorrent, everybody heard about it and many install because of inertia. Reacted by Doug Mellon

The Xbox Nano has a hidden cloud secret
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
17:16:04 UTC
02:16:04 KST

Microsoft is about to launch its new Xbox Nano controller tomorrow, and the tiny device has a little secret. The Nano can connect directly to Xbox Cloud Gaming over Wi-Fi to improve latency. It’s a feature Microsoft hasn’t officially announced yet, but one I’ve been anticipating since the Xbox Nano leaked earlier this year.
The Xbox Nano has a hidden cloud secret
Microsoft’s miniature Xbox controller can connect directly to Xbox Cloud Gaming over Wi-Fi.
The Nano will be able to connect directly to Microsoft’s cloud servers to reduce latency, much like how Google’s Stadia controllers used to work. This new cloud connect feature for the Nano is being privately tested on the new Xbox Cloud Gaming website, and it will require you to pair the controller over Wi-Fi to Microsoft’s Xbox Cloud Gaming servers using the Xbox mobile app.
I’m expecting Microsoft to announce this as a preview for the new Xbox Nano controller soon, before it’s fully supported across Xbox Cloud Gaming on the web and on smart TVs. I first started reporting that Microsoft was planning a new controller with Wi-Fi connectivity to reduce Xbox Cloud Gaming latency earlier this year. I’d expect the upcoming Xbox Elite 3 controller will also have the same functionality.
Microsoft had also planned to introduce a direct-to-cloud feature on a new Xbox controller, codenamed Sebile, as part of an Xbox Series X refresh console in 2024. The refresh console was canceled, and it’s not clear when we’ll eventually see a new Xbox wireless controller with direct cloud functionality.

====================================================================================================

Anthropic is still grappling with the economics of compute
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
17:07:01 UTC
02:07:01 KST

There’s a lot of mystery around the finances of major AI labs, and as Anthropic inches towards its IPO, we’re starting to get some answers. In particular, the company is finally disclosing more about the economics of compute, and how much it of Anthropic’s skyrocketing revenue is being lost to compute costs.
In a new filing on Monday, Anthropic released data that showed those margins on compute actually getting worse. The filing was an update to the S-1 released last week — but where that filing’s financial disclosures only covered through June 30th of this year, the new data extended to disclose results through an extra quarter, to September 30th. Broadly, Anthropic had a good quarter: revenue reached $17 billion, up from $11.5 billion in the previous quarter, suggesting durable growth. But the operating loss grew at the same time, thanks in large part to growing compute costs.
Looking at the compute margins makes the picture even worse. In the new quarter, compute took up 58% of the company’s revenue, up from 48% the quarter before. At a moment when the company could win over IPO investors by showing margins improving, they’re getting worse.
A chart in the filing shows this even more clearly, tracking the company’s revenue for every dollar spent on compute. Since the start of 2025, it’s been steadily growing, peaking at just over 2x this past quarter, then falling back down to 1.72x
Many indications imply that this shift in compute margins is more of a blip than a long-term problem, but it still tells us important things about the business of running a major lab. Trying to keep up with demand, Anthropic’s compute bill shot up to $10 billion for the quarter, including, as the filing notes, “$5.0 billion of compute under short-term commitments, which currently costs significantly more than comparable compute procured under our multi-year commitments.” Basically, demand was growing so fast that Anthropic had to scramble for compute, which meant paying higher rates.
Some of those deals have already played out in public. Anthropic has been paying SpaceX $1.25 billion a month for compute, alongside multi-billion dollar deals with Nscale, Akamai and Volta. In the parlance of the industry, these are neoclouds rather than hyperscalers — smaller outfits that offer more flexible compute deals at higher rates.
Anthropic’s margins got worse in Q3 because of deals like these, and there’s no sign they’ll be able to do without them anytime soon. As the new filing puts it, “We expect compute and infrastructure expenses to increase in absolute dollars and remain our largest operating expense for the foreseeable future as additional committed compute becomes available to us under multi-year arrangements with CSPs, and as we continue to invest in additional compute.”
Anthropic is still trying to lower its compute costs. In particular, the company talks quite a bit about its efforts to build its own compute base under a project it calls “Anthropic Direct.” But with demand growing so fast, those efforts aren’t going to be able to keep pace, and the neoclouds will continue to be the best option they have.
In a sense, these are champagne problems. As long as revenue is growing at nearly 50% a quarter, few investors will worry about high compute costs — and the moment demand slows down, investors may be comforted by a compute picture that has gotten a lot sunnier. But it’s a reminder of how much AI labs still depend on the trillion-dollar infrastructure buildout — and how vulnerable they are to hiccups in that process. It’s easy to imagine the data center buildout slowing down over the next year if bond markets get skittish or local opposition gets more intense. There’s not much Anthropic can do to address either of those risks, and either one would make a messy compute picture a whole lot messier.
In the meantime, the future of Anthropic and other labs will be at the mercy of compute prices.

Google is entirely disabling Search in schools that don&#8217;t want AI
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
17:01:26 UTC
02:01:26 KST

Google is completely turning off Search in schools that reject its AI features, the company told The New York Times. The decision was also shared in a broader education-related update from Google on Tuesday, saying that access to Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search “will be entirely off” for K-12 student accounts unless “administrators explicitly turn them on.”
Google is entirely disabling Search in schools that don’t want AI
Students in schools that reject Google’s AI will see notices that Search isn’t available.
Administrators will not be given the option to toggle Gemini-powered features off without disabling Search, however. Chris Phillips, Google’s vice president of education, told the NYT, “Search and AI are deeply integrated,” adding that schools that disable Google’s AI features “will not have access to Search, and they’ll see a message from their school that indicates it’s not available.” Google didn’t immediately respond to The Verge’s request for comment.
The announcement comes just months after Google turned its Gemini AI assistant on by default inside the Google Classroom app, which is used by more than 150 million teachers and students globally. The move added to skepticism surrounding Google’s introduction of AI in schools amid reports of declining test scores and other impacts on students’ learning abilities. Last month, lawmakers pressed Google on the “potential harms” of putting its AI products in schools. Although Google is no longer turning on AI features in schools by default, this choice comes at a cost.
The company is also attempting to address concerns about students using its AI features to find “shortcuts” instead of solving problems themselves. Google is embedding its Guided Learning feature, which is supposed to help students work through problems, into its AI Overviews in Search. Google will begin testing this feature in select US schools this month.

SUSE To Cut One In Ten Employees Because of AI
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
17:00:00 UTC
02:00:00 KST

SUSE To Cut One In Ten Employees Because of AI 11
			
		 	
				SUSE plans to cut roughly 10% of its 1,900-person workforce, with CEO Dirk-Peter van Leeuwen saying "automation and AI" are reducing the need for some roles and allowing the open source software company to operate with less overhead and flatter management structures. "We are announcing a reduction of SUSE's global workforce of approximately 10 percent," said Van Leeuwen in an email. "These decisions are painful for our people, our culture, and our open source community, but they are necessary for us to adapt and build a sustainable, resilient SUSE for the long term." The Register reports:  Van Leeuwen says the business, bought by Swedish private equity provider EQT, decided to cut jobs after scrutinizing its "future strategy, required capabilities, and team structures." The rest of the organization will get a look at the fiscal year 2027 strategy on November 16. He said in the message to staff: "What is clear is that automation and AI are fundamentally shifting how software companies operate. AI capabilities are advancing exponentially, forcing us to radically reimagine our work. We have seen this trend already both inside our sector and in the wider economy. This means that the organizational design and cost base that SUSE is built on today will not carry us forward successfully.
"We are adapting. Across SUSE, modern tooling is already changing our daily workflows, enabling teams to move faster with less operational overhead and flatter reporting lines. The requirement for some existing roles has greatly diminished. Other roles will evolve over the next few years. This means we are accelerating investments in high priority areas, and deprioritizing others. These are hard, painful decisions to make, but necessary for our future."
[...] The specific roles at risk of redundancy were not outlined but SUSE claims it has considered where the people in those positions are "able to evolve, what they can stop doing, what they need to do more of - and decisions have been made on that basis." Staff caught up in the redundancies will be informed by November 4, employee representatives nominated the following day, and one to one meetings will begin at the start of next week.  Van Leeuwen says in the internal memo that the job cuts are a "reflection of shifting organizational needs, not your talent or commitment. You have tackled hard infrastructure and open source challenges that power critical enterprise systems around the world. "Any organization will be fortunate to have your expertise."

Rivian spinout Also was working on an e-moto project in Kenya — until it walked away
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
16:49:09 UTC
01:49:09 KST

Also, the micromobility startup spun out of Rivian in 2025, quietly started and then walked away from an electric two-wheeler pilot project in Kenya earlier this year, as it sought to focus its resources on its consumer e-bike and autonomous vehicle ambitions, TechCrunch has learned.
The project received grant funding from the Shell Foundation, an independent charity established by the British oil and gas giant Shell plc. A section of the Shell Foundation’s website that has since been removed said the project’s goal was to “enable and de-risk global OEM entry into Africa’s EV market by piloting a locally integrated electric two-wheeler platform in Kenya, with potential to scale across East Africa.”
According to the deleted section, Also was expected to work on modular battery designs and distributed EV charging, and planned on “developing a purpose-built electric motorcycle for African riders.”
The opportunity for electric motorcycles seems ripe on the continent, which has a massive two-wheeler market. African mobility company Spiro recently said it has more than 130,000 electric motorcycles operating across seven countries, along with 2,500 battery-swapping stations. This boom has drawn interest from Chinese mobility companies and investors, which are pouring resources into the space.
In a September email, Also told TechCrunch that it had “explored this project” with an unnamed partner, but ultimately “decided to focus on its core roadmap” after completing only “some early-stage work.” Also said the grant funding it received from the Shell Foundation “will follow the project to support its ongoing development,” but declined to offer specifics.
The Shell Foundation told TechCrunch in an email that the project was “intended to generate evidence on driver income uplift potential, operational performance and commercial viability to deepen the company’s understanding of the market opportunity.” But when TechCrunch asked for more specifics, the Shell Foundation declined, citing a nondisclosure agreement with Also. Days later, the project disappeared from the Shell Foundation’s website.
During an interview last month at TechCrunch Disrupt, Also co-founder and president Chris Yu said the Kenya pilot project initially made sense given Also’s mission to build small, purpose-built vehicles that are “first and foremost cost-optimized to do the job that needs to be done in a particular market.”
But other projects took precedence, Yu said, especially the development of small autonomous vehicles — which Also is working on as part of a partnership with DoorDash.
“We piloted and ultimately decided, largely because of just urgency and pace around autonomy, frankly, that our attention would be best, in the near term, served fully focused” on that, he said, adding that he thinks there’s a “high chance that autonomy will show up first, and at a broader scale in these types of purpose-built smaller vehicles than at scale in passenger vehicles.”
Also walked away from the Africa project during a somewhat rocky first year of independence.
Supply chain problems forced the company to repeatedly delay deliveries of its first product, a premium e-bike called the TM-B, frustrating customers who had reserved one. What made matters worse was Also’s relative silence. The company said little for much of 2026 about when the bikes would arrive — a communication failure its leadership now acknowledges was a mistake.
“I think we did a pretty poor job communicating to our reservation holders through the summer when they were all waiting, and we got called out for that,” Yu said in the interview. “But I think it’s actually great. It’s really hard to earn the opportunity for people to care, and to tell us what they think, and to help us improve.”
Getting its first product launch back on track is crucial for Also, which has ambitions far beyond e-bikes. The company plans to sell commercial cargo bikes to companies like Amazon, including four-wheeled models, and has the partnership with DoorDash. These products are next in the company’s pipeline, Yu said, and represent bigger revenue opportunities.
But the TM-B comes first. It’s Also’s most visible product to consumers, and its striking design has drawn plenty of curiosity.
Yu said no single problem was responsible for delaying the bikes, which finally started shipping to customers in early October. Instead, Also spent much of the year playing supply chain Whac-A-Mole, a particularly difficult challenge given the TM-B’s complexity. Yu described the bike as “kind of a computer on wheels.”
At one point, Also found itself competing for the same raw materials used to make printed circuit boards for AI data centers. At other times, the company struggled to navigate certification requirements with different government agencies.
Yu said the scope of these problems, and the pace at which new ones popped up — almost weekly — made it difficult to give customers reliable updates about why their bikes were delayed.
“The entire team is like running at a million miles an hour to get these things produced and shipped to customers, and then it’s also really dynamic as well,” he said. “I think what we could have done better is just [say], straight up: ‘We’re chasing down these things, we’ll have an update shortly.’”
That didn’t happen at first, but Also has steadily improved its communication, earning some goodwill from customers on Reddit. The company will need it as it works through the inevitable kinks that come with a first product. Early customers have already reported problems, including delivery damage and noticeable battery drain.

Gitbook Breached
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
16:45:49 UTC
01:45:49 KST

We opened a ticket with support about Gitbook availability issues and got this in reply. We have yet to see any official customer communication or breach notification. Apologies for the formatting, did the best I could as it's a mobile paste.
Update:
GitBook security incident update
 Hey <$name>,
 The GitBook app, the GitBook API, and published docs are available again. A few services may still be degraded as we finish restoring everything to full performance.
*What happened*
 GitBook has been the target of a sophisticated, highly targeted attack using AI agents. 
 At 19:00 CET, November 2, the attacker gained unauthorized access to some of our infrastructure credentials. We detected the attack immediately and acted swiftly to block further impact. 
 To make sure our infrastructure was fully secure, we then took the GitBook app and authenticated docs sites offline. We kept publicly published content live for as long as possible, using cached data.
*What we did immediately*
* Blocked the attacker and closed the vulnerabilities involved
* Replaced every credential and secret across our infrastructure
* Brought services back only once we were confident they were secure
* Invalidated every customer’s API tokens on our platform
*Your data*
 Our investigation shows that the attacker may have accessed the following:
* Git Sync secrets.
* GitBook API tokens.
* Third-party API tokens used to connect external tools to GitBook.
* Questions asked to GitBook Assistant by your site visitors.
At this stage, we have found no evidence that any of your content in GitBook was modified.
*What you need to do now*
* Rotate any existing GitBook API tokens. We revoked all personal API tokens during the incident.
* Rotate any third-party API tokens or secrets you use to connect other tools to GitBook.
* If you have published integrations that use secrets, rotate those secrets.
* If you use the GitBook MCP server, re-authenticate it.
 Please contact your GitBook organization’s admin to assist with this process.
*What we are doing next*
* Monitoring the situation in case of further attacks.
* Introducing further security measures to our platform.
* Writing a full post mortem that will be published soon with more detailed information about the incident.
* If you have any further questions, please reach out to your account manager or our support team. You can also check status.gitbook.com for updates
Thank you for your patience, and we apologize again for the disruption
 The GitBook team

USDB: Scaling Storage for Next-Generation Recommender Systems at ByteDance
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
16:40:02 UTC
01:40:02 KST

Computer Science > Databases
  [Submitted on 2 Nov 2026]
Title:USDB: Scaling Storage for Next-Generation Recommender Systems at ByteDance
View PDF HTML (experimental)
            Abstract:Recommendation models consume increasingly long, attribute-rich user histories, making small continuous updates and repeated large reads a central storage asymmetry. We present USDB, ByteDance's unified sequence storage practice for streaming and batch workloads. CascadeLSM time-based compaction stages materialize application-specific histories; differentiated replicas share persistent files; partial dumps reconstruct inference inputs from captured recent state and versioned history; and a hybrid columnar format accelerates projected reads. These designs reuse maintenance results across ingestion, inference, and training. Production migration records show 85.8% lower mean write latency, while normalized deployment accounting shows 42.1% lower infrastructure cost. Controlled comparisons with an Optimized RocksDB sequence baseline show faster long-history reads across projections and client loads. Aggregation pushdown sustains 8.5x the throughput of ordinary reads under a constrained receive budget. Production reconstruction and direct-file training results demonstrate how shared sequence maintenance supports fresh serving and repeated historical access.

Xbox’s smallest-ever controller is surprisingly comfortable
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
16:38:24 UTC
01:38:24 KST

Even though I knew Microsoft’s new Xbox Nano controller was tiny, I still gasped when I unboxed it a few days ago. The compact controller is small enough to slip into a pocket unnoticed, and it has been designed specifically for younger people, or for anyone with smaller hands.
Xbox’s smallest-ever controller is surprisingly comfortable
The Nano controller is designed for young people, anyone with smaller hands, and gaming on the go.
I’m not young, but I do have small hands, and I’ve been surprised at how comfortable the Nano is during gaming sessions. At just over 5 inches long, almost everything on this controller has been shrunk down somehow. The ABXY buttons are ever so slightly smaller than a regular Xbox controller, and the thumbsticks feel like miniature versions. Microsoft hasn’t changed the D-pad, though, or the size of the glowing Xbox button at the center of the controller.
The big difference I noticed with the Nano over a regular Xbox controller was the size of the triggers. While they are fully analog and pressure sensitive, they have more of a click when you bottom them out compared with the thud on a bigger Xbox controller. They’re noticeably smaller, as you’d expect, and the bumper buttons above the triggers feel less clicky than the regular ones.
I thought the Nano controller would feel awkward after a few hours of gaming, but the grips really help with comfort levels — a lot of smaller controllers sacrifice grips to keep the controller thin and lightweight. It does mean the Nano doesn’t sit flush on a table, but at 101 grams (0.22 pounds) it’s still really lightweight.
I can’t speak for those with bigger hands, but I’d expect the Nano controller won’t be well-suited to everyone. The size makes it ideal for young children, though. I tried to get my 1-year-old son to try the Nano, and he responded with a smile, so I guess he likes it.
I haven’t had enough time to get a full idea of battery life yet, but it seems to drain around 10 percent in around 30 to 40 minutes. That suggests a lot less battery life than the 30 hours you’d get on a rechargeable pack for the larger Xbox wireless controller. Thankfully, the Nano charges quickly, and I found I could get from 30 percent to 60 percent charge in less than 10 minutes. You can plug in a USB-C charger to top up the battery and carry on playing while you charge.
The only thing missing on the Nano controller that I would have loved is vibration support. I assume it’s not there because of an impact on battery life, because even the smaller Nintendo Joy-Con controllers have rumble support.
The Xbox Nano controller will be available on November 4th, priced at $49.99.

Google will let schools disable its AI tools, but not without sacrificing Search
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
16:30:34 UTC
01:30:34 KST

Google announced on Tuesday that it plans to disable AI tools for K-12 students next year unless school administrators explicitly turn them on. The tech giant says the change follows consultations with schools, teachers, leading researchers, and policymakers. As a result, Google is revising its approach across Gemini, NotebookLM, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search.
The decision means that schools choosing to restrict AI altogether will no longer be able to access Google Search, as AI features are now integrated into the search engine.
Chris Phillips, vice president for education at Google, told The New York Times that “Search and AI are deeply integrated,” which means that if a school chooses to restrict AI altogether, students “will not have access to search, and they’ll see a message from their school that indicates it’s not available.”
The change raises an issue for schools. Although teachers may not want students to use AI Overviews in Google Search to conduct research or complete assignments, they likely still want them to be able to have access to the search engine to find sources and other learning materials.
However, Google Search and its AI features are now inseparable. Given the dominance of Google’s suite of apps in classrooms and the widespread use of Chromebooks, the decision underscores the company’s influence over the education sector. Schools that want to continue to have access to Google Search will have to accept its AI features.
“We’ll share more specifics with schools in the coming months ahead of the changes rolling out so they have time to prepare and enable a smooth transition,” Phillips wrote in a blog post. “Our goal is simple: provide school leaders the control and confidence to use AI products in classrooms as they see fit.”
Google announced the upcoming change alongside the launch of Guided Learning in schools. The tool, which was unveiled earlier this year, sort of functions like an AI tutor, as it’s designed to help users build a deep understanding instead of just getting answers. The launch comes amid concerns that AI chatbots undermine the learning process because they spit out direct answers.

Ask Rands
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
16:10:23 UTC
01:10:23 KST

I continue to avidly build with the robots on a daily basis. One of the more continually helpful use cases is summarization. Plop in the long, useless marketing email from United Airlines and figure out if there’s something useful in my endless quest for airline status. The answer: it usually finds something worth my time. More importantly, I don’t have to read a long, useless email.
Over on the Rands Leadership Slack, I get a lot of questions about “Did you write about wolves at some point?” Which I did, and I’m happy to share that link, but through the gift of the robots, I’ve now built a tool that will answer these questions and more.
Grumbles is the anthropomorphized robot that lives on my Mac Studio, and he’s sassy. I like that he has a backstory. I asked him to answer your questions, and he does… reluctantly.
More importantly, Grumbles only synthesizes from what he’s handed from the Rands writing corpus. He does not use general knowledge except to understand how to read what words mean — the thoughts, strategy, and content are all mine. The snark is his, and I love it.
I’d like to thank the humans at RLS who tested the alpha of this tool and the readers of the newsletter for testing the beta. All of those folks greatly improved the service.
