New York City’s Mamdani criticizes Madison Square Garden owner over surveillance tech
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
16:04:55 UTC
01:04:55 KST

Mayor of New York City Zohran Mamdani has criticized the owner of Madison Square Garden, one of the city’s largest entertainment venues, arguing that the company’s surveillance technology should not be allowed.
Mamdani told Wired in an interview on Tuesday that surveillance technologies are “indicative of much that is wrong in our country today.” He added that City Council member Shahana Hanif introduced two pieces of local legislation aimed at banning the collection of biometric data, such as using facial recognition, in public places across the city. Mamdani said that the draft legislation is “an important response to this kind of an issue.”
The comments come after a stream of reporting from Wired detailing how Madison Square Garden’s owner James Dolan relies on surveillance cameras and face-scanning technology to track people across and outside of its venues.
In one report, Wired revealed that Dolan’s company keeps a database that, among other things, marks some visitors, including celebrity performers, as “LGBTQIA,” for reasons that are unclear. Dolan subsequently sued Wired over its reporting.
In 2021, MSG made headlines after a woman was banned from Radio City, another Dolan-owned venue, after being recognized by facial recognition systems as an attorney at a law firm who was suing Dolan’s company but was not involved in the litigation.
When reached by TechCrunch, Madison Square Garden spokesperson Mikyl Cordova said: “Wired’s reporting is inaccurate and false. MSG is pursuing legal remedies,” without providing evidence for its claim.
Since 2021, New York City has required businesses to disclose when they are collecting biometric data from visitors.
Updated with comment from MSG.

MIT Researchers Used GPT-6 Astra To Edit Qwen's Open Weights Directly, Boosting Math Scores
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

MIT Researchers Used GPT-6 Astra To Edit Qwen's Open Weights Directly, Boosting Math Scores 19
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from VentureBeat: Researchers from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT) Computer Science & Artificial Intelligence Laboratory (CSAIL) reported today that they used OpenAI's GPT-6 Astra language model to raise a small, open source Qwen model's math score roughly sixfold by directly editing its weights -- the numerical parameters that govern its behavior. Rikiya Takehi, Jayden Teoh, Alex Zhang and Omar Khattab described their work in a research blog post published November 2. They call the approach "weight-space programming" and tested Astra's ability to interpret and alter networks without using conventional training procedures to generate the edits.
In the strongest language-model example, Astra's intervention raised Qwen3.5-0.8B's MATH-500 score from 7.9% to 48.1% while touching approximately 0.016% of its weights. The improvement came primarily from correcting repetitive reasoning—a distinction that shaped both the technical findings and the debate over their implications. Their experiments explored a broader possibility: AI systems that inspect, repair and construct other neural networks by working directly on their internal machinery. "This is cool as heck!" wrote Yuchen Jin, a technical staffer at Databricks and former co-founder and CTO of Hyperbolic Labs who was unaffiliated with the research, on X. "Imagine using a large LLM to directly edit a smaller model's weights and dramatically improve its capabilities. It's like gene editing for neural networks. Even cooler than distillation!"
Voyage AI staff researcher Chandan Singh, formerly of Microsoft AI, also reacted to the findings: "For anyone questioning whether this post is overhyped, I think they are actually *underselling* the approach."

Midterm elections 2026: all the news
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:58:39 UTC
00:58:39 KST

The 2026 midterm elections will determine the scope of President Donald Trump’s power in his final two years in office, and signal what voters care about going into the 2028 presidential election. While this year’s races are expected to be most broadly defined by affordability issues, there are several topics impacting the tech industry that appear to be on voters’ minds.
Among them are concerns about data centers, and whether voters will have to bear the burden of higher energy costs, AI safety, and the rapid expansion of surveillance technology through Flock. There’s also the question of how to tax billionaires’ wealth in California, a topic that has driven heavy tech spending and creative stock class structures meant to keep tech executives from fleeing the state should a ballot measure pass.
The next Congress is likely to continue grappling with how to regulate AI, and stem the data center backlash, among other issues. How that conversation shapes up, and which tech CEOs are hauled to testify on Capitol Hill, will be shaped by the outcome of November 3rd.
- Severe weather is affecting some voters across the country. In Florida, more than 20 polling sites are being moved due to intense flooding. Storms in the Southwest have also impacted some polling locations in Colorado, Newsweek reports, while Utah will let some voters impacted by flooding return ballots electronically, and Arizona officials are warning voters to check travel updates.
- A ballot scanner issue is impacting a New York suburb. Polling sites across Westchester County are experiencing an issue with their voting machines, but officials say residents can still safely cast their ballots. Election officials have a backup protocol in place to ensure voting can continue. Voting Machine Snags Are Reported in New York Suburbs [New York Times]
- Election protection hotline has received 3,000 calls this morning. The nonpartisan coalition running the 866-OUR-VOTE hotline says the calls range from asking about general information, to reporting long lines, lack of signage, or voting equipment issues. Texas, Michigan, Ohio, Pennsylvania, and New York account for the most calls. So far, no calls about federal agents at the polls.
- DOJ sends 1,000 election monitors to watch polls in every state. Federal election monitors are a standard procedure, but this year the Trump administration has expanded it to the “largest monitoring effort in history.” States have prepared to make sure they don’t interfere, as the administration has taken several steps this year to assert more oversight of state-run elections.
- Prop 45 in California could be hiding a loophole for data centers A high-stakes California ballot measure pitched as a fast track for “essential” infrastructure could wind up giving data center developers a loophole to evade public scrutiny, environmental advocates warn. Proposition 45 would shorten timelines for state-mandated environmental assessments for eligible projects and make it more difficult for residents to challenge those projects in court. Proponents say passing the measure would help alleviate the state’s housing shortage and meet rising electricity demand with new renewable energy projects. Environmental groups and some legal experts, however, warn that data center developers backing the proposition could escape scrutiny if Prop 45 becomes law. Read Article >
- Another election year, another tech company war room. OpenAI is the latest tech company (and according to Politico, the first major AI company) to set up a command center for fighting election misinfo and abuse of its systems, approaching the second major AI-era US election: The Election Operations Center will begin running this week until all votes are counted, to identify the potential spread of misinformation or other efforts to disrupt the elections. It’s meant in large part to combat abuse of the company’s own tools like ChatGPT, monitoring real-time events and fielding reports of unexpected behavior from models or cases of suspected violations of their platform’s terms.
- Voters in Texas agree: They hate data centers Ask Texas NAACP president Gary Bledsoe what it’s been like to see data center developers descend upon Austin and its surrounding suburbs and he can rattle off project after project facing opposition from locals. There’s the fight in Taylor over plans to build a data center at a site that’s supposed to be a park. Temple, around 40 miles away, is another sore spot, Bledsoe says. The mayor and City Council members there face recall attempts over data center developments. And some 30 miles outside of Austin, San Marcos became the first city in all of Texas to ban data centers. Read Article >
- Data center backlash is uniting America If there’s one issue that seems to activate all kinds of voters in 2026, it’s data centers. The rapid buildout of these massive server storage facilities — and the infrastructure construction and expenses that come with them — has sparked local opposition in communities across the country and become a major issue in many congressional and gubernatorial campaigns. At the same time, tech money has been pouring into many of these races. Leading the Future, a super PAC backed by venture capitalist Marc Andreessen and OpenAI President Greg Brockman, has already spent $45 million in a year. And Public First Action, an Anthropic-backed group that says it supports AI safeguards, says it’s raised $80 million. Both have funded Democratic and Republican-aligned groups. Other super PACs see an opportunity to push for stricter AI policies, like the recently created Tomorrow Together, which launched $4 million in ads for battleground House races, with messages about data center development and AI safety. Read Article >
- Republican politicians have embraced anti-Indian racism — and it’s costing them votes Two years ago, University of Houston student Vasant Bhatt was doing everything he could to help Republicans regain power. He knocked doors for Sen. Ted Cruz’s reelection campaign and helped organize his community for Donald Trump, two victories he, like many other Republicans, celebrated in 2024. But this week, Bhatt did something unexpected: He voted for Texas Democratic Senate candidate James Talarico, and he publicly urged others to do the same. Bhatt, whose father is a small-business owner, tells The Verge he was initially drawn to the Republican Party due to its economic principles. “The strong belief in individual liberty and limited government,” he says, led him to identify as a conservative. More recently, however, another aspect of his family’s experience has driven him away from the party. As an Indian American, Bhatt feels increasingly unwelcome in a party where racist rhetoric is becoming far too common. Read Article >
- Meta made a deal to keep Mark Zuckerberg from leaving California over its billionaire tax. It said in a filing that Zuckerberg “was evaluating relocating his primary residence” if California’s Proposition 40 passes. As Bloomberg reports, Meta’s board issued special shares so that he wouldn’t have to leave: Zuckerberg gets non-voting shares he can sell for liquidity without reducing his voting control. In return, he has committed to remain in California — or wherever Meta is headquartered — for eight years while he remains in a full-time leadership role.
- Trump is giving Nick Shirley $10,000 and launching a $25 million fraud bounty program Donald Trump and Vice President JD Vance said on Tuesday that the Trump administration was giving right-wing YouTuber Nick Shirley $10,000 and launching a $25 million bounty program for members of the public to report fraud. In December 2025, Shirley published a viral video alleging fraud at daycares in Minnesota that were operated by members of the local Somali community. Shirley lingered in parking lots and asked to enter childcare centers, taking refusals as evidence that the centers weren’t actually caring for children. The video quickly went viral, triggering a massive governmental response: Federal immigration agents descended on the Minneapolis area, and the Trump administration froze Minnesota’s federal funding for childcare. Shirley has since visited other states in the US under similar pretenses, showing up unannounced to grocery stores and hospice centers in search of evidence of fraud. Vance and others in the administration publicly praised Shirley, who has since become a mainstay in the MAGA-aligned content creator ecosystem. He’s helped popularize a genre of so-called independent journalism popular on the right in which content creators and influencers confront businesses or people they suspect of fraud or crime as the basis for their online content. Harassment — or even immigration crackdowns — often follow. Read Article >
- Democrats think cracking down on AI could give them an edge Democrats could win back control of Congress this November, and if they do, they’ll need to decide how to approach one of the country’s newest and thorniest political issues: AI. Polls predict Democrats will flip at least one chamber of Congress in the midterm elections, which would put committee leadership back in their hands. That could usher in a wave of newfound scrutiny for AI companies. Top Democrats on key committees have already signaled their willingness to press the industry on tax credits tied to data centers and recent hacking incidents, and would be able to set committee agendas if they take over, allowing them to call CEOs to testify in Congress and demand documents that explain companies’ inner workings. Read Article >
- Tech billionaires have reportedly donated over $340 million to fight a wealth tax in California. According to The Guardian, the campaign they’re funding is spending over $1 million per day trying to defeat Proposition 40, which would enact a one-time 5 percent tax on California’s billionaires. Google co-founder Sergey Brin is reportedly the biggest donor, contributing at least $102 million, alongside other tech executives: The donors to the no campaign are a who’s who of Silicon Valley, including the Palantir co-founder Peter Thiel, former Google CEO Eric Schmidt, DoorDash CEO Tony Xu, Stripe CEO Patrick Collison, crypto magnate Chris Larsen and several prominent venture capitalists.
- Elon Musk has donated over $276 million to Republican midterm efforts Elon Musk has handed out at least $276 million to Republican groups since July, The New York Times reports, making him both the party’s top known donor and the biggest established midterm donor. $158 million of that money — one of the largest political donations in American history — has been funneled into the Senate Leadership Fund, the main super PAC that aims to secure and maintain a Republican Senate majority. Among others, it’s fighting to retain the party’s Senate seat in Texas, which serves as a major hub for Musk’s business empire. He’s also donated over $76 million to America PAC, a super PAC Musk founded in 2024 that canvasses for hard-to-reach Republican voters. Smaller amounts have also been given to other conservative groups, including: Read Article >
- The midterms are going to be a data security nightmare One messy database is threatening to disenfranchise thousands or even millions of registered voters, while leaving even more at risk of intimidation or data breaches, in the name of solving a problem that barely exists. As the 2026 midterm elections approach, election and privacy experts are sounding alarms about the Department of Homeland Security’s Systematic Alien Verification for Entitlements (SAVE) Program, which President Donald Trump’s administration has expanded to ostensibly catch noncitizens voting. Experts say that amounts to a dangerous, error-prone effort to centralize voter data. “The federal government doesn’t have the authority to do any of that and doesn’t have the expertise either,” says Eileen O’Connor, senior counsel at the Brennan Center. “Inserting themselves into the day-to-day functioning of state elections is unprecedented and disturbing.” Read Article >
- Democrats preview how they’d go after the Ticketmaster settlement if they regain power A handful of Democrats called an unofficial hearing on Capitol Hill Monday to slam the Department of Justice’s “trivial” and “pathetic” settlement with Live Nation-Ticketmaster, previewing how they might go after Trump administration antitrust deals if they win back congressional power in November. While the DOJ settlement with Live Nation, which came one week into trial and with a promise of up to $280 million, received copious pushback, Democrats don’t at the moment have the power in either chamber to set committee agendas. That meant that Monday’s so-called shadow hearing looked different than most official proceedings. Instead of sitting at the elevated seats behind the dais, the lawmakers sat at tables below, eye level with witnesses including California Attorney General Rob Bonta, one of the AGs continuing the fight against Live Nation-Ticketmaster, ousted Trump administration antitrust official Roger Alford, and several entertainment industry players, including a member of the band The Hold Steady. Several artists asked to appear at the forum declined out of fear for their livelihoods, according to House Judiciary Committee Ranking Member Jamie Raskin (D-MD), who co-hosted the forum with Senate Homeland Security permanent subcommittee on investigations Ranking Member Richard Blumenthal (D-CT). Read Article >
- AI backlash is coming for elections Ask Americans how they feel about AI and most say they have concerns. Communities have mounted resistance to data center projects, stalling them across the US. On social media, anger at AI companies and executives is unrestrained — sometimes to the point of condoning violence. But look at the issues that most campaigns are focused on, and AI is far less prevalent, experts say. Read Article >

In the early 2010s, Quilicura began to host one of the largest
concentrations of data centers dedicated to artificial intelligence.
These use the region's water as a cooling method.

QUILICURA, CHILE SITS WITHIN THE MAIPO RIVER BASIN, ONE OF THE MAIN
SOURCES OF WATER FOR PEOPLE AND FARMING. SINCE THE EARLY 2010s,
QUILICURA HAS BECOME A MAJOR HUB FOR A.I. DATA CENTERS THAT USE
LARGE AMOUNTS OF WATER TO COOL DOWN SERVERS. BECAUSE OF THAT,
COUPLED WITH AN ONGOING CLIMATE CRISIS, CHILE IS FACING A SEVERE
DROUGHT EXPECTED TO LAST UNTIL 2040.
[Becoming AI for a Day]
Even though A.I. can move mankind ahead, the irresponsible use of
A.I. can have disastrous effects on our water and other resources.
It is inconceivable to think that someone casually using A.I. to
create funny images in NYC or Berlin, can affect the water supply in
Quilicura, Chile.To make the impact visible, and to help draw visibility to the
problem, the residents from Quilicura became “human A.I.,” for a
day. Answering prompts, creating content with local artists, and
completing other tasks manually.
The initiative helped dimensionalize a complex problem, and helped
show the world the real people affected by the the irresponsible use
of A.I. and the hidden cost it has on local water resources.
On JAN 31st from 8 AM to 8 PM: more than 40 people from Quilicura
managed to answer more than 25,000 prompts.

The State of Longevity: The healthmaxxing economy is here
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:48:31 UTC
00:48:31 KST

Make sure to also check out the companion podcast to this piece with Andrew Huberman here.
The longevity movement is here to stay (no pun intended).
Not long ago, taking care of our health largely meant following our doctor’s advice and sticking to what our insurance covered. There has always been a fringe movement of biohackers and crunchy people taking a more hands-on approach, but that mindset has only recently moved into the mainstream.
Two things accelerated this shift. The first was the COVID-19 pandemic, which made us acutely aware of our mortality and the fragility of our health. It exposed the limits of our medical system and reminded us that a yearly physical doesn’t necessarily mean we’re healthy.
The second was ChatGPT. Suddenly everyone could ask questions about their health and make sense of complex medical information in simple English. Today, Americans are taking their health into their own hands, spending more time, money, and energy on health and longevity.
So what is coming next? To understand how people are taking charge of their health – and what they want – we surveyed 500 randomly selected consumers in the U.S. on their views about everything from peptides to toxins to brain-computer interfaces.
We’re also talking to people at the cutting edge of health and technology. Our upcoming podcast conversations will explore how they approach their own health and what the rest of us might learn from them. First up, Andrew Huberman walks us through a day in his life, from food and exercise to supplements and sleep.
For founders, this newfound consumer interest in health creates an opening. We think some of the biggest opportunities in longevity will go to companies that help people take action and manage their health over time.
Below, we combine our observations on the longevity market with our recent survey results to explore where the next great companies might be built.
Trends in Longevity
The race to become a “healthcare home” or the “Muse for healthcare”
Today, there is no single place to manage your healthcare. Your records are scattered across doctors’ offices, and getting care means navigating a mix of phone calls, patient portals, pharmacies, and labs.
Companies are racing to build the ultimate healthcare home – an app that knows your medical history and records, prescribes your annual labs and imaging, connects to your wearable, and uses AI to help you understand what’s going on. And when you need care, it connects you with virtual or in-person clinicians who can prescribe treatment or perform procedures as needed.
The leading contenders in this race have different head starts. Function Health has lab and imaging results that show how our health changes over time. Oura and WHOOP already have a place in our daily routines. General Medicine and Counsel start with the ability to deliver care. Ferry Health understands the fragmented landscape of where to get insurance-covered care. OpenAI, Anthropic, Amazon, and Apple are building on large audiences and products we already use.
The race is to become the first place a consumer turns to when they need something done for their health. Asking AI a question about your health is useful, but arranging the next test, booking the appointment, and making sure the patient gets follow-up care and prescriptions is harder. As AI makes answers and interpretation cheaper, value will migrate to the companies that can figure out the action layer.
If this sounds like “Muse for healthcare,” that’s the idea. There is room to build very large companies around the healthcare needs that generalist AI assistants are unlikely to prioritize. The complexity of our healthcare system today makes this harder to build, but could also make it harder to replace.
We expect room for multiple winners. Almost everyone could use this kind of help.
From organic food to non-toxic everything
The MAHA movement has brought the toxins in our food, water, and everyday products to light, and consumer willingness to spend on “clean” non-toxic products has grown massively. Just a few years ago, the word “microplastics” was not in our lexicon; now our survey revealed that 63% of consumers are aware of the plastic particles building up in our bodies, 35% are concerned about them, and the biggest biohackers are trying plasma exchange to remove microplastics from their blood.
For consumers looking to reduce everyday exposures, the shopping list goes way beyond buying organic food into a broader focus on what we ingest. Our survey of Americans revealed lots of consumer change in this respect. 24% of respondents are swapping to nontoxic cookware, 39% are subbing out plastic for glass, and 70% are filtering their water.
That mindset extends beyond the kitchen and into the broader home. Healthmaxxers are buying air purifiers (37%), testing their homes for mold (18%), switching to organic-cotton clothing or bedding (17.5%), and even buying new natural or organic mattresses (11%). Even EMF emitted by wifi, cell phones, and microwaves is on consumers’ radar: 14% said they are trying to reduce it.
Consumer spending is moving faster than the evidence. People are willing to pay for peace of mind, even when the health benefit of a particular purchase is hard to measure. In our survey, 62% of respondents said they would pay more for products labeled as “non-toxic.” That creates opportunities across food, home goods, and personal care.
Peptides for all
GLP-1 receptor agonist medicines (e.g., semaglutide, tirzepatide, orforglipron, and others) have revolutionized health more than any drug in our lifetimes, transforming care for diabetes, obesity, and cardiovascular health – and the promise of their benefits keeps growing.
The runaway success of this class of approved GLP-1s, growing comfort with at-home self-injection, and loosening regulation have also fueled a surge of interest in other unregulated peptides. Consumers are now experimenting with compounds not approved by the FDA that are marketed for recovery, performance, and appearance, including the pursuit of “looksmaxxing.” For example, BPC-157 is promoted for tissue repair; melanotan II for tanning.
But the market’s enthusiasm to date has outpaced the actual evidence. Many of these compounds are still missing strong human data that demonstrates safety and efficacy, and buying from unregulated suppliers raises questions regarding what is actually in the vial. And while HHS’s most recent policy eases enforcement against licensed pharmacies compounding six peptides under specified conditions, these peptides remain unapproved by the FDA, with unresolved questions about safety and efficacy. Despite the buzz, the single-digit billion compounded peptide market is dwarfed by even a single FDA-approved peptide: Eli Lilly’s tirzepatide, which generated nearly $44 billion in sales under the Mounjaro and Zepbound brands in the first nine months of 2026 alone.
We see the peptide trend unfolding along two distinct paths across biotech and healthcare. From the biotech side, we predict growth in the pipeline of rigorous, FDA-regulated peptide drugs – and growing enthusiasm from investors seeking to underwrite medicines that not only meet the traditional safety and efficacy bars, but could also have vast consumer appeal. This is an unnatural trend for the biotech sector: drugs are indicated based on clinical need, and historically the market has actually struggled to identify and treat patients efficiently. Peptide medicines with robust consumer pull can create an unusually efficient path to patients, and we suspect this dynamic could extend to other disease areas, including sleep, bone health, and cardiovascular health.
On the care delivery side, we could see a repeat of the GLP-1 cycle if additional peptide drugs demonstrate safety and efficacy and gain mainstream adoption. GLP-1s helped many care delivery companies grow, but most of the enterprise value accrued to pharma. Other peptides could follow a similar pattern in care delivery: first, dedicated prescribing companies may emerge, following the model of Calibrate, Found, and Weekend Health from the GLP-1 wave. Then, existing healthcare companies could add peptide prescriptions to attract new patients. But as more companies offer the same drugs, prescribing them could become less of a competitive advantage and more of a service patients expect. At that point, offering peptides may matter more for keeping existing patients from leaving than for attracting new ones.
Cholesterol could become dinner-table conversation
Cholesterol could become dinner-table conversation for a surprising reason: while heart disease is still America’s biggest silent killer, there’s rapidly growing evidence that we can understand risk, detect disease at early stages, and intervene with medications to not only prevent but actually reverse some of the plaque already in our arteries. This is giving consumers a compelling reason to take their heart health seriously before a cardiac event.
For LDL – the “bad” cholesterol – the evidence increasingly supports “lower is better,” especially for people at higher risk. A study published this October found that intensive treatment shrank plaque in the heart’s arteries by about a fifth over 18 months in people with heart disease. Getting cholesterol into the “normal” range may not be enough for your personal risk, and medication could provide additional protection.
New treatments could bring cholesterol management to a wider audience. PCSK9 inhibitors like Repatha help the liver clear LDL from the blood, but injections, cost, and insurance restrictions have limited their use. Only 21% of our survey respondents knew about these drugs. We expect that to change and for these drugs to enter the consumer zeitgeist as miracle drugs that lower cholesterol with few side effects: Lipfendra, the first PCSK9 inhibitor available as a daily pill, received FDA approval in July 2026.
Evidence also supports treating high-risk patients before their first heart attack or stroke. In people with diabetes and no known major artery disease, adding Repatha to existing treatment lowered the estimated five-year risk of a first heart attack, stroke, or death from coronary heart disease from 7 to 5%.
The next frontier is lipoprotein(a), or Lp(a), an inherited risk factor for heart disease. Roughly one in five people has high levels, which are largely unaffected by diet or exercise. U.S. guidelines now recommend testing Lp(a) at least once in adulthood. Drugs targeting Lp(a) could give millions of people a new way to address that risk, but the clinical benefit of pharmacologic intervention still needs to be demonstrated. Novartis’s recent pelacarsen trial lowered Lp(a), but failed to meet its primary endpoint of reducing cardiovascular events. That said, we’re quite optimistic about other Lp(a) targeting drugs from Eli Lilly and Amgen now in trials.
Consumers don’t have to wait for the next drug. Knowing your Lp(a) can help you decide how aggressively to manage LDL and other heart risks. The question to ask: “Given my family history and risk, how low should I try to get my LDL – and would medication help me get there?” We see an opening for companies that connect testing to personalized treatment, help patients access medications, and track their progress. Knowing your numbers is the starting point. The opportunity is helping people act on them.
Wearables: Act 3
Wearables have become a daily habit for many consumers. In our survey, 54% of respondents use one, and 83% of those users check it every day. There’s still room to make that habit more useful: 55% of wearable users say their data has never prompted a behavior change or a doctor’s visit. What stood out to us is that nearly three in four would wear a second device if it measured something new. We expect the next wave of wearables to build on that curiosity, giving people more insight into their bodies and a clearer sense of what to do with it.
The first wave of wearables, led by Fitbit, got us counting our steps. The next generation – Oura, WHOOP, and Apple Watch – made checking our sleep, heart rate, and recovery routine.
Today’s third generation is being built to answer more specific questions about our health. Clair is developing a wearable designed to estimate hormone patterns without a blood draw. Lumia is building blood-flow tracking into earrings. These products give us new ways to understand parts of our health that have been difficult to track day to day.
The category leaders are expanding in this direction too: Oura now uses nighttime data to flag patterns related to blood pressure and lets users log GLP-1 doses and side effects alongside their biometric data, helping them follow how their body responds over time.
Deregulation is aiding this innovation. In January 2026, the FDA updated its general wellness policy, clarifying how certain non-invasive wearables can offer wellness insights without medical-device clearance. In June, it closed its warning letter to WHOOP after the company changed its Blood Pressure Insights feature and labeling. Oura is also offering blood-pressure-related insights as wellness features. There’s more room to build here, provided the products stay within the boundaries of wellness claims.
The bigger prize is a device small enough to wear without thinking about it, capable of reliably tracking a much wider range of biomarkers in real time. Imagine a non-invasive version of a CGM that measures far more than glucose. If we get there, we’ll have a fundamentally different understanding of what’s happening in our bodies in real time and mere step trackers will feel archaic.
Brain health & brain-computer interfaces
Neurotechnology could open up new ways to improve health and interact with consumer technology.
Elon Musk’s Neuralink has brought implantable brain-computer interfaces (BCIs) into public view. Its surgically implanted devices already enable clinical trial participants with paralysis to control computers by decoding intended movement from motor cortex activity. The company is also developing technology intended to restore some visual perception for people who are blind by stimulating the brain’s visual cortex.
Other emerging implantable approaches record from the brain’s surface without penetrating the underlying tissue, offering potential advantages for safety and long-term use. At UCSF, Dr. Edward Chang’s team uses high-density electrode arrays placed on the brain’s surface to record signals across several brain regions. In 2021, Chang’s team became the first to decode full words directly from the brain activity of a person who was paralyzed and unable to speak. More recently, the same team decoded speech and gestures together, showing the ability of a single implant to support both words and body language.
For other application areas, non-invasive approaches could bring neural interfaces to the masses even sooner. Companies are building brain-sensing patches, headbands, or hats with electrodes that can sense the state of the brain and are attempting to read it. Companies like Conduit, Alljoined, and Aleph Neuro are approaching neural decoding as a data scaling problem, collecting large datasets to train AI models that translate weak, noisy signals recorded non-invasively from the brain into text and images reflecting what someone is thinking or seeing. One day, we may be able to communicate ideas directly to AI agents without first putting them into words. But neurotechnology also has “write” applications relevant to health, where wearable devices could go further than just reading what’s happening in our brains and actually change brain activity itself. We’re seeing more and more neuro wearable devices coming to market that aim to improve sleep and focus through targeted stimulation of the brain or peripheral nerves.
This all sounds like science fiction, but we’re betting in our lifetimes, brain-computer interfaces and “wearables” for the brain will become a real consumer category and part of everyday life. Our survey suggests early consumer interest: 39% would use a non-invasive brain-sensing device for sleep, focus, or mood, and 29% for thought-controlled devices, with greater interest among men.
What Founders Should Take From This
The encouraging signal across these six areas is that consumers are ready to act. They’re seeking out tests, trying new products, and spending their own money on health.
That is the opportunity for founders: help people turn their interest in health into something useful in their lives. Start with a problem they care about, show that the product helps, and make it easy enough to keep using. Companies that do that well can grow with their customers as their needs change.
We believe some of the next great consumer health companies will be built around that relationship. If you’re building one, we’d love to hear from you.
Special thanks to Vineeta Agarwala, Julie Yoo, and Zak Doric for their help with this piece.

The strongest sign of a healthy democracy is that people participate in it.  It's up to you to decide who to vote for, but no matter who you support, please show up and cast a ballot.  A democracy with 75% voter turnout is healthier than a democracy with 50% voter turnout even exactly the same politicians got elected, because citizens participating in a democratic process signals that they value the process and want to keep it.
(Obviously, this only applies to people eligible to vote in ongoing elections.  If you're not eligible to vote, please don't vote.)

Collins Obijiaku’s Tender Portraits Conjure Memories and Community
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:43:32 UTC
00:43:32 KST

Collins Obijiaku’s Tender Portraits Conjure Memories and Community
Through eloquent linear details and brushy, color-blocked backgrounds, Collins Obijiaku renders tender portraits in oil. Often directly meeting the viewer’s gaze, the striking figures inspired by individuals within the artist’s community command our attention. The artist harnesses a sense of timelessness, with a palette that swings from earthy to jewel-toned and subtle hints of architecture, nature, and art historical iconography that transcend specific eras.
Raised in Nigeria’s Kaduna region, Obijiaku recently moved to Chicago, where he has grappled with the emotional resonance of distance and getting used to an entirely new place. “Seen through a sentimental lens, these settings lend the paintings an intimate, dreamlike quality, as though recalling a presence from memory,” says a statement from Roberts Projects, which will be presenting the artist’s forthcoming solo show, Wherever You Are, Remember Me.
Obijiaku relishes how everyday experiences have the potential to prompt memories. Food smells can evoke thoughts of holidays or relatives. Sounds can transport us to specific times and places in our past. And when exploring somewhere new, it’s common to experience a kind of illusory familiarity, such as when a stranger passes on the street and they remind us of someone we objectively know is thousands of miles away.
A handful of the works shown here, including “Rengaji” and “Portrait of Benedicta Rufus” will be included in the show, complementing a suite of still lifes. “Rengaji,” for example, nods to Renaissance paintings in the Christian tradition of the likes of Fra Angelico, while also referencing Obijiaku’s own childhood memories of royal processions in Nigeria, where crowds would gather to observe the king.
Wherever You Are, Remember Me opens on November 7 and continues through December 19 in Los Angeles. See more on Obijiaku’s Instagram.

Are the Fridges Ok?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:41:52 UTC
00:41:52 KST

Latest wave: 2 November 2026
Based on incident dates in news reports ↗
Are the fridges ok?
Supermarket refrigeration systems are still being hacked. Internet-exposed Danfoss controllers are part of the problem.
How we got here
Our baseline counts each IP once, even when it exposes several StoreView services. Shodan-tagged honeypot records are excluded, but an IP with both tagged and untagged records can remain. IP addresses are a proxy for systems, not a count of physical fridges.
Indexed StoreView IPs · tagged service records excluded
Loading measurement…
Loading total including tagged honeypots…
One IP.
Many fridges.
One public IP can front several controllers, and each controller can manage many fridges. Exposure alone does not prove compromise.
What we're measuring ↗
Still out in the open.
Historical service totals before IP deduplication and honeypot filtering. These use a different unit from the distinct-IP baseline above; they do not count compromised fridges.
These totals include tagged honeypots. Historical sources did not provide a honeypot breakdown. Published observations may use different search details and collection times. Gaps are unmeasured; points are not interpolated.
View observations & sources
Is the exposed fleet getting newer?
Daily inferred firmware mix across indexed Danfoss StoreView services worldwide. This chart is weighted by services, so one IP can contribute multiple ports; the headline baseline counts distinct IPs. These daily versions come from cached headers.
A rising share of newer versions can reflect upgrades, older systems going offline, new arrivals, or changes in Shodan's index. This chart does not follow individual devices or prove upgrades. Multiple ports can still inflate counts, and untagged honeypots may remain.
Latest version breakdown & methodology
Exact cached Last-Modified fingerprints are matched to the firmware-build timestamps published by Censys. Shared fingerprints remain ambiguous; unmatched banners remain unknown. The chart uses the same StoreView query as the headline count, with an optional honeypot exclusion. Newer StoreView Web titles were present in this query during our baseline census; this is still not a complete inventory of all Danfoss products.
Counts are collected from Shodan's existing index, not from a live vulnerability test. The queries run sequentially and are not a single atomic snapshot. The headline uses a separate distinct-IP snapshot; this chart counts services. Exposure and firmware totals may differ because Shodan answers those API requests separately. The collector rejects changing population totals or inconsistent sums and preserves the last valid observation. “4.3.1 or 5.x” is a version grouping, not an assurance that equipment is secure. Download full measurement history ↗
A pattern, not a one-off.
From suspected attacks at military bases to repeated disruption in European supermarkets.
The problem predates 2026. Danfoss also documented attacks on supermarket refrigeration in Russia and Israel in 2022. Danfoss newsletter, page 9 ↗
Cold facts.
Clear limitations.
A quiet news cycle is not an all-clear.
What does “No” mean?
This is an editorial assessment of the reported attacks and continuing exposure, not a live test of every fridge. It does not automatically turn to “Yes” after a quiet stretch.
What resets the counter?
A reviewed, substantial attack wave that disrupts refrigeration at multiple sites. We use the incident date, not the date of a later article. Suspected incidents are labeled; a new headline about an old incident does not reset the clock. Days use UTC calendar dates.
What does the Shodan number measure?
The prominent baseline counts distinct IP addresses with at least one matching service not tagged by Shodan as a honeypot; the small-print totals include tagged service records. An IP with both tagged and untagged records remains eligible. The exposure history retains unfiltered historical totals, while the firmware chart excludes tagged honeypots by default. All counts are matching internet service banners, worldwide, for http.title:"Danfoss StoreView". It is a narrow fingerprint, not all Danfoss products. Multiple ports can inflate the total; one controller can serve many fridges. Shodan's index also lags real-world changes. Download distinct-IP history ↗ · API methodology ↗
What did the one-off landing-page measurement find?
On 3 November 2026, a separately authorized active measurement made one ordinary unauthenticated request to / at one cached endpoint for each of 2,050 eligible IPs. It recognized 212 StoreView landing pages; 1,409 requests timed out and 332 had TLS failures. The remaining 97 produced other pages, responses or errors. Another 51 IPs had no eligible cached endpoint. There were no retries, redirect following, logins, scripts or controller changes.
This is a conservative observation, not a complete live-device count: timeouts and TLS failures do not establish that equipment is offline. Among recognized pages, 205 retained the cached firmware inference and seven differed or became unknown; this does not establish seven upgrades. Visible UI versions are not controller firmware. A later code audit reproduced an undercount risk: the original reader could receive a valid title, then time out waiting for more body data. The number of affected timeouts cannot be recovered, so 212 is only the number recognized by that checker. The checker has since been corrected and tested locally; this measurement has not been rerun.
A later cached Shodan query found 106 IPs with honeypot-tagged StoreView records. The original filter excluded tagged records, rather than every IP with any tagged record. The overlap between those IPs and the 212 recognized pages is unknown because no IP-to-outcome linkage was retained. Download the cached honeypot audit ↗. Only aggregate results are retained. Daily updates continue to use cached Shodan data; this active measurement is not scheduled. Download the aggregate report ↗
How does this stay up to date?
GitHub Actions requests aggregate Shodan IP, service and firmware counts daily at 11:17 UTC and saves their dated history. News is reviewed separately each day by a scheduled Codex task; its last completed review is shown above. Failed collections preserve prior data and show its age. Historical published snapshots remain separate from our own samples.
How can operators reduce exposure?
Follow the manufacturer's and your national CERT's guidance: restrict access to management interfaces, use protected remote access, and apply supported updates. Internet exposure does not by itself identify an exploited vulnerability. Read CERT-FR's advisory ↗

Bear turns 10, and it still feels like day one
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:41:46 UTC
00:41:46 KST

Back in 2013, three friends who loved Apple and design couldn’t find a notes app they liked, so they built their own. That side project became Bear. It arrived on the App Store in November 2016, and within a year it had been named Mac App of the Year and won an Apple Design Award. This November, the little Bear turns 10. Today it helps millions of people around the world capture their ideas and organize their lives every day, and we’ve felt so much love and support from all of you along the way.
The idea
We couldn’t find a notes app that fit how we write, so we started building our own.
More
We built it in our spare time. Our day job back then was building apps and websites for clients.
First prototype
Our friends loved it. Would everyone else?
Launch day!
Bear arrives on the App Store and hits #1 on Product Hunt.
Mac App of the Year
Apple names Bear its Mac App of the Year.
Apple Design Award
Bear wins a 2017 Apple Design Award.
More
…and we got a selfie with Tim Cook!
Project Panda
We start rewriting Bear’s editor from scratch, code name “Panda”.
More
Your feedback showed us where the original Bear was hitting its limits. Six months in, we realized we needed our own Markdown parser too, so we started over. Again.
Bear 2 is here!
Bear 2 launches with an all-new editor and 20+ new features.
Bear Web beta
Bear comes to your browser in public beta.
Bear Web
The full release of Bear Web is almost here, and we have more features brewing in house…
Over the years, one thing we hear again and again is that Bear just feels good to use. So for this special occasion, we want to share the principles behind every design decision we make. Along the way you’ll find some tiny details we’ve hidden all over the app (you may be using a few right now without knowing it), a peek at an idea that never shipped, and some brand new Bear swag. Grab a slice of cake and come on in!
Bear Principles
Every change in Bear goes through a long journey from first idea to final release. Big or small, visible or under the hood, they all follow the same two principles: Bear should be delightful to use, and safe to trust.
A feeling-good experience by design
We want you to feel delighted every time you open Bear. In practice, that comes down to three things.
Simple. No sign up, no tutorial. Just open a note and write. Bear has grown much more powerful over the years, but all of that power waits quietly underneath and never gets in your way.
💡 Did you know?
To make sure Bear stays easy to use, we always ask our parents to test it first!
Natural. We believe the best tools disappear while you use them. So we put a lot of effort into making things work exactly the way you expect, and you’ll find these quality of life touches all over the app.
Take moving lines around. Press ⌘⌥ with the up or down arrow to move the current line, which is perfect for rearranging your writing. Bear understands what you’re moving and where it’s going, so it won’t break apart a list or a table along the way. It’s one of those things that feels so obvious you’d never guess someone had to design it.
Rewarding to explore. Bear has a lot of depth, but we don’t believe in pushing everything to your face to promote it because we don’t know when or if you will need them. Many of our favorite user stories start with a happy little accident. Resize an image from its corner, and it snaps to line up with the images around it; swipe left on a note on iOS, and the Info Panel slides into view. We love those “Oh wow, I didn’t expect that to work!” moments. They’re a big part of why we sign off every post with “Happy writing!”
💡 Did you know?
Some of our favorite details are so small that you may be using them without ever noticing. Here are a few to try:
✍️ Typing in bold or italic? Press Return or the same shortcut again to step right out of the style.
⌨️ Press ⌘A inside a code block to select just the code. Press it again to select the whole note.
🖱️ Select some text and type (, [, " or`. Bear wraps your selection instead of replacing it.
🔗 Select some text and paste a URL. The text becomes a link.
Built to be safe
You can’t feel truly comfortable in a space you don’t trust. Your notes hold your ideas, your plans, and sometimes things you’d never share with anyone. That’s why safety has shaped Bear’s most fundamental choices from the very start.
Private. We have no servers holding your notes, and we never see what you write. We also don’t build Bear on top of third party APIs, plugins, or outside services we can’t control. If one of them changed its rules, shut down, or simply stopped working, you would be the one paying the price. So we build what matters ourselves, and Bear keeps working the way you expect.
💡 Did you know?
We collaborated with Cossack Labs for note encryption to ensure a reliable and smooth experience. Even if your iCloud account were ever hacked, you can rest assured your encrypted notes stay unreadable.
Keeping a note taking app private and standard wasn’t a revolutionary idea in 2016 when we first launched Bear, but today, it kind of is. And we are glad we stick to what we believe since day 1. The world is changing and we don’t know where it leads us to, but we know your notes are safe with us and you have full control of them anytime, anywhere.
How features get made
Every design in Bear starts with a problem: a feature people need, something that’s broken, or simply something we don’t like. From there, we are on a journey of problem solving.
That usually means a designer and a developer pairing up for a week or two, then bringing something working to the whole team. Then the real fun begins. Matteo, one of our cofounders, describes fun as “clashing heads with wonderful people to make great things.” Everyone in the team gets to express their opinions no matter what title they have. As a matter of fact, job titles mean nothing to us as you can see on our website.
The feedback discussion can be brutal. “No” is a very welcome answer here, with one house rule: you have to give a reason. Wall of text and hour long slack huddles come and go, then the paring up buddies return to the improvement part and provide a second round, a third round, until we are all happy with it. There are lucky days that some ideas get a big yes on the first try. Others spark weeks and months of debate and never ship at all. We’d rather be a little stubborn about what feels like Bear than chase every trend.
💡 Did you know?
Back in 2022, we had the idea to create a fourth column for the info panel, TOC, and Backlinks in Bear 2 update. The concept was discussed internally and also shared with on our forum, but at the end we were not convinced by the design at practical level, and thus the feature was never born in Bear 2.
A milestone, and more to come
Over the years, Bear has grown up much bigger than we expected with millions of users enjoying Bear everyday. We’ve seen so many different ways of using Bear: prepare teaching materials in school, work as their second brain, or just write grocery lists (yes, that’s our parents!). 10 years is a huge milestone for us and we want to say a huge thank you to everyone no matter if you have been here since day 1 with us, or just downloaded Bear two minutes ago.
We won’t hand you a flashy roadmap, but here’s what we can say for sure. Bear Web is almost ready for its full release, and we’re also working on note versioning and some other top secret things. Beyond that, we’ll keep doing what we’ve done for ten years: working our best to provide you a comfortable, private space to create beautiful notes.
🎁 Present time!
Kumagotchi: a writing pal for your Home Screen
Meet Kumagotchi1, a cute pixel bear who lives in a widget on your Home Screen and keeps you company while you write. Its status quietly follows how you use Bear: it strolls around the meadow when you’ve opened Bear recently, scribbles in its own little notebook while you write, watches the clouds while you read, and takes a nap in the grass when you’ve been away for a while. It’s as old as your very first note, and its weight grows with every note you write.
Six new app icons
What’s a birthday without a little dress up? We’ve crafted six new app icons2 for the occasion: a special 10th anniversary icon, Bear with Birdie, a golden Bear, a peeking Bear, and two classic pixel Bears. We hope they bring a bit of fun to your Home Screen, and a gentle reminder to enjoy the little things in life.
Did somebody say Bear swag?
Yes, they are finally here! Many of you have been asking for Bear merch and we have been secretly working on them! Our 10th anniversary merch features pixel art of a classic Macintosh with the Bear logo glowing on its screen and our little Birdie perched on top, a nod to where it all began. Check our merch page below to grab your Bear swag!
See you in the next decade
Thank you for being part of the story, whether you’ve been here since day one or found Bear last week. We’d love to hear about your favorite hidden detail, or one you just discovered in this post. Come say happy birthday to us on our Community Forum, Reddit, X (Twitter), Bluesky, or directly at bear@shinyfrog.net.
Happy Writing!
🐻❤️📝

Bear Web
The full release of Bear Web is almost here, and we have more features brewing in house…
Over the years, one thing we hear again and again is that Bear just feels good to use. So for this special occasion, we want to share the principles behind every design decision we make. Along the way you’ll find some tiny details we’ve hidden all over the app (you may be using a few right now without knowing it), a peek at an idea that never shipped, and some brand new Bear swag. Grab a slice of cake and come on in!
Bear Principles
Every change in Bear goes through a long journey from first idea to final release. Big or small, visible or under the hood, they all follow the same two principles: Bear should be delightful to use, and safe to trust.
A feeling-good experience by design
We want you to feel delighted every time you open Bear. In practice, that comes down to three things.
Simple. No sign up, no tutorial. Just open a note and write. Bear has grown much more powerful over the years, but all of that power waits quietly underneath and never gets in your way.
💡 Did you know?
To make sure Bear stays easy to use, we always ask our parents to test it first!
Natural. We believe the best tools disappear while you use them. So we put a lot of effort into making things work exactly the way you expect, and you’ll find these quality of life touches all over the app.
Take moving lines around. Press ⌘⌥ with the up or down arrow to move the current line, which is perfect for rearranging your writing. Bear understands what you’re moving and where it’s going, so it won’t break apart a list or a table along the way. It’s one of those things that feels so obvious you’d never guess someone had to design it.
Rewarding to explore. Bear has a lot of depth, but we don’t believe in pushing everything to your face to promote it because we don’t know when or if you will need them. Many of our favorite user stories start with a happy little accident. Resize an image from its corner, and it snaps to line up with the images around it; swipe left on a note on iOS, and the Info Panel slides into view. We love those “Oh wow, I didn’t expect that to work!” moments. They’re a big part of why we sign off every post with “Happy writing!”
💡 Did you know?
Some of our favorite details are so small that you may be using them without ever noticing. Here are a few to try:
✍️ Typing in bold or italic? Press Return or the same shortcut again to step right out of the style.
⌨️ Press ⌘A inside a code block to select just the code. Press it again to select the whole note.
🖱️ Select some text and type (, [, " or`. Bear wraps your selection instead of replacing it.
🔗 Select some text and paste a URL. The text becomes a link.
Built to be safe
You can’t feel truly comfortable in a space you don’t trust. Your notes hold your ideas, your plans, and sometimes things you’d never share with anyone. That’s why safety has shaped Bear’s most fundamental choices from the very start.
Private. We have no servers holding your notes, and we never see what you write. We also don’t build Bear on top of third party APIs, plugins, or outside services we can’t control. If one of them changed its rules, shut down, or simply stopped working, you would be the one paying the price. So we build what matters ourselves, and Bear keeps working the way you expect.
💡 Did you know?
We collaborated with Cossack Labs for note encryption to ensure a reliable and smooth experience. Even if your iCloud account were ever hacked, you can rest assured your encrypted notes stay unreadable.
Keeping a note taking app private and standard wasn’t a revolutionary idea in 2016 when we first launched Bear, but today, it kind of is. And we are glad we stick to what we believe since day 1. The world is changing and we don’t know where it leads us to, but we know your notes are safe with us and you have full control of them anytime, anywhere.
How features get made
Every design in Bear starts with a problem: a feature people need, something that’s broken, or simply something we don’t like. From there, we are on a journey of problem solving.
That usually means a designer and a developer pairing up for a week or two, then bringing something working to the whole team. Then the real fun begins. Matteo, one of our cofounders, describes fun as “clashing heads with wonderful people to make great things.” Everyone in the team gets to express their opinions no matter what title they have. As a matter of fact, job titles mean nothing to us as you can see on our website.
The feedback discussion can be brutal. “No” is a very welcome answer here, with one house rule: you have to give a reason. Wall of text and hour long slack huddles come and go, then the paring up buddies return to the improvement part and provide a second round, a third round, until we are all happy with it. There are lucky days that some ideas get a big yes on the first try. Others spark weeks and months of debate and never ship at all. We’d rather be a little stubborn about what feels like Bear than chase every trend.
💡 Did you know?
Back in 2022, we had the idea to create a fourth column for the info panel, TOC, and Backlinks in Bear 2 update. The concept was discussed internally and also shared with on our forum, but at the end we were not convinced by the design at practical level, and thus the feature was never born in Bear 2.
A milestone, and more to come
Over the years, Bear has grown up much bigger than we expected with millions of users enjoying Bear everyday. We’ve seen so many different ways of using Bear: prepare teaching materials in school, work as their second brain, or just write grocery lists (yes, that’s our parents!). 10 years is a huge milestone for us and we want to say a huge thank you to everyone no matter if you have been here since day 1 with us, or just downloaded Bear two minutes ago.
We won’t hand you a flashy roadmap, but here’s what we can say for sure. Bear Web is almost ready for its full release, and we’re also working on note versioning and some other top secret things. Beyond that, we’ll keep doing what we’ve done for ten years: working our best to provide you a comfortable, private space to create beautiful notes.
🎁 Present time!
Kumagotchi: a writing pal for your Home Screen
Meet Kumagotchi1, a cute pixel bear who lives in a widget on your Home Screen and keeps you company while you write. Its status quietly follows how you use Bear: it strolls around the meadow when you’ve opened Bear recently, scribbles in its own little notebook while you write, watches the clouds while you read, and takes a nap in the grass when you’ve been away for a while. It’s as old as your very first note, and its weight grows with every note you write.
Six new app icons
What’s a birthday without a little dress up? We’ve crafted six new app icons2 for the occasion: a special 10th anniversary icon, Bear with Birdie, a golden Bear, a peeking Bear, and two classic pixel Bears. We hope they bring a bit of fun to your Home Screen, and a gentle reminder to enjoy the little things in life.
Did somebody say Bear swag?
Yes, they are finally here! Many of you have been asking for Bear merch and we have been secretly working on them! Our 10th anniversary merch features pixel art of a classic Macintosh with the Bear logo glowing on its screen and our little Birdie perched on top, a nod to where it all began. Check our merch page below to grab your Bear swag!
See you in the next decade
Thank you for being part of the story, whether you’ve been here since day one or found Bear last week. We’d love to hear about your favorite hidden detail, or one you just discovered in this post. Come say happy birthday to us on our Community Forum, Reddit, X (Twitter), Bluesky, or directly at bear@shinyfrog.net.
Happy Writing!
🐻❤️📝

====================================================================================================

Apple restricts dating apps and AI companions to users 18 and up
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:38:27 UTC
00:38:27 KST

Apple is adjusting its App Store policies to make it more difficult for minors to use online dating apps or AI companions that engage in “sexual or romantic content,” the tech giant shared in an announcement to developers on Monday.
The new rules follow other changes the company has made to better protect minors, which include the rollout of its age assurance technology globally and more parental controls and child safety features.
While most major dating services typically restrict their platform to adults, Apple’s new policy will require all apps offering dating or romantic relationship features to carry an 18+ rating. Starting November 16, developers will be required to include new content descriptors to ensure their app’s age ratings reflect its features, the company said.
It’s perhaps more interesting how this rule could impact AI companion apps, or those where such functionality is one of many features. Often targeted at younger people, Character.AI, Replika, and other AI apps let people create AI companions, including friends, lovers, boyfriends and girlfriends, and other fantasy characters.
xAI last year also launched 3D animated AI companions that allowed users to unlock NSFW capabilities. As of last summer, top AI companion apps across the Apple App Store and Google Play had been downloaded 220 million times globally.
In addition to “dating and relationships,” apps will also need to indicate if they are sharing the real-world locations of a user with another person or group as part of their functionality. Any app doing so raises obvious privacy concerns, especially if the user wasn’t aware that their location was being shared.
The company noted that in Brazil, apps that provide location-sharing features for families can request an official rating from the government’s Ministry of Justice and Public Security (MJSP/Ministério da Justiça e Segurança Pública), instead of being required to list them with the A16 rating, as the local law requires.

Study estimates 75% of NFL players who died over 15-year span had CTE
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:38:04 UTC
00:38:04 KST

An estimated 75% of professional football players who died over a 15-year span had signs of chronic traumatic encephalopathy, or CTE, a study published Wednesday found.
It’s the highest estimate to date. Most recently, a study published in August by the same group estimated that one-quarter of NFL players who died in a five-year span had CTE. The new study, published in JAMA Network Open, put the figure at three times higher.
Not all people with signs of CTE in the brain go on to experience cognitive decline or dementia, and some people may never experience symptoms at all. A smaller proportion of the former players in the analysis were estimated to have dementia, about 27%, the study found.
The results were surprising, said study leader Yorghos Tripodis, the director of statistics and data management at the Boston University Alzheimer’s Disease and CTE Center. Boston University houses the UNITE Brain Bank, which studies the effects of repetitive head trauma in donated brains, including the brains of athletes, military personnel and others.
“I had to double-check the numbers,” Tripodis said. Still, he said, the number is a best guess. “Right now we don’t know how to diagnose CTE when people are alive, so we are trying to estimate.”
In an emailed statement, an NFL spokesperson said, in part, that the league “continuously strives to make the game of football safer, including by implementing strategies to reduce concussions and head impacts.”
Repeated blows and bias
When a person’s head jolts during an impact, the brain — which is suspended in fluid inside the skull — can slam into the skull or twist, said Dr. Julian Bailes, chair of the neurosurgery department at Endeavor Health in the Chicago area. Each blow is an opportunity for proteins called tau to build up in the brain.
“If that happens over and over again, that damage accrues,” said Bailes, who wasn’t involved with the new research.
The disease can affect parts of the brain involved with regulating behavior and emotions, which can lead to memory loss, depression, violent mood swings and other symptoms. Those symptoms can also be linked to other illnesses.
Although CTE can cause symptoms similar to those of other neurodegenerative diseases, such as dementia and Parkinson’s disease, the signature it leaves on the brain is unique, Bailes said.
One huge hurdle in studying the prevalence of CTE is that the microscopic changes don’t show up on brain scans. That means the condition can be diagnosed only during an autopsy.
Past research has been criticized for bias, since people with cognitive decline and their families are much likelier to either donate their brains to brain banks after they die or ask for autopsies — a previous study by Boston University researchers found that 99% of the brains donated to research by former NFL players had signs of CTE.
In the new study, Tripodis and his team tried to remove as much of that bias as possible to determine a more accurate estimate of CTE rates in former NFL players. They combined data from the UNITE Brain Bank with national death records of other former NFL players who died from 2008 to 2023.
In total, the researchers looked at data from 396 donated brains, plus information from 1,966 other deceased former NFL players. They factored in the ages of death along with estimated rates of mild cognitive impairment and dementia in former NFL players older than 50.
About 93% of the donated brains had signs of CTE, which shows up as structural damage in the brain. The research team calculated that the families of players with symptoms of cognitive decline were about five times likelier to donate brains than the families of players without symptoms and then performed a statistical analysis to correct for that bias.
FOR SUBSCRIBERSDOJ is deploying a record number of monitors on Election Day
FOR SUBSCRIBERSHow NBC News has prepared to cover election night
FOR SUBSCRIBERSWelcome to the gas price election
FOR SUBSCRIBERSThe fight for young men comes to Michigan
FOR SUBSCRIBERSTrump holds final rally of a 28-stop midterm blitz
FOR SUBSCRIBERSDOJ is deploying a record number of monitors on Election Day
FOR SUBSCRIBERSHow NBC News has prepared to cover election night
FOR SUBSCRIBERSWelcome to the gas price election
FOR SUBSCRIBERSThe fight for young men comes to Michigan
FOR SUBSCRIBERSTrump holds final rally of a 28-stop midterm blitz
From there, they estimated that among the roughly 2,360 former players who died during the 15-year time frame, about 1,760, or nearly 75%, had CTE. In comparison, only about 1% of brains donated to banks in the U.S., Austria and Brazil have signs of the disease, the researchers said.
But plenty of people with signs of CTE never develop symptoms, Tripodis said.
The researchers estimated that 27% of the 2,360 players studied had dementia.
The dementia “can be from all causes,” Tripodis said. “But it is very likely that in this specific population that CTE is causing dementia.”
Reducing impacts
Until doctors figure out a way to diagnose CTE while people are still alive, any studies quantifying how many people have the condition will be estimates, said Dr. Jim Kelly, a professor emeritus of neurology at the University of Colorado Anschutz.
“These are all estimates. They had no autopsies on five times as many players who died as they did have autopsies for,” said Kelly, who wasn’t involved in the research.
Some of the autopsies may also have revealed just one or a couple of lesions associated with CTE, “and it seems unlikely that that would lead to anything in terms of a behavior change or dementia,” he said.
Those are difficult studies to do, Bailes agreed. They seek to determine “how many people out there have a condition when not all of them are symptomatic,” he said, adding that the findings are a call for more research into diagnosing CTE in people who are still living.
“If you can’t tell if someone has a certain disease until they’re dead, you really can’t help them,” he said.
In the meantime, prevention is key, Tripodis said: “If you reduce the number of brain injuries, brain impacts, you substantially decrease the risk.”
The NFL has already implemented measures to try to reduce the number of high-impact hits players face. They include redesigned helmets — such as the Guardian Cap — and the dynamic kickoff rule, which leads to lower collision speeds.

Kalshi and its co-founders face felony charges for violating Utah gambling laws
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:29:15 UTC
00:29:15 KST

The Trump administration is trying to stop Utah from enforcing its gambling ban against Kalshi, making Utah the 10th state sued by the US government agency that regulates prediction markets.
Meanwhile, Utah Attorney General Derek Brown filed criminal charges in a state court against Kalshi and its founders on Wednesday. The attorney general’s office provided us with court filings listing charges against KalshiEX, LLC, Kalshi co-founder and CEO Tarek Mansour, and Kalshi co-founder and COO Luana Lopes Lara.
The company and two co-founders each face 13 felony charges and one misdemeanor for violating gambling laws. The charges are based on sports bets offered on Kalshi between September 13 and September 24. “The state did not issue arrest warrants for Monsour and Lopes Lara. Instead, they have been issued summons to respond to the charges in Third District Court in Salt Lake County,” The Salt Lake Tribune wrote.
The Utah News Dispatch quoted a Kalshi statement vowing to fight the charges. “This is a crazy overreach; Utah is clearly playing politics. We look forward to putting a stop to this in court, just like we did in Arizona,” the statement said, referring to similar charges the company beat in Arizona in May.
Utah, which prohibits gambling, filed a civil lawsuit against Kalshi in a state court last week. Utah’s lawsuit seeks an order prohibiting Kalshi from offering bets on sports, elections, politics, and other events.
“Kalshi runs an online casino in Utah and calls it a prediction market. That violates Utah law,” Utah Attorney General Derek Brown, a Republican, said last week. “Not only do our state laws ban gambling but Utah is one of only two states where gambling is also prohibited under the state constitution. For 130 years, Utah has been clear: gambling has no place here, and we will enforce our laws.”
Utah already defeated Kalshi in federal court after the prediction market filed a preemptive lawsuit that sought to prevent the state from enforcing its gambling laws. Now that Utah is moving ahead in its attempt to shut down Kalshi, it must defend against a lawsuit filed by the US Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC).

Designing responsible AI for learning
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:25:47 UTC
00:25:47 KST

Designing responsible AI for learning
If you’ve ever watched a student use AI tools to help with their schoolwork, you’ve probably wondered: Are they actually learning, or just finding a shortcut? Questions like this are important, and they guide our work when building products for learning.
We believe technology paired with great educators can unlock incredible learning potential, and we’re focused on building technology responsibly to help students thrive. Today, we’re making important updates to our products used in schools, including building Guided Learning capabilities into Google Search and Google Classroom, and evolving our approach to school controls.
Developing products built for learning
We believe AI products should support “productive struggle” — enabling students to be actively challenged without getting so overwhelmed that they give up. That’s the philosophy behind Guided Learning. In Gemini, Guided Learning acts as an interactive study tool that coaches students through the process of problem solving through open-ended questions and step-by-step breakdowns. Our research shows that Guided Learning leads to measurable learning gains for students, and we’re now bringing Guided Learning to more products.
- Google Search: We’re building Guided Learning capabilities into Search for student accounts to help them work through problems without skipping straight to answers. When our systems understand a student is looking for certain types of homework help, Search will walk them through the topic or problem step by step. For example, if a student pastes a complex physics problem or asks for help writing a book report in Search, they’ll see prompts to help them think critically and work through the concepts, without going directly to the answer. We’re starting with a pilot in select U.S. schools later this month to gather and incorporate educator feedback before expanding to other school districts in the coming months.
Google Classroom: Starting today, Guided Learning is globally available in Google Classroom, a central hub where teachers and students organize schoolwork, share assignments and stay connected. When a student starts a Guided Learning activity assigned by their teacher, it acts like a 1-1 study tool, anchored in class material to help them stay on task. It also gives teachers real-time insights to spot where the class might need extra support.
Before launching Guided Learning in Google Classroom, we worked closely with schools and educators to test the feature and integrate their feedback.
Enhancing school controls
We believe schools should have control over AI in their classrooms and in providing administrators with controls to help them manage their approach. After listening to schools, teachers, leading researchers, and policymakers, we are now updating our approach to controls for Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search. Access to these products will be entirely off for K-12 student accounts until school administrators explicitly turn them on.
We’ll share more specifics with schools in the coming months ahead of the changes rolling out so they have time to prepare and enable a smooth transition. Our goal is simple: provide school leaders the control and confidence to use AI products in classrooms as they see fit.
We’ll continue to listen to feedback and evolve our approach as we work in partnership with schools, teachers, pedagogical experts, and parents on helping the next generation learn and thrive.

Enhancing school controls
We believe schools should have control over AI in their classrooms and in providing administrators with controls to help them manage their approach. After listening to schools, teachers, leading researchers, and policymakers, we are now updating our approach to controls for Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search. Access to these products will be entirely off for K-12 student accounts until school administrators explicitly turn them on.
We’ll share more specifics with schools in the coming months ahead of the changes rolling out so they have time to prepare and enable a smooth transition. Our goal is simple: provide school leaders the control and confidence to use AI products in classrooms as they see fit.
We’ll continue to listen to feedback and evolve our approach as we work in partnership with schools, teachers, pedagogical experts, and parents on helping the next generation learn and thrive.
