Apple is all in on AI — its own and everyone else&#8217;s
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:24:51 UTC
00:24:51 KST

Apple’s latest earnings show how it’s continuing to capitalize on the AI boom without busting its budget on the infrastructure spending consuming its competitors.
Apple is all in on AI — its own and everyone else’s
AI was mentioned 45 times on the quarterly earnings call.
Overall, Apple posted its best-ever fiscal year for revenue and earnings per share. iPhone and Mac sales continued to grow strongly in the fourth quarter. iPhone revenue rose 17.5% from a year earlier to a September quarter record of $57.6 billion while Mac revenue climbed 24% to $10.8 billion, its highest level in four years. Shares nevertheless fell in premarket trading Tuesday after Apple warned that rising memory costs would squeeze its profit margins in the holiday quarter.
Apple hasn’t disclosed how much of its revenue growth reflects AI demand or higher prices, but its executives clearly see the technology as a way to drive further sales.
During the earnings call, CFO Kevan Parekh cited a “wide range of ways that AI can contribute to revenue growth over time.”
“On the product side, we feel AI offers a compelling reason for customers to upgrade and switch in order to get the latest capabilities,” he said. “And we also feel there’s opportunities across third-party apps and partnerships as well as first-party opportunities.”
Apple can sell its giant user base the devices they use for AI, even if the AI itself comes from someone else. It can collect fees on subscriptions to AI apps. And it can charge customers who want more access to its own AI features.
“We see tremendous opportunity in AI,” John Ternus said on his first earnings call as CEO.
AI and Apple Intelligence were mentioned a record 45 times on the earnings call, according to financial research platform AlphaSense — even more than on the call following Apple’s unveiling of the technology and plans for a revamped Siri in June 2024.
Mentions of AI or “Apple Intelligence” on earnings calls
On the call, Apple continued to position itself as the interface through which consumers use AI — including competitors’ technology. With more than 2.5 billion active devices, it already has an enormous audience for whatever AI services emerge.
Apple, unlike its Big Tech peers, isn’t pouring comparable sums into infrastructure. Its approach combines on-device processing, its own servers, and outside AI technology. Siri AI uses models developed with Google’s Gemini technology while Apple reportedly plans to let users choose among outside AI providers for features such as writing tools and image generation.
Apple spent $2.2 billion on capital expenditures last quarter, compared with Amazon’s $77.3 billion and Alphabet’s $51.4 billion. Those totals cover companywide CapEx, not just AI, and exclude some AI expenses. Apple’s operating expenses rose 21% to $19.2 billion as it increased R&D spending, including on AI. Siri AI’s cloud costs will appear in services cost of sales. Those costs could grow with usage: Parekh said it’s too early to predict how many requests will require servers rather than on-device processing. Still, Apple’s capital spending remains just a fraction of its peers’.
More AI usage also gives Apple something to sell.
Siri AI is only beginning to reach customers. Apple launched the revamped assistant in beta for US English users in September and expanded it to French, Japanese, Korean, Portuguese, and Spanish in October.
Apple plans to turn some of that usage into subscription revenue. Ternus said features relying on server-side models, including Siri AI and intelligent photo editing, will have usage limits. Customers who need more capacity “will be able to purchase expanded access in the future,” he said. “In the beginning, iCloud tiers were largely about storage, but we’ve been adding more capabilities and features to it.”
Apple also benefits when customers buy its hardware to run AI locally, including models developed by other companies. Mac, Ternus said, is “having an AI moment all its own.”
“We feel AI offers a compelling reason for customers to upgrade and switch in order to get the latest capabilities.”
Apple offered specific examples of companies putting its computers to work on AI. Gap is running AI entirely on Mac Mini computers for loss prevention across hundreds of stores, avoiding cloud token costs for those tasks. Christie’s is running local models on Mac Studio computers to improve workflows including pricing estimates.
For those customers, buying Apple hardware provides a way to use AI without paying a cloud provider for every request.
Mac Mini and Mac Studio, Ternus said, “are pushing the boundaries of local, private AI, giving users the ability to do things once considered impossible.”
The Mac Mini’s emergence as an AI machine has given the small, screenless desktop a new constituency among developers and hobbyists looking for a relatively affordable way to run models locally. Earlier this year, then-CEO Tim Cook said customer recognition of the Mac Mini and Mac Studio’s capabilities for AI and agentic tools was happening faster than Apple had predicted, leading to higher-than-expected demand.
Apple also makes money when customers subscribe to someone else’s AI. Earlier this year, The Wall Street Journal reported that Apple was on pace to bring in more than $1 billion in App Store fees from generative AI apps in 2026, according to analytics firm AppMagic.
That revenue, of course, depends on customers paying through Apple’s system. OpenAI has begun directing potential subscribers to pay through its website instead of the App Store, bypassing Apple’s commissions, The Wall Street Journal reported. Apple’s position gives it a way to profit from competitors’ AI, but those competitors have an incentive to find a route around its fees.
Still, Apple already has multiple ways to benefit from people using AI. It can sell the computer, collect a subscription commission, and eventually charge for more of its own services. It doesn’t have to build every model — or fund infrastructure on its competitors’ scale — to get paid.

Only 0.5% of 383,000 CVEs are ever exploited in real-world attacks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:17:02 UTC
00:17:02 KST

The vulnerability landscape continues to expand. From 1999 through September 2026, approximately 383,000 software vulnerabilities had been disclosed across platforms and technologies, yet only about 2,000 CVEs (roughly 0.5%) have been linked to real-world exploitation.
Those vulnerabilities follow clear patterns. Threat actors disproportionately target flaws that enable system takeover, particularly remote code execution, privilege escalation, and authentication bypass. AI-assisted vulnerability discovery and exploit development could further shorten the window defenders have to respond.
Patching remains essential, but remediation can take weeks or months on critical systems, and some legacy or unsupported systems cannot be patched. Defenders therefore need to concentrate protection where it can have the greatest impact, considering both the likelihood of exploitation and the potential blast radius of a successful attack.
Classifying the vulnerability landscape
To make this large vulnerability population actionable, we analyzed each CVE across two dimensions: impact class and affected platform.
Impact class captures the outcome a threat actor could achieve by exploiting a vulnerability. Each CVE is assigned to the most consequential impact it directly enables. For example, when a vulnerability can bypass authentication and directly enable remote code execution (RCE), we classify it as RCE. This approach helps distinguish the underlying type of vulnerability from the security impact an attacker could achieve.
- Server-side RCE: Remote code execution on a network-exposed service without user interaction.
- Client-side RCE: Remote code execution that requires a user to open or run malicious content.
- Local code execution: Code or command execution requiring local access or an existing foothold.
- Local privilege escalation: Elevation from lower-level access to SYSTEM or root privileges.
- Authentication or authorization bypass: Circumvention of authentication or access controls.
- Security-control bypass: Circumvention of protections such as sandboxing, secure boot, or SmartScreen.
- Information disclosure: Exposure of sensitive data or secrets.
- Tampering or integrity: Unauthorized modification of data or system state.
- Denial of service: Disruption of system or service availability.
- Cross-site scripting (XSS): Script execution within a victim’s browser context.
- Arbitrary file access: Unauthorized access to or placement of files.
- Spoofing or redirect: Spoofing, redirection, or other forms of UI deception.
We also classified vulnerabilities by the platforms they affect, including Windows, Linux/Unix, mobile, network appliances, IoT/OT, virtualization hosts, and developer packages. A CVE can span multiple platforms. Developer packages are treated as a separate category because they are often cross-platform and represent a significant share of the overall vulnerability population.
Most CVEs in the analysis affect application code, including servers, web applications, browsers, productivity software, libraries, and firmware tooling, while a smaller share affects OS and system code such as kernels, drivers, and firmware.
This analysis covers CVEs published through September 2026 using Microsoft’s catalog of tracked vulnerabilities. Each CVE is counted once in the impact analysis and across each relevant platform in the platform analysis.
Vulnerabilities exploited in the wild
The subset of vulnerabilities exploited in the wild is much smaller than the overall CVE population, and looks very different from it. Fewer than 1 in 200 published CVEs are ever used in a real attack, and those that do get used are overwhelmingly concentrated in the takeover classes that maximize threat actor impact and exposure.
The difference becomes clear when we look at impact. Exploitation is heavily concentrated in vulnerabilities that give attackers greater control over a system. Server-side RCE rises from 20% of published CVEs to 37% of exploited CVEs, local privilege escalation from 11% to 18%, and client-side RCE from 4% to 13%. By comparison, XSS falls from 15% to 4% and denial of service from 16% to 7%. OS vulnerabilities show a similar shift, increasing from 23% of the overall CVE population to 41% of exploited vulnerabilities.
These patterns are consistent with threat actors favoring vulnerabilities that can provide meaningful access and control. Remote code execution and privilege escalation can create footholds that support subsequent stages of an attack, while vulnerabilities that primarily cause a crash or limited script execution may offer less utility for broader compromise.
Importantly, a vulnerability’s stated classification does not always reflect what an attacker can reliably achieve in practice. For example, some vulnerabilities described as enabling RCE may ultimately result only in denial of service under real-world conditions. Understanding the demonstrated impact and exploitability of a vulnerability is therefore critical to effective prioritization.
Where defenders should focus
Patching is slow, while exploitation is fast
Patching is slow, and far slower on critical systems than others. Across platforms, servers are the critical assets and the slowest to patch. Microsoft Security Exposure Management data from 2026 shows that the median vulnerability is patched in about two weeks on Windows workstations but in more than a month on Windows Servers. It stretches to 10+ weeks on Linux Servers and for applications running on MacOS, and beyond a year on network appliances. That gap is the threat actor’s window, widest on the critical systems that stay exposed the longest.
Exploitation runs on the opposite clock: the most impactful vulnerabilities are weaponized in days, increasingly on or before disclosure. According to VulnCheck, 29% of the exploits in 2025 were launched within 1 day of CVE publication. According to CISA, the mean time from CVE publication to CISA KEV catalog fell from 89 days in 2024 to 57 days in 2025. 2026 data up to September already have that number dropping to 20 days, with a median of only 3 days since publication. Industry research suggests that advances in AI are a significant factor in the time reduction required to develop exploits, further increasing pressure on defenders to respond quickly.
Impact, exposure and where to focus
Two things determine a vulnerability’s value to a threat actor: impact and exposure. Impact is what successful exploitation provides, such as remote code execution or privilege escalation. Exposure is an asset’s position in the environment and the opportunities it creates for further compromise. An asset’s exposure is shaped by the identities, privileges, and connections available from it.
Servers often use service accounts, application identities, or cloud-managed identities with permissions beyond their operational needs. Compromising such a system can provide access to additional resources, sensitive data, administrative privileges, or trusted network paths. Together, impact and exposure determine the blast radius: a vulnerability that enables full control of a highly exposed system can enable widespread on-premises or cloud compromise.
Threat actors do not exploit at random: they go after the vulnerabilities that give the highest impact and exposure: full control of a system and the privileged identities and critical assets it has access to. As defenders, focusing on these vulnerabilities first can deliver the highest return on remediation investment.
Takeover vulnerabilities like remote code execution, authentication bypass and local privilege escalation lead here. They drive the broadest exposure and the largest blast radius, account for 78% of real-world exploited vulnerabilities, and stopping their exploitation interrupts attacks at the earliest stage, before compromise can propagate across the environment.
Next in line
A second slice deserves attention where context makes it severe: denial of service against availability-critical assets is recoverable in general, but can be mission-ending in parts of your environment where uptime is everything; disclosure of credentials or secrets can be minor as an insulated incident, but it feeds the same privileged-identity path mentioned above.
How Microsoft Security helps defenders act
Threat actors do not begin with a vulnerability list. They begin by looking for a reachable way in. Microsoft Defender External Attack Surface Management approaches the environment from that same outside-in perspective, continuously discovering internet-facing infrastructure, including previously unknown or unmonitored assets, shadow IT, and third-party dependencies. This gives defenders an opportunity to remove unnecessary exposure before a reachable vulnerability becomes an entry point.
Once the reachable assets are known, the next question is which weaknesses deserve immediate attention. Defenders need a way to prioritize exposure beyond CVSS alone. Microsoft Defender Vulnerability Management addresses this challenge through Exposure Score and prioritized security recommendations.
Prioritization incorporates threat and exploitability signals, breach likelihood, internet exposure, and asset criticality, helping teams focus remediation on vulnerabilities that create the greatest actual exposure.
An initial foothold is only part of the risk. Attack Path Analysis connects assets and threat actor techniques into end-to-end routes from an entry point to a critical asset. Microsoft Security Exposure Management Attack Path Analysis provides a cross-workload view spanning endpoints, cloud, and hybrid environments, using vulnerability context from MDVM, endpoint data from Defender for Endpoint, and cloud asset data from Defender for Cloud. Within cloud environments, Defender CSPM Attack Path Analysis adds detailed context about internet exposure, vulnerabilities, network connections, identities, and permissions across Azure, AWS, and Google Cloud.
Together, these experiences show how an exposed workload could become a path through a privileged identity to sensitive data or another critical resource, helping defenders identify where to break the path before the initial compromise expands.
Patching remains essential, but it is not the only defensive action available. Microsoft Security helps defenders discover the doors threat actors can see, prioritize the weaknesses they are most likely to exploit, understand where a compromise could lead, and identify opportunities to break attack paths before compromise spreads to critical assets.

Octomind – open-source AI harness that saves your tokens, static rust
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:02:58 UTC
00:02:58 KST

Interactive, stdin, daemon, WebSocket, or ACP — one engine, one config.
A supervising model plans and checks the work.
Policy lives in TOML guardrails, not approval prompts.
Website · Docs · Tap Registry · Benchmarks · Issues
Octomind is an open-source coding agent harness. It gives an LLM real access to your repo — reading and writing files, running shells, searching code, delegating to sub-agents — through MCP tools. The same harness runs interactively, non-interactively through stdin, as a background daemon, over WebSocket, or as an ACP agent inside your editor or another agent's stack.
# Interactive
octomind run developer:general
# Piped — CI, scripts, automation
echo "Explain the auth module" | octomind run developer:general --format plain
# Daemon — keep it alive, talk to it from another terminal
echo "watch the build" | octomind run --name watcher --daemon --format jsonl
octomind send --name watcher "run the test suite"
Most coding agents assume a human is watching the screen. Octomind is built for the work that keeps going after you leave.
- Policy as code, not approval popups. Deterministic guardrails — pre-call guards, post-result hooks, post-turn validators — live in TOML and scripts, so they work in CI, daemons, and scheduled runs.
- Proven on real PRs. It solved 24 of 25 held-out tasks at lower cost than the closest competitor. See the benchmarks.
- Ready-made specialists. octomind run developer:general pulls a packaged agent (prompt, model, MCP servers, tool permissions) from a tap. Publish your own with one repo.
- Sessions that stay sharp. Adaptive compression keeps long tasks moving while retaining task knowledge and the live exchange.
- Remembers your rules. Rules and corrections you state in a session become lessons, grounded in your own words and scoped to the project and role, that come back in later sessions. See learning.
- Any model, any budget. Use Octomind Cloud or your own key (OpenRouter, Anthropic, OpenAI, DeepSeek, Ollama, and more), pick a model per role, and cap spend per request and per session. Over budget, a terminal session asks y/N; a non-interactive run stops with exit code 75.
- One config. Models, tools, roles, guardrails, budgets: all TOML, no framework code.
1. Install (macOS, Linux):
curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/muvon/octomind/master/install.sh | bash
On Windows, in PowerShell:
irm https://raw.githubusercontent.com/muvon/octomind/master/install.ps1 | iex
On macOS, you can also install with Homebrew:
brew install muvon/tap/octomind
2. Get model access. The fastest path is Octomind Cloud, which includes models and needs no configuration:
octomind login
Prefer your own provider key? Skip login and export one (also OPENAI_API_KEY, ANTHROPIC_API_KEY, DEEPSEEK_API_KEY), then follow Model access to point the config at it:
export OPENROUTER_API_KEY="your_key"
3. Run a specialist in your project:
cd ~/your/project
octomind run developer:general
You're now in a session with an agent that can read your code, run commands, and edit files. The interface is a plain line prompt with markdown rendering — no TUI to learn, so it works over SSH, in tmux, and in CI logs. The first time you run a specialist, it may install its tools and ask for credentials.
developer:general comes from the default tap muvon/tap, not your local config. Plain octomind run starts the config's default role, assistant:concierge.
Try the recorded bug-fix demo: two failing tests, an agent fix, and an independent test rerun.
Measured on octobench against 25 tasks harvested from merged pull requests across Python, PHP, Rust, C++, and JavaScript. Each agent works in the pre-fix repo; held-out tests from the merged fix decide pass or fail. These are the published July 30, 2026 results, not a measurement of this checkout.
- The harness matters. opencode used the same model and endpoint; octomind solved 24 vs 19 at roughly half the cost. All four agents ran stock invocations with no tuning, so this measures the whole harness, not individual features.
- Worst-case pricing, still ahead. glm-5.2 ran without prompt caching while Opus billed ~97% of context re-reads at cache rates — and octomind still led on solves, cost, and wall time.
- Reproducible. Per-case table, artifacts, and reproduction guide: BENCHMARK.md. Background: blog post.
Drop a .agents/guardrails.toml in your repo. Octomind enforces it deterministically, with no code to compile and no plugin to install.
# Pre-call deny — block a class of calls before they execute
[[guard]]
match   = "shell(command=^rm\\s+-rf?)"
message = "rm -rf blocked."
# Conditional rule — only fires after the agent ran git status this session
[[guard]]
match   = "shell(command=git push)"
when    = ["+shell(command=git status)"]
message = "Review changes before pushing."
If you know Claude Code hooks: guards are PreToolUse denies declared in TOML, hooks are PostToolUse, validators are Stop — no scripts required for the common cases.
Hook and validator script examples: Guardrails guide.
The same harness, exposed wherever your workflow needs it.
Need machine-readable results? Pass --schema schema.json to constrain the final response to a JSON Schema (requires a model with structured-output support). See Structured output.
octomind run developer:general    # general dev, language skills auto-activate
octomind run doctor:blood         # blood-test interpretation specialist
octomind run lawyer:sg            # legal specialist
Running a specialist fetches its manifest from the tap registry, installs any required binaries, resolves credentials, starts the right MCP servers, and then opens the session.
Build and register your own:
octomind tap init yourteam/my-tap   # scaffold, validate, git-init, and register locally
octomind tap yourteam/my-tap        # on another machine, after publishing to GitHub
The octomind- prefix is added automatically: yourteam/my-tap maps to the repository
yourteam/octomind-my-tap.
The install script detects your OS and architecture (Linux and macOS on x86_64/aarch64) and installs to ~/.local/bin/. Pass --install-dir <DIR> or set OCTOMIND_INSTALL_DIR to override. On Windows (x86_64/aarch64), install.ps1 installs to %LOCALAPPDATA%\octomind\bin and adds it to your user PATH; it honors OCTOMIND_INSTALL_DIR and OCTOMIND_VERSION too. install.sh still works from Git Bash or MSYS2.
Command not found? Add the install directory to your PATH:
export PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"
Building from source requires protoc; see Building from source for per-OS prerequisites.
Verify:
octomind --version
octomind config      # generate the default config
octomind run         # start your first session
Shell completions: octomind completion <bash|zsh|fish|elvish|powershell>.
Octomind Cloud — octomind login (device-code sign-in, like gh auth login) stores a gateway key locally. The default config already uses octohub:auto. Learn more.
Bring your own key — set the provider's environment variable (OPENROUTER_API_KEY, OPENAI_API_KEY, ANTHROPIC_API_KEY, DEEPSEEK_API_KEY, …) and point the model tables at it. The supervisor and compression profiles also default to octohub:auto, so update all three:
[model]
name = "anthropic:claude-sonnet-5-5"
[supervisor.model]
name = "anthropic:claude-haiku-5-5"
[compression.model]
name = "anthropic:claude-haiku-5-5"
Provider details and per-role overrides: Providers & models.
Config lives at ~/.local/share/octomind/config/config.toml on macOS and Linux, and under %LOCALAPPDATA%/octomind/config/ on Windows. Set OCTOMIND_DATA_DIR to override the data root.
octomind config --show       # view current config
octomind config --validate   # validate config
Override any value for a single run with -c:
octomind run -c supervisor.enabled=false
Full reference: Configuration reference · Environment variables.
Full list: Session commands.
Octomind reports anonymous usage: which commands, tools, and models are used, plus timings, token counts, and error kinds. It never sends your code, prompts, file paths, tool arguments, or environment values. Opt out in any of three ways:
export DO_NOT_TRACK=1          # the cross-tool standard, honored first
export OCTOMIND_TELEMETRY=0    # for subsequent commands in this shell
# or set `telemetry = false` in config.toml
Exact field list: Telemetry.
Full index: doc/README.md · Hosted: octomind.run/docs.
The most impactful contribution isn't code — it's specialist agents. A cardiologist publishing doctor:medications. A tax attorney publishing lawyer:us. A security researcher publishing security:owasp. One TOML file, and everyone facing that problem gets a specialist-grade AI.
Code contributions are welcome too. Set up the hooks and run the gate before you push:
pip install pre-commit && pre-commit install
make dev    # fmt + clippy + test
Commits follow Conventional Commits. Full guide, architecture overview, and code conventions: CONTRIBUTING.md · internals: Architecture.
Community participation follows our Code of Conduct. Report vulnerabilities privately using the security policy.
Apache License 2.0 — see LICENSE.

OpenAI is quietly renaming data centers ‘compute campuses’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
15:00:44 UTC
00:00:44 KST

People hate data centers. They’re loud, resource-hungry eyesores that are so profoundly unpopular they have managed to achieve the near-impossible in today’s polarized political landscape: genuine bipartisan opposition. But what about “compute campuses”? Those sound lovely, right?
OpenAI is quietly renaming data centers ‘compute campuses’
Critics say OpenAI should tackle people’s concerns about data centers, not rebrand them.
OpenAI seems to be gambling that a quiet rebrand could be all it takes to defuse the growing political backlash that threatens to derail the infrastructure fueling the AI boom.
OpenAI debuted the new terminology on Monday in an announcement launching its new “Grid Protectors Program.” The initiative, starting with an upcoming site in Effingham County, Georgia, aims to address concerns about the strain its sprawling AI infrastructure places on local power grids, particularly during extreme weather and emergencies. It’s a familiar data center problem. Yet in the roughly 900-word announcement, the term “data center” appeared just twice, outnumbered by “compute campus.”
The initiative addresses worries about electricity use and the prospect of consumers being left to foot the bill for the AI buildout. OpenAI promises to “pay the costs we create” and “help communities keep the lights on.” Its two mentions of “data center” reinforce the connection: One references US data center capacity and electricity consumption, while the other directly compares OpenAI’s new “compute facilities” with “data centers built even a few years ago.” They’re “much different,” OpenAI says, with the new campuses allowing the company to be rather vaguely “flexible in our power demand.”
The terminology appears to be new for OpenAI. A quick search of its website turned up no previous uses of the term. The timing is noteworthy, too. On Monday, Axios reported that OpenAI was planning to rebrand data centers as “AI compute campuses” as part of a policy and PR push after Tuesday’s midterms, anticipating a more hostile regulatory environment if Democrats make gains. Apparently, the rebrand couldn’t wait.
Critics, however, aren’t buying it. A new name does nothing to lessen data centers’ environmental footprint, ease the burdens they place on local resources, or address the concerns of nearby residents. Activist groups who spoke to The Verge see the rebrand as a fairly transparent attempt to break free from an increasingly toxic label without meaningfully tackling what made it toxic to begin with. It fits within a broader lobbying push by Big Tech to counter souring public sentiment.
“OpenAI is clearly responding to the unpopularity of data centers,” said Holly Elmore, founder and executive director of PauseAI US, a grassroots group campaigning to pause AI development.
“Rebranding them ‘compute campuses’ may fool listeners once or twice but it’s not the name ‘data center’ that is unpopular — it’s the giant, polluting construction projects that apparently threatened to turn off people’s heat in the winter,” Elmore told The Verge in an email, calling OpenAI’s electricity announcement “woefully tone deaf.”
“Communities that despise them will not be fooled into thinking they have transformed into something else.”
Abre’ Conner, director of the NAACP’s Center for Environmental and Climate Justice, was similarly unimpressed. “This feels like an empty solution to an existing issue, which is that the data center infrastructure causes real harm,” she told The Verge. “Changing the name does not fix the problem and makes it seem like people’s health and our environment are public relations problems not environmental and climate concerns.”
Conner argued that OpenAI should focus on changing its practices rather than what it calls data centers. “At a time when companies could utilize their resources to develop new models, create more transparency, and slow the infrastructure development, their solution is to just change their name.”
Elmore agreed, suggesting the data center rebrand was an attempt by OpenAI to keep up with its rival Anthropic, but that it isn’t “spinning it nearly as well.” Earlier this year, Anthropic pledged to cover 100 percent of the upgrades needed to connect its data centers to power grids, including those that would normally be passed on to consumers. The company has also promised to invest billions of dollars in community projects developed with local leaders, protect local water supplies, and refrain from asking public officials to sign NDAs — a practice that has recently come under scrutiny. Anthropic said those commitments apply to both leased and owned data centers. Leased facilities currently make up most of its infrastructure, though its recent S-1 filing ahead of its IPO indicates the company wants to aggressively build its own, too, with many relying on gas.
OpenAI did not respond to The Verge’s request for comment about its decision to change nomenclature, its motivations for doing so, or whether doing so is an attempt to distance itself from the growing controversy surrounding data centers.

Handles are the better pointers (2018)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
14:54:30 UTC
23:54:30 KST

Handles are the better pointers
28-Nov-2018: I’ve added a small update at the end on how to prevent ‘handle collisions’ with a per-slot generation counter
Original Post:
…wherein I talk a bit about how I’m doing dynamic memory management in C and C++ these days which basically replaces raw- and smart-pointers with ‘index-handles’.
In my last blog post I was mentioning pointer- and allocation-free programming, but was skipping over the details. This is what the following blog post is about.
This is all based on the (sometimes painful) experience of wrestling for 15+ years with fairly big C++ code bases (0.5 to around 1 mloc) where memory is often managed through smart pointers. The worst case being tens- to hundreds-of-thousands of small C++ objects, each in its own heap allocation, pointing to each other through smart pointers. While such code is quite robust in terms of memory corruption (segfaults and corruption rarely happens, since most attempts are caught by asserts when dereferencing smart pointers), this type of ‘object spiderweb code’ is also dog-slow without obvious starting points for optimization, since the entire code is full of cache misses. Other typical problems are memory fragmentation and ‘fake memory leaks’ because a forgotten smart pointer prevents freeing the underlying memory (I call them ‘fake leaks’ because this type of leaks cannot be caught by memory debugging tools).
Nothing presented here is particularly new or clever, it’s just a collection of a few simple ideas which together work fairly well in bigger code bases to prevent (or at least detect early) a number of common memory-related problems in C and C++, and may even be useful in higher-level garbage-collected languages to reduce pressure on the garbage collector.
However, the underlying design philosophy doesn’t fit very well into a classical OOP world where applications are built from small autonomous objects interacting with each other. That’s why it’s also quite tricky to implement those ideas in a big existing OOP code base, where object creation and destruction happens ‘decentralized’ all over the code.
The approach described here works very well though with a data-oriented architecture, where central systems work on arrays of data items packed tightly in memory.
Most of the following blog post is written from a game developer’s perspective, but should also apply to other areas where a program needs to juggle a few hundred to a few million objects (or generally ‘data items’) in memory, and where such items are created and destroyed frequently.
The gist is:
- move all memory management into centralized systems (like rendering, physics, animation, …), with the systems being the sole owner of their memory allocations
- group items of the same type into arrays, and treat the array base pointer as system-private
- when creating an item, only return an ‘index-handle’ to the outside world, not a pointer to the item
- in the index-handles, only use as many bits as needed for the array index, and use the remaining bits for additional memory safety checks
- only convert a handle to a pointer when absolutely needed, and don’t store the pointer anywhere
I’ll explain each of those points in detail below. But the idea is basically that common ‘user-level’ code doesn’t directly call memory allocation functions (like malloc, new or make_shared/make_unique), and reduces the use of pointers to an absolute minimum (only as short-lived references when direct memory access to an item is absolutely needed). Most importantly, pointers are never the ‘owner’ of an item’s underlying memory.
Instead, direct memory manipulation happens as much as possible inside a few centralized systems where memory-related problems are easier to debug and optimize.
Move memory management into central systems
In this blog post, a ‘system’ is a (usually fairly big) part of a code base which takes care of a number of related tasks, like ‘rendering’, ‘physics’, ‘AI’, ‘character animation’ and so on. Such a system is separated from other systems and ‘user-code’ through a clearly defined function API, and the work that happens on the system’s data is performed in tight central loops instead of being spread out all over the code base.
Systems often work on items created and destroyed under control of user code (but note that creation and destruction of items is different from allocating and freeing the memory used by those items!). For instance a rendering system might deal with vertex buffers, textures, shaders and pipeline state objects. A physics system works with rigid bodies, joints and collision primitives, and an animation system works with animation keys and curves.
It makes sense to move the memory management for such items into the systems themselves, because a general memory allocator doesn’t have the system-specific ‘domain knowledge’ about how data items are processed and the relationships between data items. This allows the system to optimize memory allocations, perform additional validation checks when creating and destroying items, and arrange items in memory for making best use of the CPU’s data caches.
A good example for this ‘system domain knowledge’ is the destruction of rendering resource objects with modern 3D APIs: a resource object can not simply be destroyed immediately when the user code says so because the resource might still be referenced in a command list waiting to be consumed by the GPU. Instead the rendering system would only mark the resource object for destruction when the user code requests its destruction, but the actual destruction happens at a later time when the GPU no longer uses the resource.
Group items of the same type into arrays
Once all memory management has moved into systems, the system can optimize memory allocations and memory layout with its additional knowledge about how items are used. One obvious optimization is to reduce the number of general memory allocations by grouping items of the same type into arrays, and allocate those arrays at system startup.
Instead of performing a memory allocation each time a new item is created, the system keeps track of free array slots, and picks the next free slot. When the user code no longer needs the item, the system simply marks the slot as free again instead of performing a deallocation (not different from a typical pool allocator).
This pool allocation is most likely a little bit faster than performing a memory allocation per item, but this is not even the main reason for keeping items in arrays (modern general allocators are quite fast too for small allocations).
A few additional advantages are:
- items are guaranteed to be packed tightly in memory, general allocators sometimes need to keep some housekeeping data next to the actual item memory
- it’s easier to keep ‘hot items’ in continuous memory ranges, so that the CPU can make better use of its data caches
- it’s also possible to split the data of a single item into several subitems in different arrays for even tighter packing and better data cache usage (AoS vs SoA and everything inbetween), and all those data layout details remain private to the system and are trivial to change without affecting ‘outside code’
- as long as the system doesn’t need to reallocate arrays, it is guaranteed that there will be no memory fragmentation (although this is less of an issue in a 64-bit address space)
- it’s easier to detect memory leaks early, and provide more useful error messages: when a new item is created a system can trivially check the current number of items against an expected upper bound (for instance a game might know that there should never be more than 1024 textures alive at a time, and since all textures are created through the rendering system, the system can print out a more useful warning message when this number is exceeded)
Public index-handles instead of pointers
Keeping system items in arrays instead of unique allocations has the advantage that an item can be identified through an array index instead of requiring a full pointer. This is very useful for memory safety. Instead of handing memory pointers to the outside world, the system can treat the array base pointers as ‘private knowledge’, and only hand out array indices to the public. Without the base pointer to compute an item’s memory location, the outside code can’t access the item’s memory, even with a lot of criminal energy.
This has a number of further advantages:
- In many situations, code outside the system never even needs to directly access memory of an item, only the system does. In such an ‘ideal’ situation, user code never accesses memory through pointers, and can never cause memory corruption.
- Since only the system knows the array base pointers, it’s free to move or reallocate the item arrays at will without invalidating existing index handles.
- Array indices need fewer bits than full pointers, and a smaller data type can be picked for them, which in turn allows tighter packing of data structures and better data cache usage (this has the caveat that additional handle bits can be used to increase memory safety, more about this below)
If user code needs to access the memory of an item directly it needs to obtain a pointer through a ‘lookup function’ which takes a handle as input and returns a pointer. As soon as such a lookup function exists, the fairly watertight memory safety scenario outlined above is no longer guaranteed, and the user code should adhere to a few rules:
- pointers should never be stored anywhere, since the next time the pointer is used it may no longer point to the same item, or even to valid memory
- a pointer should only be used in a simple code block, and not ‘across’ function calls
Every time a handle is converted into a pointer, the system can guarantee that the returned pointer still points to the same item that the handle was originally created for (more on this below), but this guarantee ‘decays’ over time since the item under the pointer may have been destroyed, or the underlying memory may have been reallocated to a different location (this is the same problem as iterator invalidation in C++).
The two simple rules above are easy to memorize and are a good compromise between not exposing pointers to user code at all, and having a (somewhat costly) handle-to-pointer conversion for every single memory access.
Memory safety considerations
First, each type of handle should get its own C/C++ type, so that attempting to pass the wrong handle type to a function is already detected at compile time (note that a simple typedef isn’t enough to produce a compiler warning, the handle must be wrapped into its own struct or class - however this could be limited to debug compilation mode).
All runtime memory safety checks happen in the function which converts a handle into a pointer. If the handle is just an array index, it would look like this:
- a range check happens for the index against the current item array size, this prevents segmentation faults and reading or writing allocated but unrelated memory areas
- a check needs to happen whether the indexed array item slot contains an active item (is not currently a ‘free slot’), this prevents the simple variant of ‘use after free’
- finally the item pointer is computed from the private array base pointer and public item index
The resulting pointer is safe to use as long as:
- the item array isn’t reallocated
- the indexed item hasn’t been destroyed
Both can only happen inside one of the system’s functions, that’s why the two ‘pointer usage rules’ exist (don’t store pointers, don’t keep pointers across function calls).
There is a pretty big hole in the above use-after-free check though: if we only check if an array slot behind an index-handle contains a valid item, it’s not guaranteed that it is the same item the handle was originally created for. It can happen that the original item was destroyed, and the same array slot was reused for a new item.
This is where the ‘free bits’ in a handle come in: Let’s say our handles are 16-bits, but we only ever need 1024 items alive at the same time. Only 10 index bits are needed to address 1024 items, which leaves 6 bits free for something else.
If those 6 bits contain some sort of ‘unique pattern’, it’s possible to detect dangling accesses:
- When an item is created, a free array item is picked and its index is put into the lower 10 handle bits. The upper 6 bits are set to a ‘unique bit pattern’
- The resulting 16-bit handle (10 bits index + 6 bit ‘unique pattern’) are returned to the outside world, and at the same time stored with the array slot.
- When an item is destroyed, the item handle stored with the array slot is set to the ‘invalid handle’ value (can be zero, as long as zero is never returned as a valid handle to the outside world)
- When the handle is converted to a pointer, the lower 10 bits are used as array index to lookup the array slot, and the entire 16-bit handle is compared against the handle that’s currently stored with the array slot:
    
  - if both handles are equal, the pointer is valid and points to the same item the handle was created for
  - otherwise this is a dangling access, the slot item has either been destroyed (in that case the stored handle would have the ‘invalid handle’ value), or has been destroyed and reused for a new item (in that case the upper 6 ‘unique pattern’ bits don’t match)
This handle-comparison check when converting a handle to a pointer works quite well to detect dangling-accesses, but it isn’t waterproof because the same combination of array index and ‘unique pattern’ will be created sooner or later. But it’s still better than no dangling protection at all (like raw pointers), or a ‘fake memory leak’ which would happen in similar situations with smart pointers.
Finding good strategies to create unique handles that collide as rarely as possible is the most important part of course, and left as an excercise to the reader ;P
Obviously it’s good to use as many bits for the unique pattern as possible, and the way how free array slots are reused is important as well (e.g. LIFO vs FIFO). It’s probably also good to write a little creation/destruction stress-test which checks for handle collisions and can be used to tweak the unique-pattern creation for specific use cases. A system where items are created and destroyed with a very high frequency needs more effort (or simply more handle bits) than systems where item creation and destruction is a rare occurance.
Other useful properties of handles
Apart from the whole memory safety aspect, handles are also useful for other situations where pointers are problematic:
Handles can be used as shared object identifiers across processes (all you need is some sort of ‘create_item_with_handle()’ function, which doesn’t create a new handle, but takes an existing handle as input argument). This is especially useful for online games where handles can be shared between the server and all clients in a game session, or in savegame systems to store references to other objects.
Sometimes it’s useful to create a whole group of related items (for instance animation keys and curves), and reference the whole item group with a single handle. In that case some sort of ‘range handle’ can be used, which contains not only an index (of the first item), but also the number of items in the range.
In some situations it’s also useful to reserve a few handle bits for an item type if static type checking at compile time isn’t sufficient.
In conclusion, I find it quite surprising how naturally and elegantly handles solve many problems I encountered in the past with the traditional ‘pointers to objects on the heap’ model, and how little I miss this model now (and the parts of C++ that are built around it).
Some real-world examples
The sokol-gfx API is an example of a C-API which uses handles instead of pointer of rendering resource objects (buffers, images, shaders, …):
The Oryol Gfx module is a similar 3D API wrapper, but written in C++:
The Oryol Animation extension module is a character animation system which keeps all its data in arrays:
Update 28-Nov-2018
…in the post above I was sort of handwaving away the problem of creating the same unique-tag twice for the same slot, and a nice person on twitter hinted me about a very simple, elegant and embarrassingly ‘obvious’ solution:
Each array slot gets its own generation counter, which is bumped when a handle is released (can also happen when the handle is created, but bumping on release means you don’t need a reserved value for “free slots” to detect an invalid handle).
To check if a handle is valid, simply compare its unique-tag with the current generation counter in its slot.
Once the generation counter would ‘overflow’, disable that array slot, so that no new handles are returned for this slot.
This is a perfect solution for avoiding handle collisions, but the handles will eventually run out since all array slots will be disabled eventually. But since each slot has its own counter, this only happens after all handle-bits are exhausted, not just the few unique-tag bits.
So with 32-bit handles, you can always create 4 billion items, with at most 2^(32 - num_counter_bits) alive at the same time. This also means the number of bits for the unique-tag can be reduced without compromising ‘handle safety’.
It may also be possible to re-activate disabled slots once it can be guaranteed that no more handles for that slot are out in the wild (maybe at special places in the code like entering or exiting a level).

Mac at Any Scale
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
14:53:42 UTC
23:53:42 KST

Mac at any scale
Chances are you’ve seen me on X: unpacking hundreds of MacBook Pros to deploy in a datacenter. The video hit a nerve: insane enough at the first sight, yet kind of rational if you know what we know now.
Unboxing Macs at scale for our new server racks.
So today I want to give some background into what it takes to build custom Mac racks, how we can start macOS instances from scratch in seconds and deliver new Xcode versions mere hours after Apple releases them.
So why MacBooks?
Because our customers need Macs. And because Apple didn’t give us another option.
Many of our customers build and test iOS apps. You can't build iOS apps without a Mac. The iOS Simulator only runs on a Mac. Moreover, in order to release an app to the App Store, Apple requires using a recent Xcode version.xtoolxtool is cool, but it is not (yet) a serious replacement. So your coding agent needs a Mac.
But still, why laptops? Isn’t it a major waste of screen pixels and keyboard buttons and battery ions? Well yes, Mac Mini would be a better choice for a datacenter. Sure, it has very rudimentary remote management, no redundant power, and a beautiful case that ensures that cooling will be difficult regardless of which side you lay it on. But these are all known challenges that we have learned to manage.
But have you actually tried to order a couple hundred Minis? We have. Ordered in January, delivered in July. Welcome to the world of 2026 when the supply chains are in disarray. Never mind that Apple doesn’t release updated CPU models to all devices at once. So Mac Mini didn’t get M5 cores for 10 months (and then suddenly got M6 before any other device model!). We promise the best and the newest to our customers so we had to be inventive. That’s how the MacBook-in-Datacenter project was born.
Our beautiful Mac racks
Good design takes iterations; and we surely didn’t get everything right from the first try. But I think we have the system nailed down.
Every MacBook is connected to an IP KVM for remote management. Indispensable when dealing with laptops. Unlike Mac Mini, laptop firmware is designed with the screen in mind: some maintenance actions can only be done by clicking buttons in the beautiful GUI.
Of course, every MacBook gets an external 10G Ethernet NIC. Many folks at Namespace are networking geeks and we are proud of the connection speed and reliability that we provide to customers.
And then there is power. So in total, four cables per laptop, forty-eight per shelf, plus twelve for Ethernet, plus KVM wiring. Let’s just say that careful cable management is essential.
Heat management too. Currently we are deploying custom-made holders, designed to wick the heat from the hotspots and guide hot air away instead of blowing it at the nearby machines.
Not the problems we expected: HDMI, FileVault, and USB NICs
Naturally, we expected problems and challenges. Just not where they appeared.
I bet some of you looked at the pic above, saw MagSafe cables plugged in and already started writing a snarky tweet that MagSafe is not safe and may get accidentally disconnected.
And yes, I was worried about that too. But the reality is that we have seen very few cases of MagSafe disconnections.
The most unreliable cable? HDMI! Often thick and inflexible. Tends to fall out every time you touch the shelf.
Luckily by now we found almost perfect cables that bend exactly as we want them to bend. And we bought the entire supply of them. There are zero more of them available to order on Amazon.
Another source of pain: FileVault disk encryption. Or rather decryption. When a MacBook reboots (in an unplanned fashion) it prompts for a password to unlock the local disk. The prompt is shown on the screen (of a closed laptop somewhere in the rack, doh). No problem, we thought: we have the KVM connected just for this case!
Nope, not so easy. The password prompt disappears from the external screen after 5 minutes of inactivity. Then we remembered that macOS Tahoe introduced a useful feature: we could unlock FileVault remotely using SSH protocol. Still no luck. On laptops it expires after 5 minutes too. Looks like the only way out of this state is to reboot by physically pressing the button and racing to unlock in time.Mac rebootWhile on this topic, a small tip: you can reboot a Mac in a way that will keep the disk unlocked when you need it: fdesetup authrestart.
And then there are simply bugs. Under certain conditions, an external USB NIC stops working and acts as if it is not powered and shows no link. Trying another cable, another NIC, another switch port, no luck. But a quick visit to Network Preferences, deleting and recreating the “network service” in MacOS Settings brings it back to life.
What about Mac Minis and Studios?
At the moment we actually have it easy: M5 and M6 Mac Minis and Studios are coming into our fleet soon. They are way simpler to manage in the DC: they have a built-in 10 Gb network card, they boot up automatically after a power cycle and the OS can be configured entirely automatically via MDM.
But Namespace has some special sauce for the Minis too. We design custom-made shelves that hold the machines with focus on efficient cooling. We have tested a few designs using airflow simulation to arrive at what we are deploying now.
In the end installing Mac Minis is a breeze: unpack them, throw 10 pieces into the holder, connect power and network and press the power button once (famously inconveniently located). From there on automation takes care of them.
Virtualizing Macs
Under the hood a Namespace instance is backed by a virtual machine. We have multiple virtual machine engines integrated into a unified platform to run Linux, Windows, and Mac instances in the most efficient way. This way we can provide the same features that make Namespace special, like cache volumes, telemetry, or VPC connections.
Unsurprisingly, our Mac solution is based on Apple’s excellent Virtualization framework. It is an embeddable and flexible virtual machine engine. It’s easiest to compare it to Firecracker in how it fits the system architecture: it implements generic VM operations end-to-end as well as typical peripherals.
For completeness, Apple also provides a lower-level Hypervisor framework, which is comparable to the KVM API of Linux. For instance QEMU uses it on macOS under the hood. Hypervisor provides a way to execute machine code in isolation, but requires the application developer to implement the bulk of machine architecture and peripherals. It could give us a lot of control, but unfortunately it cannot run macOS (it requires an Apple proprietary environment that third parties cannot, or shouldn’t, try to recreate).
In fact, most competing platforms are built on the same foundation. But the details matter: Apple gives us a few primitives that need to be arranged in just the right way to work fast and be secure. The more interesting thing is to watch how different people solve similar problems with different constraints.
Limitations of Apple Virtualization
A well-known limitation is that Apple only allows two macOS VMs per physical Mac. It can be a Mac Pro with 24 cores and 192 GB memory, yet it is limited to running only two guest VMs. This limitation leaks into our product offering: we can only slice Mac resources in two halves. So we run instances with 6 or 12 cores on 14-core Mac Minis and 8/16-core instances on 18-core MacBooks (we reserve a couple cores for management overhead).
An important consideration of Virtualization VMs is that the virtual CPU is executed by a separate OS process (VMM process in the diagram) from the software integrating Virtualization (here Namespace VM host). If we want to hook some VM behavior or implement custom virtual devices, every interaction with them requires an IPC roundtrip between the VM process and the “host” process. Such context switches are unavoidable and need to be taken into account when designing the system.
Virtual networking
Virtualization framework offers a few ways to connect a virtual machine to the network. For instance, bridged and NAT networking modes route traffic directly from the VMM process to the host network interfaces. These are suitable for VMs running on a local computer where the workload is trusted.
Namespace instances run untrusted code and we need to enforce policies on what a VM can or cannot access (these policies can also be customer-defined!). Also we provide a few additional services to the instances that should appear “as if” they were on the local network for the guest system.
This requires us to take full control of guest networking in the mode where we process every packet that comes out and into the VM in software. We based our guest networking on a userspace TCP/IP stack from Google gVisor project and use it to implement an IP gateway/firewall for the instances. This way we minimize exposure of the host OS to unprocessed packets controlled by the guest.
Network packet handling needs to be very efficient, but as outlined above the path involves IPC. The VMM process needs first to pass the packets to the “VM host” process, which then processes and passes them to the network interface. It takes careful implementation and batch processing wherever possible to minimize the number of IPC roundtrips.
The above approach allows Namespace MacOS instances to reach 4 Gbps upstream bandwidth (per Fast.com). This is unfortunately still lower than the line rate. We have been exploring alternative approaches, including ideas on how to avoid IPC for network processing entirely. But productionizing the new setup is still left for the future. After all, 4 Gbps is still quite respectable.
VM images
Creating your first VM using Apple Virtualization is very easy: download a macOS installer XIP, use UTM/Tart/any of the dozens of open-source Virtualization wrappers to start a machine, click through Setup Assistant, install Xcode and you are good to go. Now you have a VM image that you can fork and run any workload locally.
But you need to test on multiple versions of iOS, so you install a few iOS runtimes. And then you install the upcoming Xcode beta version (and new runtimes too). And before you know it your VM image has grown to 500 GB (iOS runtime packages are huge).
You also want to have the previous version of macOS for testing that one little thing. Another 500 GB right here. Apple releases the new macOS developer preview, one more image.
And then you realize that the Xcode beta version subtly breaks the non-beta Xcodes on the same system (true story!). After all, Xcode installation bumps the “global” versions of CoreDevice and CoreSimulator. Hence any bugs in the beta Xcode simulator are visible when you’re using ostensibly stable Xcode versions. So now you are maintaining image versions with and without the pre-release Xcodes.
And now you have 2 TB of VM images. On what? A 2 TB MacBook hard drive?
That’s the challenge we faced: we need to build, distribute, and store huge images across the fleet of hundreds of machines.
Build process
Building images is a repeatable process so that we can reliably produce new images when Apple releases new software versions. Building is driven by a script that replicates installation and configuration as a human user would do it (e.g. the build script can run shell commands, click UI buttons or reboot the VM). Wherever possible we stick to using controls designed by Apple to configure the VMs in order to avoid surprising behaviours inconsistent with what a typical local user would be seeing. Naturally, building images is an automated and repeatable process. It is driven by a workflow expressed as a sequence of operations in a YAML file. Operations can be VM control (e.g. reboot), running a shell script or programmatic interaction with the system or setup UI over VNC.
Resulting VM images are handed over to Namespace internal Image Registry, in fact the same one that handles Linux and Windows image storage, replication and distribution (covered in the excellent article by Matteo). Disk images are of course quite different from normal container images: OCI layers are simple tarballs of files, while disks are generally flat arrays of bytes. Very large arrays in fact. And to a large extent they contain empty space: that’s the disk space that would be available to users as free space.
Naturally, it would be very wasteful to transfer and store the “empty” data in the registry. So we developed a simple format that allows us to preserve the “sparse-ness” of the images when stored in the registry. Unallocated spans of the image take just a few bytes.
And there is another important optimization. Remember the example scenario mentioned in the VM Images section: we have an image of the same system that differs only in addition of an extra Xcode installation. Obviously the latter image is built using the former as a base and most of the bytes are unchanged.
Our storage format preserves the “shallow copy” relationship and stores such images as multiple layers: the base layer is shared between them, while subsequent layers only contain the modified blocks.
Distribution
At this point we have the images in an OCI registry now. But of course Virtualization framework cannot start a VM directly using a disk residing in the registry. It needs to have something on the node itself: a VM disk can be backed by either an image file on the local host SSD, or it can route disk I/O requests to a server using Network Block Device (NBD) protocol. The latter would allow us to intelligently serve highly demanded data from a local cache, while conserving on-device space by not storing unneeded blocks.
But NBD has the same drawback as userspace networking from above: every I/O operation would incur an IPC roundtrip at minimum, including buffer copying and context switches. You see the same pattern as with networking: a very direct hardwired and fast option and an indirect “software-defined” but disappointingly slow option.
At Namespace we value performance a lot, so the choice is clear: we’ll serve the images from the local disk. We need to materialize them from OCI images in the registry on the SSD of every node in the fleet. 500 gig images to hundreds and thousands of nodes. And we need to do it fast.
Obviously, every node naively downloading from the central registry was not going to work. With the example numbers we are talking about half a petabyte of traffic transferred within, conservatively, tens of minutes. The registry (and the network fabric) would need to serve terabits per second!
The bytes that we are distributing are the same going to every node so the obvious solution is to organize a peer-to-peer delivery of these blobs. Each block only needs to be downloaded from the registry once by some node. Then another node that is close by (by certain metric) can download the block directly from the peer and we have two copies. So two more nodes can pull these bytes and so on. The fleet is exponentially flooded.BitTorrentObviously, BitTorrent was a heavy inspiration for this design. But unlike BitTorrent, which works in a public network of untrusted peers, we could make different tradeoffs leading to better efficiency and predictability. But that’s a topic for another post.
Storage
Now the bytes are on the node disk. But remember the storage optimizations we did in the Build Process? With the naive approach of storing the images on disk there are a lot of bytes that are useless: they are either holes or could be shared with another image.
Some of you probably know that these are exactly the types of storage optimization techniques that are possible with modern file systems, namely APFS.ZFSLinux users have seen the same features in ZFS.
Using these cool features is also pretty easy: open a new file for writing, seek to offset 100G, write a block. And there you just made a file with a large hole in it. Only the block you wrote is allocated on disk, the rest is 100 GB of imaginary zeroes. When reading the file you won’t even notice the holes. The OS will return actual zeros.
% truncate -s 1g hole.bin
% dd if=/dev/random of=hole.bin bs=4k seek=256k count=256 
% ls -lh hole.bin
-rw-r--r--  1 kirill  staff   1.0G Sep 29 05:36 hole.bin
% du -h hole.bin
 1M	hole.bin
Sharing the blocks is similarly transparent: just use the cp -c mode or clonefile syscall. The new file will share the same disk blocks and essentially not take any space.
Writing to the cloned file is also transparent: as soon as you try to overwrite some blocks, these particular blocks will be allocated on the disk, while the shared portion will remain shared.
What’s next?
The pace at which we are expanding our fleet is increasing and the experience from all the above has transformed into a repeatable process. We know exactly how long it takes to bring a MacBook rack into prod. The rack assembly line is dialed in. DCOps people are trained. Disk images are built and distributed. We can bring whatever number of machines our customers need into the fleet when we need to (well, as long as we can purchase them at all).
We can still do better! What if there was a “holy-grail” device to host a shelf of MacBooks, providing power, ethernet and KVM connections over a single USB-C cable? It should be possible, yet there is currently no hardware on the open market that satisfies our requirements. So we are working on something very custom.
Or maybe we’ll do another crazy thing? What if we deploy phones to the datacenter? See what the future brings on our X soon!
