Mac at Any Scale
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
14:53:42 UTC
23:53:42 KST

Mac at any scale
Chances are you’ve seen me on X: unpacking hundreds of MacBook Pros to deploy in a datacenter. The video hit a nerve: insane enough at the first sight, yet kind of rational if you know what we know now.
Unboxing Macs at scale for our new server racks.
So today I want to give some background into what it takes to build custom Mac racks, how we can start macOS instances from scratch in seconds and deliver new Xcode versions mere hours after Apple releases them.
So why MacBooks?
Because our customers need Macs. And because Apple didn’t give us another option.
Many of our customers build and test iOS apps. You can't build iOS apps without a Mac. The iOS Simulator only runs on a Mac. Moreover, in order to release an app to the App Store, Apple requires using a recent Xcode version.xtoolxtool is cool, but it is not (yet) a serious replacement. So your coding agent needs a Mac.
But still, why laptops? Isn’t it a major waste of screen pixels and keyboard buttons and battery ions? Well yes, Mac Mini would be a better choice for a datacenter. Sure, it has very rudimentary remote management, no redundant power, and a beautiful case that ensures that cooling will be difficult regardless of which side you lay it on. But these are all known challenges that we have learned to manage.
But have you actually tried to order a couple hundred Minis? We have. Ordered in January, delivered in July. Welcome to the world of 2026 when the supply chains are in disarray. Never mind that Apple doesn’t release updated CPU models to all devices at once. So Mac Mini didn’t get M5 cores for 10 months (and then suddenly got M6 before any other device model!). We promise the best and the newest to our customers so we had to be inventive. That’s how the MacBook-in-Datacenter project was born.
Our beautiful Mac racks
Good design takes iterations; and we surely didn’t get everything right from the first try. But I think we have the system nailed down.
Every MacBook is connected to an IP KVM for remote management. Indispensable when dealing with laptops. Unlike Mac Mini, laptop firmware is designed with the screen in mind: some maintenance actions can only be done by clicking buttons in the beautiful GUI.
Of course, every MacBook gets an external 10G Ethernet NIC. Many folks at Namespace are networking geeks and we are proud of the connection speed and reliability that we provide to customers.
And then there is power. So in total, four cables per laptop, forty-eight per shelf, plus twelve for Ethernet, plus KVM wiring. Let’s just say that careful cable management is essential.
Heat management too. Currently we are deploying custom-made holders, designed to wick the heat from the hotspots and guide hot air away instead of blowing it at the nearby machines.
Not the problems we expected: HDMI, FileVault, and USB NICs
Naturally, we expected problems and challenges. Just not where they appeared.
I bet some of you looked at the pic above, saw MagSafe cables plugged in and already started writing a snarky tweet that MagSafe is not safe and may get accidentally disconnected.
And yes, I was worried about that too. But the reality is that we have seen very few cases of MagSafe disconnections.
The most unreliable cable? HDMI! Often thick and inflexible. Tends to fall out every time you touch the shelf.
Luckily by now we found almost perfect cables that bend exactly as we want them to bend. And we bought the entire supply of them. There are zero more of them available to order on Amazon.
Another source of pain: FileVault disk encryption. Or rather decryption. When a MacBook reboots (in an unplanned fashion) it prompts for a password to unlock the local disk. The prompt is shown on the screen (of a closed laptop somewhere in the rack, doh). No problem, we thought: we have the KVM connected just for this case!
Nope, not so easy. The password prompt disappears from the external screen after 5 minutes of inactivity. Then we remembered that macOS Tahoe introduced a useful feature: we could unlock FileVault remotely using SSH protocol. Still no luck. On laptops it expires after 5 minutes too. Looks like the only way out of this state is to reboot by physically pressing the button and racing to unlock in time.Mac rebootWhile on this topic, a small tip: you can reboot a Mac in a way that will keep the disk unlocked when you need it: fdesetup authrestart.
And then there are simply bugs. Under certain conditions, an external USB NIC stops working and acts as if it is not powered and shows no link. Trying another cable, another NIC, another switch port, no luck. But a quick visit to Network Preferences, deleting and recreating the “network service” in MacOS Settings brings it back to life.
What about Mac Minis and Studios?
At the moment we actually have it easy: M5 and M6 Mac Minis and Studios are coming into our fleet soon. They are way simpler to manage in the DC: they have a built-in 10 Gb network card, they boot up automatically after a power cycle and the OS can be configured entirely automatically via MDM.
But Namespace has some special sauce for the Minis too. We design custom-made shelves that hold the machines with focus on efficient cooling. We have tested a few designs using airflow simulation to arrive at what we are deploying now.
In the end installing Mac Minis is a breeze: unpack them, throw 10 pieces into the holder, connect power and network and press the power button once (famously inconveniently located). From there on automation takes care of them.
Virtualizing Macs
Under the hood a Namespace instance is backed by a virtual machine. We have multiple virtual machine engines integrated into a unified platform to run Linux, Windows, and Mac instances in the most efficient way. This way we can provide the same features that make Namespace special, like cache volumes, telemetry, or VPC connections.
Unsurprisingly, our Mac solution is based on Apple’s excellent Virtualization framework. It is an embeddable and flexible virtual machine engine. It’s easiest to compare it to Firecracker in how it fits the system architecture: it implements generic VM operations end-to-end as well as typical peripherals.
For completeness, Apple also provides a lower-level Hypervisor framework, which is comparable to the KVM API of Linux. For instance QEMU uses it on macOS under the hood. Hypervisor provides a way to execute machine code in isolation, but requires the application developer to implement the bulk of machine architecture and peripherals. It could give us a lot of control, but unfortunately it cannot run macOS (it requires an Apple proprietary environment that third parties cannot, or shouldn’t, try to recreate).
In fact, most competing platforms are built on the same foundation. But the details matter: Apple gives us a few primitives that need to be arranged in just the right way to work fast and be secure. The more interesting thing is to watch how different people solve similar problems with different constraints.
Limitations of Apple Virtualization
A well-known limitation is that Apple only allows two macOS VMs per physical Mac. It can be a Mac Pro with 24 cores and 192 GB memory, yet it is limited to running only two guest VMs. This limitation leaks into our product offering: we can only slice Mac resources in two halves. So we run instances with 6 or 12 cores on 14-core Mac Minis and 8/16-core instances on 18-core MacBooks (we reserve a couple cores for management overhead).
An important consideration of Virtualization VMs is that the virtual CPU is executed by a separate OS process (VMM process in the diagram) from the software integrating Virtualization (here Namespace VM host). If we want to hook some VM behavior or implement custom virtual devices, every interaction with them requires an IPC roundtrip between the VM process and the “host” process. Such context switches are unavoidable and need to be taken into account when designing the system.
Virtual networking
Virtualization framework offers a few ways to connect a virtual machine to the network. For instance, bridged and NAT networking modes route traffic directly from the VMM process to the host network interfaces. These are suitable for VMs running on a local computer where the workload is trusted.
Namespace instances run untrusted code and we need to enforce policies on what a VM can or cannot access (these policies can also be customer-defined!). Also we provide a few additional services to the instances that should appear “as if” they were on the local network for the guest system.
This requires us to take full control of guest networking in the mode where we process every packet that comes out and into the VM in software. We based our guest networking on a userspace TCP/IP stack from Google gVisor project and use it to implement an IP gateway/firewall for the instances. This way we minimize exposure of the host OS to unprocessed packets controlled by the guest.
Network packet handling needs to be very efficient, but as outlined above the path involves IPC. The VMM process needs first to pass the packets to the “VM host” process, which then processes and passes them to the network interface. It takes careful implementation and batch processing wherever possible to minimize the number of IPC roundtrips.
The above approach allows Namespace MacOS instances to reach 4 Gbps upstream bandwidth (per Fast.com). This is unfortunately still lower than the line rate. We have been exploring alternative approaches, including ideas on how to avoid IPC for network processing entirely. But productionizing the new setup is still left for the future. After all, 4 Gbps is still quite respectable.
VM images
Creating your first VM using Apple Virtualization is very easy: download a macOS installer XIP, use UTM/Tart/any of the dozens of open-source Virtualization wrappers to start a machine, click through Setup Assistant, install Xcode and you are good to go. Now you have a VM image that you can fork and run any workload locally.
But you need to test on multiple versions of iOS, so you install a few iOS runtimes. And then you install the upcoming Xcode beta version (and new runtimes too). And before you know it your VM image has grown to 500 GB (iOS runtime packages are huge).
You also want to have the previous version of macOS for testing that one little thing. Another 500 GB right here. Apple releases the new macOS developer preview, one more image.
And then you realize that the Xcode beta version subtly breaks the non-beta Xcodes on the same system (true story!). After all, Xcode installation bumps the “global” versions of CoreDevice and CoreSimulator. Hence any bugs in the beta Xcode simulator are visible when you’re using ostensibly stable Xcode versions. So now you are maintaining image versions with and without the pre-release Xcodes.
And now you have 2 TB of VM images. On what? A 2 TB MacBook hard drive?
That’s the challenge we faced: we need to build, distribute, and store huge images across the fleet of hundreds of machines.
Build process
Building images is a repeatable process so that we can reliably produce new images when Apple releases new software versions. Building is driven by a script that replicates installation and configuration as a human user would do it (e.g. the build script can run shell commands, click UI buttons or reboot the VM). Wherever possible we stick to using controls designed by Apple to configure the VMs in order to avoid surprising behaviours inconsistent with what a typical local user would be seeing. Naturally, building images is an automated and repeatable process. It is driven by a workflow expressed as a sequence of operations in a YAML file. Operations can be VM control (e.g. reboot), running a shell script or programmatic interaction with the system or setup UI over VNC.
Resulting VM images are handed over to Namespace internal Image Registry, in fact the same one that handles Linux and Windows image storage, replication and distribution (covered in the excellent article by Matteo). Disk images are of course quite different from normal container images: OCI layers are simple tarballs of files, while disks are generally flat arrays of bytes. Very large arrays in fact. And to a large extent they contain empty space: that’s the disk space that would be available to users as free space.
Naturally, it would be very wasteful to transfer and store the “empty” data in the registry. So we developed a simple format that allows us to preserve the “sparse-ness” of the images when stored in the registry. Unallocated spans of the image take just a few bytes.
And there is another important optimization. Remember the example scenario mentioned in the VM Images section: we have an image of the same system that differs only in addition of an extra Xcode installation. Obviously the latter image is built using the former as a base and most of the bytes are unchanged.
Our storage format preserves the “shallow copy” relationship and stores such images as multiple layers: the base layer is shared between them, while subsequent layers only contain the modified blocks.
Distribution
At this point we have the images in an OCI registry now. But of course Virtualization framework cannot start a VM directly using a disk residing in the registry. It needs to have something on the node itself: a VM disk can be backed by either an image file on the local host SSD, or it can route disk I/O requests to a server using Network Block Device (NBD) protocol. The latter would allow us to intelligently serve highly demanded data from a local cache, while conserving on-device space by not storing unneeded blocks.
But NBD has the same drawback as userspace networking from above: every I/O operation would incur an IPC roundtrip at minimum, including buffer copying and context switches. You see the same pattern as with networking: a very direct hardwired and fast option and an indirect “software-defined” but disappointingly slow option.
At Namespace we value performance a lot, so the choice is clear: we’ll serve the images from the local disk. We need to materialize them from OCI images in the registry on the SSD of every node in the fleet. 500 gig images to hundreds and thousands of nodes. And we need to do it fast.
Obviously, every node naively downloading from the central registry was not going to work. With the example numbers we are talking about half a petabyte of traffic transferred within, conservatively, tens of minutes. The registry (and the network fabric) would need to serve terabits per second!
The bytes that we are distributing are the same going to every node so the obvious solution is to organize a peer-to-peer delivery of these blobs. Each block only needs to be downloaded from the registry once by some node. Then another node that is close by (by certain metric) can download the block directly from the peer and we have two copies. So two more nodes can pull these bytes and so on. The fleet is exponentially flooded.BitTorrentObviously, BitTorrent was a heavy inspiration for this design. But unlike BitTorrent, which works in a public network of untrusted peers, we could make different tradeoffs leading to better efficiency and predictability. But that’s a topic for another post.
Storage
Now the bytes are on the node disk. But remember the storage optimizations we did in the Build Process? With the naive approach of storing the images on disk there are a lot of bytes that are useless: they are either holes or could be shared with another image.
Some of you probably know that these are exactly the types of storage optimization techniques that are possible with modern file systems, namely APFS.ZFSLinux users have seen the same features in ZFS.
Using these cool features is also pretty easy: open a new file for writing, seek to offset 100G, write a block. And there you just made a file with a large hole in it. Only the block you wrote is allocated on disk, the rest is 100 GB of imaginary zeroes. When reading the file you won’t even notice the holes. The OS will return actual zeros.
% truncate -s 1g hole.bin
% dd if=/dev/random of=hole.bin bs=4k seek=256k count=256 
% ls -lh hole.bin
-rw-r--r--  1 kirill  staff   1.0G Sep 29 05:36 hole.bin
% du -h hole.bin
 1M	hole.bin
Sharing the blocks is similarly transparent: just use the cp -c mode or clonefile syscall. The new file will share the same disk blocks and essentially not take any space.
Writing to the cloned file is also transparent: as soon as you try to overwrite some blocks, these particular blocks will be allocated on the disk, while the shared portion will remain shared.
What’s next?
The pace at which we are expanding our fleet is increasing and the experience from all the above has transformed into a repeatable process. We know exactly how long it takes to bring a MacBook rack into prod. The rack assembly line is dialed in. DCOps people are trained. Disk images are built and distributed. We can bring whatever number of machines our customers need into the fleet when we need to (well, as long as we can purchase them at all).
We can still do better! What if there was a “holy-grail” device to host a shelf of MacBooks, providing power, ethernet and KVM connections over a single USB-C cable? It should be possible, yet there is currently no hardware on the open market that satisfies our requirements. So we are working on something very custom.
Or maybe we’ll do another crazy thing? What if we deploy phones to the datacenter? See what the future brings on our X soon!

My company is pretty paranoid about leaking IP to the frontier labs through prompts, so we've all been diligent about disabling data sharing in Codex/Claude Code/etc. But yesterday my coworkers and I noticed that the setting had been silently reenabled in Codex. None of us knowingly opted back in.

Curious if this is an isolated issue or something more widespread.

Windows 11 is now ‘up to 40 percent faster’ on PCs with 8GB of RAM
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
14:43:41 UTC
23:43:41 KST

Microsoft has been doing a lot of work to improve memory usage and increase performance on Windows 11 PCs over the past year. Last month the company revealed Windows 11 is now “more responsive” and runs better on PCs with 8GB of RAM, and now the company is releasing a new update today that includes further memory improvements.
Windows 11 is now ‘up to 40 percent faster’ on PCs with 8GB of RAM
A new Windows 11 update is improving memory usage and performance.
“On 8 GB PCs, Windows is now up to 20 percent more responsive, and multitasking is up to 40 percent faster in our lab test,” says Windows chief Pavan Davuluri. One of the key ways Microsoft has achieved better performance on PCs with 8GB of RAM is through compression.
“As a result, Windows can now keep up to 2 GB more of your active data in memory instead of moving it to slower storage, so an app or tab you left open is less likely to reload when you return,” says Davuluri. This compression helps Windows avoid writing data to a virtual memory paging file on a disk, slowing down apps and performance.
Microsoft is also making improvements across Windows, Edge, and Microsoft 365 apps that reduce “committed-memory growth by up to 900 MB.” Memory improvements in Word, PowerPoint, Excel, and Outlook alone add up to 360MB of RAM savings. Windows 11 no longer loads widgets on a PC with 8GB of RAM until you open the widgets panel, saving around 200MB of RAM. Inactive tabs in Edge now sleep after 15 minutes, reducing memory consumption further.
While Microsoft is shipping an update to Windows 11 with these memory improvements, the company is still working on improving RAM usage in Windows in the coming months. Memory compression will get “smarter and more efficient” over the next few months, and Windows features and apps will share more common parts like widgets and search sharing a single web engine. Microsoft is also working with PC makers and chipmakers to optimize how much memory a PC needs to reserve for its hardware, enabling more for Windows.

The TechCrunch Founder Summit agenda is only half the reason to be in Boston on Nov. 4
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
14:30:00 UTC
23:30:00 KST

You can learn a lot from a great founder session. But sometimes the conversation that changes what you do next happens five minutes after it ends. And with the event happening tomorrow, there is another reason to make sure you are in the room: the format is designed to help founders learn in different ways throughout the day.
On November 4, TechCrunch Founder Summit 2026 brings founders and investors together at SoWa Power Station in Boston for one day of practical learning, candid conversations, live pitching, smaller roundtables, and networking.
Register now to save up to $100 on your pass, plus get a second for free. Or save up to 30% on group bundles.
All-day founder bootcamp
Start with the Build Stage: practical lessons for building what comes next
The Build Stage focuses on the decisions founders make while they are still shaping, testing, and growing the business. These sessions get into the mechanics behind building a company, from distribution and fundraising to team building and pitching.
Hunter Worland of NEA opens the conversation on why distribution can become a startup’s next competitive advantage, especially when strong technology alone is not enough to win customers.
Kristen Craft of Fidelity Private Shares brings a current view of fundraising trends, helping founders understand how the capital environment is shifting and what that means for the way they approach their next raise.
The stage also gets tactical about building high-performing teams and sharpening the story you tell investors, customers, and potential hires.
Dave Balter of True Ventures and Edisyl takes on a deceptively simple question: Does your pitch earn the next conversation?
The value of the Build Stage is straightforward: these are lessons founders can take back to their companies immediately.
Use the Impact Stage to think about what happens after something starts working
If the Build Stage is about creating momentum, the Impact Stage is about what happens once that momentum becomes real. This is where founders can step back and think about bigger decisions around product-market fit, scaling, enterprise relationships, leadership, and long-term growth.
Steve Fredette, president, co-founder, and director of Toast, brings the perspective of building and scaling one of Boston’s most recognizable startup success stories.
Rudina Seseri, founder and managing partner of Glasswing Ventures, explores the tension every founder eventually faces: when to scale and when hitting the brakes may actually protect the business.
Kent Bennett of Bessemer Venture Partners tackles product-market fit before scale, helping founders think more critically about whether the signals they are seeing truly justify the next phase of growth.
Chase Garbarino, CEO and co-founder of HqO, brings the conversation home with the Boston Founder Playbook and a perspective on building ambitious companies outside the Bay Area.
The Impact Stage is valuable because it creates space for founders to get out of the weeds for a moment and ask whether they are scaling the right things, in the right way, at the right time.
Go deeper in the roundtables
Main-stage sessions can give you perspective. Roundtables give you room to get specific.
Founder Summit’s smaller roundtables are built around highly focused problems that many founders are actively solving right now, from entering difficult verticals and building enterprise trust to creating lean teams and opening doors with major customers.
Chirag Shah of Define Ventures digs into what happens when your first vertical is also one of the hardest to enter.
A technical session with Shishir Tewari of Procore Technologies moves the AI conversation from broad hype to the practical challenge of reducing hallucinations with better enterprise context.
Merve Kitapci of ADIN.AI gets highly specific about enterprise selling and what it takes to land major global customers before Series A.
Andy Tang of Draper Associates opens a different kind of founder conversation: what if you are building something the market does not even have a name for yet?
Linden Tibbets of IFTTT explores how AI may change the assumptions founders make about team size, productivity, and what a smaller company can realistically accomplish.
The value here is participation. Instead of simply hearing how someone else approached a problem, founders can bring their own questions, compare experiences with the people around the table, and leave with ideas that are much closer to their actual business.
Watch the startup pitch advice get tested live
Founder Summit also gives attendees the chance to see what happens when founders put their stories in front of investors in real time.
During So You Think You Can Pitch?, founders make their cases in front of investors, including Dave Balter, Chirag Shah, and Simran Suri of Maveron.
This is one of the most interactive parts of the day because it turns advice about pitching into something founders can actually watch: what gets an investor’s attention, where a story loses momentum, and which questions come up immediately.
For anyone preparing to raise, sell, recruit, or simply explain their company more clearly, seeing those reactions live can be incredibly useful.
Spark meaningful connections with Braindate networking
The Networking Lounge powered by Braindate gives attendees another way to connect intentionally throughout the day.
Schedule a one-on-one conversation. Join a small group. Find someone who wants to talk about the exact challenge you are working through.
Hear a session on product-market fit, then find another founder working through the same issue. Watch the pitch competition, then compare notes with someone preparing for their own fundraise. Hear the Boston Founder Playbook, then meet other founders doing exactly that. Braindate helps turn the content into conversation.
Keep it going at the closing reception
The day wraps up with a closing reception presented by Fidelity Private Shares, giving attendees time to keep the conversations going without rushing to the next session.
That matters because some of the most useful connections at an event happen after you have already shared a room, a question, or a conversation earlier in the day.
One day, several ways to learn
Founder Summit is not built around one type of conference experience. The Build Stage gives founders practical lessons they can apply now. The Impact Stage creates room for bigger growth and leadership decisions. The roundtables let you ask questions and go deeper. The live pitch competition puts founder storytelling to the test. Braindate makes networking intentional. And the reception gives those conversations somewhere to continue. That mix is what makes the day valuable.
Bring a partner and cover more ground
TechCrunch Founder Summit 2026 takes place November 4 at SoWa Power Station in Boston. There is still time to join us in Boston. Current Founder Summit passes include breakout sessions, roundtables, Braindate networking, the Expo Hall, and access to session materials after the event.
Even better, buy one pass now, and you can get a second pass free. Bring your co-founder. Bring a partner. Bring the person you will be texting session takeaways to all day anyway. Get your pass, bring someone with you for free, and join the founder community in Boston.

A truck-sized NASA space telescope is about to crash to Earth
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
14:17:55 UTC
23:17:55 KST

Almost 22 years after it was launched, NASA’s Swift Observatory is now about to fall back to Earth on Wednesday after attempts to push it back into a higher orbit failed. NASA announced that the aging space telescope is anticipated to re-enter Earth’s atmosphere at approximately 3:30AM ET (give or take 11 hours) and that it expects most of the spacecraft to burn up, but some components will likely survive re-entry.
A truck-sized NASA space telescope is about to crash to Earth
The Swift Observatory is expected to re-enter Earth’s atmosphere on Wednesday and is ‘unlikely’ to cause harm.
The observatory weighs around 3,200 pounds and measures in at 5.6 meters (18.4 feet) high and 5.4 meters (17.7 feet) wide, though much of the latter includes the observatory’s deployable solar arrays. The risk of surviving components causing any harm is low, according to NASA:
Re-entry locations of any surviving components can vary from hemisphere to hemisphere and depend on when it re-enters. Due to Swift’s low inclination, re-entry locations of surviving fragments are limited between 20.6 degrees south latitude and 20.6 degrees north latitude. The risk of harm coming to anyone on Earth is unlikely — approximately 1 in 2,100. NASA and Space Force will continue to monitor the re-entry and update predictions.
The agency gave up on trying to save the falling telescope earlier this year, after the Link spacecraft that was attempting the rescue experienced technical issues with attitude control. Still, Swift far outlived its original two-year prime mission to study the origin of gamma-ray bursts, and went on to serve as a general-purpose multiwavelength observatory until solar storms pushed it into a lower orbit. You can follow Swift’s descent by monitoring NASA’s blog for continued updates.

Just a little AI resistance, as a treat
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
14:14:12 UTC
23:14:12 KST

At the recent EMFCamp, I bumped in to Professor Kate Devlin who was talking a little about the AI Resist List. I think one of the useful things we can do is loudly complain about shitty uses of AI. Here's an example.
As mentioned in a previous post, I'm contacting companies which send malformed calendar invites. One of the organisation I contacted was the British online supermarket Ocado. They don't have an easy way to contact their technical team, and no obvious paths to raise a bug report, so I filled in their usual customer service feedback form. I included a clear statement of the problem and a detailed technical analysis of why I thought it was a problem with their service.
After sending in my message, I received this back from "Lulu" at Ocado:
						
Thanks for getting in touch and sharing this feedback about our calendar files.
You're entirely right—having a technical glitch pop up when you're trying to organise your delivery is a real pain, so I've made sure to pass your feedback on to our technical and webshop team.
We really appreciate you taking the time to help us improve our service. I hope you have a wonderful rest of your day
I mean, that's AI right? It starts with the classic tell of a sycophantic agreement.
Next up is the em-dash. Beloved by chatbots but almost unheard of in British English style guides.
Finally (and this is hard to quantify) it just doesn't feel like a customer service response. They're usually poorly spelled, have dodgy grammar, and ask a bunch of irrelevant questions. The message gave me no confidence that anyone had looked at the problem and I doubted it had been passed on to a technically competent human.
I immediately got a customer satisfaction survey. Usually I give top marks to humans because, even though Net Promoter Score is bullshit, I don't want customer service agents to suffer the indignity of a bad review. But in this survey I vented my spleen - giving crappy 1 ⭐ scores on every metric and complaining vociferously about how rude it is to use an AI to deal with a genuine complaint.
I was both surprised and delighted to receive this back a few days after my invective was sent:
						
I saw your survey comment about getting a robotic, AI-generated response previously, and I wanted to reach out personally to apologise. There is nothing more frustrating than pointing out a legitimate bug only to feel like you're talking to a brick wall. I promise you there is a real person typing this out!
Second, I wanted to send a massive thank you for the incredibly detailed diagnostic. It is rare to get feedback that tells us exactly what is broken and how to fix it.
I can assure you that this has been passed to our team to look into. Your feedback is going to make things much smoother for a lot of people, and we really appreciate you taking the extra time to help us get it right.
Thank you again, I do hope that this issue is resolved quickly. If there is anything else I can help with in the meantime, please don't hesitate to get in touch.
Perhaps I'm being touchingly naïve, but that feels a lot more human, right? The grammar isn't perfect, the sentences are a bit more rambly, and it appears to have actually engaged with the contents of my message.
I get that having a million irate customer service messages is a hassle. Using AI to triage them probably makes business sense, summarising the verbose ones could be useful for customer service agents, and totting up general themes is almost certainly a sensible way to gather metrics without overloading the workforce.
But using an AI to reply to a human is just disrespectful. It says the organisation values their customer's time at less than their own.
It's OK to call out shitty AI responses and insist that a human engages with you.

====================================================================================================

Just a little AI resistance, as a treat
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03

Apple is restricting dating apps and romanceable chatbots to over 18s
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
13:22:38 UTC
22:22:38 KST

Apple will soon start asking developers to provide more details about their apps to enforce new App Store age rating requirements for dating and location-sharing features. The upcoming content descriptors announced on Monday will require developers to declare if their apps allow users to share their real-world location with others, or include features that enable dating, relationship, or sexual behaviour between real people or virtual companions.
Apple is restricting dating apps and romanceable chatbots to over 18s
New App Store age rating guidance is being introduced for dating apps, romanceable virtual companions, and location sharing.
Apps that include dating and relationships features will receive a minimum age rating of 18 while those with location sharing will see an impact to their minimum age rating in some regions, depending on local regulations. These updates will be included in the App Store Connect age rating questionnaire starting November 16th, and all apps will be required to complete the new declarations by January 12th, 2027.
Apple already has an 18-plus rating category for the App Store, but this has largely applied to apps that feature gambling; violence; references to drugs, alcohol, or tobacco; and nudity or sexual content. Most dating platforms are also adult-only, but Apple extending its ratings is an effort to prevent minors from slipping through the cracks. These expanding restrictions also aim to keep them away from both AI chatbots that support sexual or romantic interactions, and virtual “girlfriend / boyfriend” simulators that could pose mental health risks.

Peak Design expands its collection of wireless charging accessories
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
12:53:37 UTC
21:53:37 KST

Peak Design, best known for its backpacks and travel bags packed full of useful features, announced it’s expanding its collection of chargers and charging accessories with new devices that focus on pairing premium materials with innovative functionality. “Somewhere along the way, we accepted that the electronics we use every day should be cheap, disposable and just good enough,” says Peter Dering, Peak Design’s CEO and founder. “We didn’t set out to make another collection of chargers and cables. We set out to show just how much better these everyday products could be.”
Peak Design expands its collection of wireless charging accessories
The collection includes what Peak Design claims is the ‘world’s first Qi2.3-certified wireless charger.’
The $99.95 Peak Design Wireless Kickstand Travel Battery, available in a black or moon (cream) finish, wraps a 5,000mAh battery in a magnetic housing made from machined anodized aluminum with a matching folding kickstand. The company describes it as the “world’s first Qi2.3-certified wireless charger,” which is the latest iteration of the Qi2 spec. Most 5,000mAh battery cells can’t safely wirelessly charge a device at 25W. It’s a feature that’s more common with batteries with at least a 10,000mAh capacity. But by supporting Qi2.3, Peak Design’s new travel battery can wirelessly charge a compatible device at 25W while it’s connected to a USB power source. On battery power alone, its wireless charging speed maxes out at 15W.
Peak Design’s $99.95 Wireless Charging Station 2-in-1, available in the same color options as its new travel battery, is a Qi2.2-compatible accessory featuring a pair of 25W wireless charging pads that can be used simultaneously at full power. One of the pads is on the charger’s base that’s made from die-cast aluminum and zinc, while the other is attached to an adjustable arm that folds flat so the charging station is easier to travel with.
The Dual Slim Charger is just a half-inch thick but can charge two devices with a maximum output of 65W.
Neither of the new wireless chargers comes with an adapter, but you can pair them with Peak Design’s new $64.95 Dual Slim Charger that uses GaN technology so it’s just a half-inch thick but delivers up to 65W of power through a pair of USB-C ports. They’re joined by an assortment of new Peak Design USB-C UltraStrand Cables ranging from $24.95 to $39.95. The cables are available in lengths ranging from 15 centimeters to 2 meters for a 60W / 480Mbps version, or 15 centimeters to a meter for the 240W / 40Gbps option.
Most of the new chargers and accessories are available starting today, but the Wireless Kickstand Travel Battery and certain lengths of the UltraStrand USB-C cables will be available a few days later on November 5th.

Charles Tandy, the mastermind behind Radio Shack
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
12:42:30 UTC
21:42:30 KST

Charles David Tandy was the chairman of the board, president, and chief executive officer of the Tandy Corporation. He was born May 15, 1918 and died November 4, 1978. Tandy was involved in the creation or expansion of three retail chains: Tandy Leather, Radio Shack, and Pier 1 Imports.
Early life
Tandy was the second of three children born to Carmen (née McLain) and David Lewis Tandy in Brownsville, Texas, on May 15, 1918. Charles’ youngest brother, Bill, went on to run his own chain of leather craft stores in the early 1950s before merging with Tandy Corporation.
At the age of 10, Charles and a classmate started selling scraps of leather from his father’s store to classmates for spare change, teaching them how to make belts out of them. With another classmate whose father owned a boot company, they started making heavier belts from scraps of boot leather that they sold to department stores.
In 1935 Tandy graduated from Central High School in Fort Worth, Texas.
Charles Tandy’s education and early career
Tandy’s father co-owned the Hinckley-Tandy Leather Company with his friend Norton Hinckley.
After flunking out of Rice Instutute in his freshman year, he graduated from Texas Christian Academy in 1940. While in college, he sold women’s shoes. He attended Harvard Business School but did not graduate. In the spring of 1941, the Navy offered commissions to Harvard Business School students. Tandy applied and was accepted, serving 6 years.
While in the Navy he set a record for selling war bonds. In 1945, he married Gwen Johnston, a wealthy widow 14 years his senior.
While he was in the Navy, he observed patients recovering in Navy hospitals doing leathercraft for therapy and recreation. He wrote a letter to his father suggesting this might be a way to expand Hinckley-Tandy’s business after World War II ended. This led to them setting up a mail order business, Tandycraft, after he left the Navy.
Tandy developed his small family leather business into an international corporation. He first turned it into a leather craft company when shoe rationing in World War II almost killed the business, and later expanded into selling leather and tools to make such products as wallets. Hinckley and Tandy split in 1950, with Hinckley taking the part of the business that sold shoe findings, and Tandy taking the leathercraft business.
Taking Tandy Corporation Public
In 1954, David Tandy suffered a heart attack, exposing a weakness in the corporate structure. Since Tandy was a private company, his death would have forced a liquidation of the company in order to pay the inheritance taxes. They sought a way to take the company public to protect it. The company wasn’t really large enough to conduct a traditional IPO, so instead, they sought to merge with a company that was already listed on the New York Stock Exchange. After shopping around for prospects, Charles Tandy engineered a leveraged buyout of the Tandy Leather Company by the American Hide and Leather Company, based in Boston, in 1955.
The Tandys and their leadership didn’t agree with the direction American Hide and Leather took the company after the merger. A power struggle ensued, resulting in the Tandys, their extended family, and Tandy managers buying as much stock in the company as they could, often using borrowed money. In 1959, they forced a proxy battle to take over the company. As a result, Tandy partisans received five of the nine seats on the board. They quickly divested three unprofitable subsidiaries American Hide and Leather had purchased after merging with Tandy, moved the corporate headquarters to New York to facilitate the divestitures, then moved operations to Fort Worth. In 1960, they changed the name to Tandy Corporation, taking the ticker symbol TAN.
Pier 1 Imports
By 1962, Charles Tandy had concluded he had ambitions bigger than the leather craft business could satisfy.
One of the businesses they looked into was Cost Plus Imports, which sold imported furniture and other goods out of a converted warehouse at near-wholesale prices. A failed public offering left the company in a cash crunch. Charles and Gwen were already planning a vacation to San Francisco in August 1962, so they visited the store as part of the trip. They found a 15,000 square foot store, several times larger than a Tandy store, selling $7-$8 million worth of merchandise per year when a Tandy store might sell $200,000. Tandy called one of his lieutenants, Luther Henderson, to come visit the store.
Tandy bought into the business, acquiring with it the right to operate stores under the Cost Plus name under Henderson’s leadership. But the partnership only lasted three years. Tandy found the business model worked well for one giant store in San Francisco, but needed substantial adjustment to work as a chain throughout the country. In 1965, they parted ways, with the Tandy-backed operation taking the name Pier 1 Imports.
But Henderson and Tandy found the way Tandy operated its leathercraft stores didn’t fit the furniture business, with its longer lead times. In 1966, Tandy sold Pier 1 to Henderson for $1.8 million.
Henderson and Tandy remained friendly, with Tandy even taking a seat on Pier 1’s board. Tandy chided Henderson, asking why they didn’t run the company that way when it was part of Tandy Corporation. The 1992 book Tandy’s Money Machine quoted Henderson as saying Tandy was a good mentor but he didn’t take the time to understand the differences between Pier 1’s and Tandy Leather’s business models. Given more room, Henderson thought the partnership could have worked.
Radio Shack
Charles Tandy saw the electronics business as a natural expansion for Tandy Corporation, another hobby with a similar business model. He approached Lafayette Electronics and Allied Electronics to no avail. So in 1961, he opened an electronics store called Electronics Crafts, soon opening a second and a third.
Meanwhile, in Boston, the same bank that had helped Tandy match up with American Hide and Leather had another creditor who was having trouble meeting its obligations, an electronics store called Radio Shack. The bank had told Tandy about Radio Shack before, when he was talking with Lafayette and Allied. Tandy wasn’t able to work with Milton Deutschmann, the head of Radio Shack, but with the company $7 million in debt and losing $200,000 a month, the bank forced Deutschmann to come to the table. Deutschmann agreed to an audit, and agreed to sell for a $300,000 advance that would give Tandy the right to acquire 62 percent ownership of Radio Shack at book value. Price Waterhouse conducted an audit and concluded its book value was zero, giving Tandy control of Radio Shack for $300,000.
How Charles Tandy turned around Radio Shack
Tandy’s turnaround plan was to close the large stores and open a large number of smaller stores in rented locations so he could close struggling stores with minimal risk, close the catalog business, and go to cash-only sales, eliminating credit.
By 1966, Radio Shack was profitable. And a decade later, it was a nationwide chain with over 3,500 company owned stores and another 3,500 franchises.
This quote dates itself, but business writer Milton Moskowitz called Radio Shack “the most spectacular retail expansion under one name since the explosion of the A&P grocery chain in the early part of the century.” The subsequent disappearance of both of those chains shows how fragile business is. Maybe Charles Tandy is an Ozymandias, but at least for now there are still a fair number of people living who remember his empire.
As for A&P, I had no idea it liquidated in 2015 because I live west of the Mississippi River. I remember them pulling out of Missouri in the 1970s and slowly fading away. If you live east of the Mississippi, you’re more likely to remember it as something more than the grocery store your grandmother or great grandmother shopped at.
Charles Tandy as a manager
Charles Tandy worked hard and drove his people hard. Although his business was hobbies, he had no hobbies himself. He was notorious for coming into the office late, but then he stayed more than 12 hours and often worked weekends.
He was frequently a harsh critic. By some accounts he would find the person in the room who could best handle a chewing out, then chew that person out in front of everyone.
“There are some delicate souls in the world who wilt at the first harsh words. Then ther are others that you need to use a baseball bat to discuss things with. So I’d have to say that Charles had a dial setting for everybody,” Tandy corporate secretary Bill Michero said.
Ted and Elaine Yamagata, his primary importers from Japan, said he was very demanding. “He would sometimes tear us apart, but he always put us together and we always left very encouraged,” Elaine Yamagata said.
Bill Brown, his banker in Boston, called the act of getting up in the middle of a meeting when things aren’t going your way, walking toward the door, and announcing you were going back to wherever you came from, “pulling a Charles Tandy.”
Charles Tandy’s last years
Tandy made other acquisitions after Radio Shack, including an attempt at running department stores. None of them were as successful as Radio Shack, and in most cases Tandy divested the acquisitions within a few years. His last major project was revitalizing downtown Fort Worth, culminating in the construction of the eight-block Tandy Center.
Tandy died of a heart attack in his sleep on 4 November 1978 at the age of 60, and was buried at Greenwood Memorial Park in Fort Worth, Texas.
David Farquhar is a computer security professional, entrepreneur, and author. He has written professionally about computers since 1991, so he was writing about retro computers when they were still new. He has been working in IT professionally since 1994 and has specialized in vulnerability management since 2013. He holds Security+ and CISSP certifications. Today he blogs five times a week, mostly about retro computers and retro gaming covering the time period from 1975 to 2000.

Tandy found the business model worked well for one giant store in San Francisco, but needed substantial adjustment to work as a chain throughout the country. In 1965, they parted ways, with the Tandy-backed operation taking the name Pier 1 Imports.
But Henderson and Tandy found the way Tandy operated its leathercraft stores didn’t fit the furniture business, with its longer lead times. In 1966, Tandy sold Pier 1 to Henderson for $1.8 million.
Henderson and Tandy remained friendly, with Tandy even taking a seat on Pier 1’s board. Tandy chided Henderson, asking why they didn’t run the company that way when it was part of Tandy Corporation. The 1992 book Tandy’s Money Machine quoted Henderson as saying Tandy was a good mentor but he didn’t take the time to understand the differences between Pier 1’s and Tandy Leather’s business models. Given more room, Henderson thought the partnership could have worked.
Radio Shack
Charles Tandy saw the electronics business as a natural expansion for Tandy Corporation, another hobby with a similar business model. He approached Lafayette Electronics and Allied Electronics to no avail. So in 1961, he opened an electronics store called Electronics Crafts, soon opening a second and a third.
Meanwhile, in Boston, the same bank that had helped Tandy match up with American Hide and Leather had another creditor who was having trouble meeting its obligations, an electronics store called Radio Shack. The bank had told Tandy about Radio Shack before, when he was talking with Lafayette and Allied. Tandy wasn’t able to work with Milton Deutschmann, the head of Radio Shack, but with the company $7 million in debt and losing $200,000 a month, the bank forced Deutschmann to come to the table. Deutschmann agreed to an audit, and agreed to sell for a $300,000 advance that would give Tandy the right to acquire 62 percent ownership of Radio Shack at book value. Price Waterhouse conducted an audit and concluded its book value was zero, giving Tandy control of Radio Shack for $300,000.
How Charles Tandy turned around Radio Shack
Tandy’s turnaround plan was to close the large stores and open a large number of smaller stores in rented locations so he could close struggling stores with minimal risk, close the catalog business, and go to cash-only sales, eliminating credit.
By 1966, Radio Shack was profitable. And a decade later, it was a nationwide chain with over 3,500 company owned stores and another 3,500 franchises.
This quote dates itself, but business writer Milton Moskowitz called Radio Shack “the most spectacular retail expansion under one name since the explosion of the A&P grocery chain in the early part of the century.” The subsequent disappearance of both of those chains shows how fragile business is. Maybe Charles Tandy is an Ozymandias, but at least for now there are still a fair number of people living who remember his empire.
As for A&P, I had no idea it liquidated in 2015 because I live west of the Mississippi River. I remember them pulling out of Missouri in the 1970s and slowly fading away. If you live east of the Mississippi, you’re more likely to remember it as something more than the grocery store your grandmother or great grandmother shopped at.
Charles Tandy as a manager
Charles Tandy worked hard and drove his people hard. Although his business was hobbies, he had no hobbies himself. He was notorious for coming into the office late, but then he stayed more than 12 hours and often worked weekends.
He was frequently a harsh critic. By some accounts he would find the person in the room who could best handle a chewing out, then chew that person out in front of everyone.
“There are some delicate souls in the world who wilt at the first harsh words. Then ther are others that you need to use a baseball bat to discuss things with. So I’d have to say that Charles had a dial setting for everybody,” Tandy corporate secretary Bill Michero said.
Ted and Elaine Yamagata, his primary importers from Japan, said he was very demanding. “He would sometimes tear us apart, but he always put us together and we always left very encouraged,” Elaine Yamagata said.
Bill Brown, his banker in Boston, called the act of getting up in the middle of a meeting when things aren’t going your way, walking toward the door, and announcing you were going back to wherever you came from, “pulling a Charles Tandy.”
Charles Tandy’s last years
Tandy made other acquisitions after Radio Shack, including an attempt at running department stores. None of them were as successful as Radio Shack, and in most cases Tandy divested the acquisitions within a few years. His last major project was revitalizing downtown Fort Worth, culminating in the construction of the eight-block Tandy Center.
Tandy died of a heart attack in his sleep on 4 November 1978 at the age of 60, and was buried at Greenwood Memorial Park in Fort Worth, Texas.
David Farquhar is a computer security professional, entrepreneur, and author. He has written professionally about computers since 1991, so he was writing about retro computers when they were still new. He has been working in IT professionally since 1994 and has specialized in vulnerability management since 2013. He holds Security+ and CISSP certifications. Today he blogs five times a week, mostly about retro computers and retro gaming covering the time period from 1975 to 2000.

The 'Astra can read and write weights to improve Qwen' post is misleading
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
12:21:17 UTC
21:21:17 KST

Stage magicians often do the same trick multiple ways. One iteration uses a gimmick prop, the next one shows you the prop isn’t gimmick but that one palms the object, the third time was a forced choice etc. When you watch the performance you assume there’s only one technique, so each time they do it they seem to debunk your hypothesis about how it must be working.
This is pretty much how the experiments in the ‘Astra can read and write weights to improve Qwen’ post work. The post’s abstract says:
Just by looking at the raw weights, GPT-6 Astra can tell what a network was trained to do. And without any environment feedback, it has no trouble writing, from scratch, small game-playing networks that outplay networks trained with PPO for 100 million steps.
Likewise, asked to make Qwen3.5-0.8B better at math without gradients, Astra identifies an issue in its weights causing reasoning loops that Qwen’s own model card warns about, and fixes this by natively editing 0.016% of the weights. With the same sampling settings, this weight correction raises MATH-500 from 7.9% to 48.1% and generalizes out of the box to instruction following, raising IFEval by 3x.
If you read that, would you assume that the Qwen experiment worked ‘just by looking at the raw weights’? Well it doesn’t. There’s experiments that look at raw weights, and experiments that improve a game-playing network, and an experiment that improves Qwen at maths. But those latter two aren’t just working with raw weights, they write Python programs.
Here’s a summary of what the post actually does and how the techniques work.
The “just by looking at the raw weights” setting (raw floats in context, no tools, no running the network) only appears in experiments 1, 2 and 4, on networks of 17K–172K weights. Every experiment involving a real language model uses Python, and the Qwen result, which is the headline one, also gets to run the model on up to 50 batches of practice problems.
What the Qwen result actually shows is that Astra can be prompted to devise and perform tasks that a human ML engineer could do. Human ML engineers write snippets of code that analyse or even modify network weights “directly” all the time. Figuring out how to do this productively is not nothing, and AI models of a year or two ago would have failed at all these tasks.
But to me the Qwen result and the other experiments that use Python don’t belong in the same post or paper as the experiments that read the weights directly. A single technique that could achieve both, “Author weights for a small game from scratch” and “Improve the performance of a large model on a sample task” would imply a deep capability. If you test a single technique that way you’ve demonstrated a sort of ‘gestalt’ of the weights (it can write them out from scratch) and also that it understands a large existing network. But that’s not at all true here, because it’s not a single technique.

Monsters of Ohio is OUT!!!

Monsters of Ohio is OUT!!!
Posted on November 3, 2026 Posted by John Scalzi 14 Comments
It’s time! Monsters of Ohio is out in the world, available at your favorite bookseller in print and ebook, and also in audio through Audible. In the UK, it will be available on the 5th, because that’s just when books get put out over there. Here are links for Amazon, Barnes & Noble, Bookshop and of course it’s available just about everywhere else as well, including your own local bookstore.
MOO (as it rather delightfully initializes down to) has gotten great reviews, including starred reviews in Bookpage, Kirkus and Library Journal, which makes me happy. It’s also a Library Reads Hall of Fame pick, which also delights me.
Also, I’m on a book tour — starting today! — through (mostly) the American Midwest, because it seemed the right place to tour this particular book. Please come see me and support the booksellers hosting me this time around.
Not on my tour schedule but still want a signed copy? You can order a copy from any one of the bookstores hosting me on my tour and I will be happy to sign the book while I am in their shop so they can ship it to you. I will also be signing copies for my own local bookstore, Jay & Mary’s Book Center, today, so they will have signed copies available as well. All of those are likely to ship only to the US, sorry.
(Also we’ll be announcing our annual holiday signing thing tomorrow, so stay tuned for that!)
Monsters of Ohio takes place, as the title suggests, in Ohio — but more than that, it takes place where I live, in the fictional town of Richland, located in the very real Darke County. Lots of elements of the book are taken from or inspired by my immediate surroundings, and in many ways this is the most personal novel I’ve written, and certainly the novel closest to me in a geographical sense. Now, it also has monsters and exploding things, so there’s that too. There’s a lot going on here.
The point is, I’m really proud of this one. I hope you all like it.
Also, in the US it’s election day. If you’ve not done so already, vote! And then get this book as a reward. If every voter in the United States rewarded themselves with this book, then, well. It would be a very good day for me.
— JS

Adobe’s Premiere mobile app is adding licensed music from ‘leading artists’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
11:41:30 UTC
20:41:30 KST

Adobe is teaming up with Universal Music Group and Warner Music Group to add a library of licensed music clips to the Premiere mobile video editing app. No specific musicians have been announced with this partnership, but Adobe said it will include “popular songs” from “leading artists,” and enable Premiere mobile users to quickly jump on “the latest trend.”
Adobe’s Premiere mobile app is adding licensed music from ‘leading artists’
Universal and Warner music groups are working with Adobe to provide a browsable library of fully licensed song clips.
“By bringing licensed music from UMG artists into Adobe Premiere mobile, we’re giving creators new ways to build their stories around the songs they love for the first time, while helping our artists and songwriters reach new audiences,” Universal Music Group’s Bryan Stone said in the announcement.
Adobe says this feature is “coming soon,” and will allow Premiere mobile users to:
- Add up to 60 seconds of a popular track directly to the video.
- Browse, search and preview trending music by artist or track, filter by genre and duration, bookmark favorites, and explore popular music through label-featured collections.
- Access customizable templates that are already paired with music to speed up editing.
- Export finished videos in Premiere mobile and post to licensed social platforms.
The Premiere Pro desktop app and Premiere mobile both already include a built-in library of royalty-free music that editors can work into their videos, but licensed audio needs to be manually imported. This update aims to make it easier to incorporate recognizable music into video edits, and help Adobe’s mobile video editor compete against popular rivals like CapCut. We have asked Adobe if these updates will also be coming to Premiere Pro, and will update if we hear back.

Sam Altman's Case Against Your RAM Is Efficient. That's the Problem
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
11:32:43 UTC
20:32:43 KST

Sam Altman’s Case Against Your RAM Is Efficient. That’s The Problem
from the you-optimized-the-wrong-thing dept
By now it’s likely you’ve heard of the whole concept of the AI paperclip maximizer problem — a hypothetical first put forth by Nick Bostrom — suggesting that a misaligned AI system that looks to optimize on the wrong single thing (in this case creating paperclips) could lead to the end of humanity. Specifically, the thought experiment is that if you have a sufficiently powerful AI tool that is told to “maximize” the creation of paperclips, it might decide the best way to do that is to seize control of all resources and dedicate them solely to making paperclips. And, naturally, that might include grinding up humans, either as raw material for paperclips or as fuel to power the paperclip factory.
The supposed “lesson” of this is to be cautious about these AI tools, though I think the actual lessons are twofold: (1) thought experiments can only take you so far and (2) never over optimize on a single metric. Indeed, this was part of what our recent series, The Metric is Not the Mission, was all about.
Anyway, everyone in the AI world knows about the whole paperclip thing. I guarantee you Sam Altman knows about it. He has praised the book where Bostrom put forth the hypothetical, claiming that it was “the best thing I’ve seen” on the topic of “will AI kill us all.” And he’s joked about it in a memey way:
But it’s not clear that he truly has internalized that lesson regarding not optimizing on just a single thing. Last week he went on John Collison’s (co-founder of Stripe) “Cheeky Pint” podcast to talk about the state of AI.
A bunch of the headlines focused on one specific thing that he said, however, that it’s “quite terrible” for people to be wasting RAM in home machines over having them working full steam in a data center somewhere. The context here matters a bit. Collison catches Altman a bit off guard by asking “how’s the compute shortage going?” just as Altman takes a big gulp from his pint of beer, and you see Altman try not to laugh/spit out his beer, before there’s an obvious cut in the video, where he recomposes himself.
They then have a discussion about the increasing demand for compute, Altman talks about how they’re working on having robots build “self-replicating data centers” (what?!?) to which Collison rightly interjects that the problem with data centers right now isn’t the lack of labor available to build them. Collison then shifts the conversation back to the stuff that most people care about: the fact that RAM prices have increased dramatically recently, which is jacking up the price of everything from computers to phones.
Collison notes that people keep saying the market should take care of this: if prices keep going up, shouldn’t Adam Smith’s “invisible hand” motivate more companies to make more RAM and ease the shortage? Altman shows that he’s not a big believer in the invisible hand of the market concept:
Collison: My observation, the compute stuff is they say: “The solution to high prices is high prices.” And it’s like, okay.
Step 1: We have the high prices for memory, and GPUs, and everything. The solutions should be here any moment now. How are we doing on the solutions front?
Altman: Bad.
Collison: And I’m not saying this is OpenAI’s job. I feel this is the invisible hand’s job, right? Memory’s gotten really expensive. So, new memory makers are going to flood the market any moment now.
Altman: I think the invisible hand is failing right now. I mean, the invisible hand… It has like a checkered track record at best. But, I think it’s not quite working here.
He then points out that, for decades, DRAM was a commodity product with razor-thin margins and barely any profits. His argument is that memory makers aren’t willing to expand much because they’re afraid that the bubble might burst and they’d have overbuilt (which… might not be wrong?).
Of course, Altman leaves out of this the fact that OpenAI has basically tried to corner the market on RAM, trying to lock up 40% of the world’s supply without even guaranteeing that it will follow through on the actual purchase orders. I mean, I think that alone would have at least some impact on the current price for RAM, wouldn’t it?
Collison pushes a bit further, and still wants to know why others aren’t getting into the memory market, and even (half jokingly?) suggests Intel could get into the market — a reference to one of the key parts of Intel’s lore and mystique: the Andy Grove decision in 1985 to get completely out of the memory chip business to focus on processors, which got a bunch of recent attention when Intel’s current CEO suggested the (struggling) company might reverse that decision 40 years later.
That brings Altman around to the critical quote regarding local RAM, in which he dismisses the argument that local inference is important, as a way of suggesting that the more efficient use of memory would be in large datacenters where it can be utilized at a much higher rate:
Oh, I think there are all of these ways where the supply chain—it’s just super non-obvious.
An example of this is people…A lot of people want local inference. And somehow, they assume that the problem is solved now. Because, we are going to have open-source models. We’re going to be able to run them on our computers. And so we’re not going to need all the stuff in the data center.
But from a global allocation perspective, actually, it’s quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world’s limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer. And, being used—Mm-hmm. 5% of the time. You want maximum utilization in the cloud if you’re trying to deal with this very supply constrained environment.
And somehow everyone’s… many people’s intuition is the opposite of that.
So he’s correct… in one very, very, very narrow sense of this quote. If you are only interested in dealing with the current “world’s limited supply of DRAM” he’s not wrong that it is more “efficient” to have that RAM be in a data center and then rented out to you. And he’s not exactly wrong that if everyone just paid per token for remote compute, the cost of RAM in your own PC would matter a lot less.
But… he’s kinda missing the larger point: that people are not just focused on optimizing for the most efficient use of memory. They’re not just optimizing for what’s best for “the global supply chain of RAM makers.” They have other concerns — and other uses beyond AI — as well, such as their own sovereignty and control over their own tools and systems. There’s a reason my essay on the need for decentralization (including in AI) went so viral a few months back.
And it’s not because people think they’re single-handedly going to solve the global supply crisis in DRAM by running everything locally. It’s because we’ve seen what happens when a central provider controls key parts of our stack, and the results are very, very, very bad.
Using open weight models on local hardware may not be the most efficient for the exact price of RAM (which, again, Altman tried to corner the market on), but it certainly leads to much more actual user empowerment by people who want to use these tools, but without outsourcing control to someone like Altman.
In a pure “efficiency” model where you are solely optimizing on what’s the most cost efficient way to use memory the answer would be to have it in datacenters where you’d benefit from both economies of scale as well as flexible utilization. But that has consequences. It’s the same logic by which a fascist dictator may seem “more efficient” to those sick of democratic quirks and bureaucratic red tape. It only sounds good if your mental model is completely screwed up and simplistic.
In both cases, you’re talking about a very simplified mental model, optimizing on a suboptimal point. Personal autonomy over our tools and our systems is more important than making your data center more efficient, Sam. The paperclip problem comes from focusing on a narrow metric and over optimizing on it. And in this clip, Sam is paperclipping RAM.
	Filed Under: ai, data centers, john collison, local ai, memory, optimization, ram, sam altman, supply, supply and demand, supply chain
	
Companies: intel, openai
