Yvonne Bajela is bringing $17M in fresh capital to Europe’s startup scene
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
08:01:00 UTC
17:01:00 KST

Yvonne Bajela knew it was time to launch her own fund. She spent more than a decade as a VC, most recently at LocalGlobe and Impact X Capital Partners, where she made bets on now-unicorn companies like the fintech Wise, the insurance company Marshmallow, and robotaxi Mooves.
On Tuesday, she announced that her own fund, Evertrue Capital, officially closed an oversubscribed $17 million Fund I.
“After more than a decade backing founders who bet everything on their ideas, it felt like time to make a bet of my own,” she said. “I wanted the independence to act on those convictions and build something that reflected my own approach to investing.”
The firm, which has already backed seven companies, will focus on early-stage investing across the U.K. and Europe. The average check size will be around £350,000, she said, with plans to back at least 20 companies over the next three years.
Perhaps unsurprisingly, she said this AI boom helped her realize that this was the right time to launch her own fund. Not only has it changed the economics of launching a company, she said, but it has created new opportunities for early-stage investors like herself. “The ability to build has been democratized,” she said. “But the ability to build something enduring remains rare.”
As TechCrunch has previously reported, the U.K.’s startup scene is having a moment. King’s Cross, for example, is one of the world’s top AI hubs, while companies like Revolut, Synthesia, ElevenLabs, and the Bezos-backed CuspAI are redefining what it means to scale and attract success in the U.K. market. For someone like Bajela, this moment means there is an opportunity to snap up investments in the next generation of top British talent early. “I think more globally significant businesses will emerge from a wider range of starting points,” she said.
However, this is also a tough market for emerging fund managers. In the U.S., it has been described as a barbell market where capital is concentrated at the top and among firms led by those who already have solid track records. Things may be slightly rosier in the U.K., as PitchBook data through the end of September shows that emerging fund managers in this region raised $7.4 billion, already surpassing the $6.2 billion they raised in all of 2025.
Still, Bajela said fundraising was a grind. The hardest rejections weren’t necessarily the outright nos. “They were the ones that came after months of conversations and significant time invested, only for the answer to ultimately be no,” she said. “Time is one of the most valuable resources a founder has, and a prolonged process without clarity has a real cost.”
Still, she said the fund nabbed five institutional backers, including the British Business Bank and Basecamp, the fund-of-funds of the investment firm Phoenix Court, which is also home to LocalGlobe. Evertrue was part of the £400 million British Business Bank’s Investor Pathways capital initiative, which saw it distribute £90 million to 10 first-time fund managers. Bajela said Evertrue wanted to raise around $13 million but saw a lot of investor interest. She decided to turn down additional investors rather than expand the fund size.
“Fund size is a strategic decision, not a measure of success in itself,” she said. “I wanted enough capital to build meaningful positions in my highest-conviction investments without creating pressure to deploy capital simply because it was available.”
Around 80% of Evertrue’s LPs are U.K. based, with the rest scattered across Europe, Asia, and the U.S. She said her international LP base represents the relationships she’s built “over more than a decade investing across markets.” It also reflects the fund’s global ambition, she said, especially since so many British founders keep their eyes on a U.S. expansion; more than half her portfolio already operates in the U.S.
Evertrue will invest mainly across AI and fintech, she said, adding that she’s “particularly interested in people with a unique insight into a problem, an unusual ability to execute, and the ambition to build something much bigger than their initial product.”
“I also pay attention to the distance a founder has traveled, what they’ve overcome, and how that has shaped their perspective,” she continued.
She’s lived in Japan as a senior investment manager at Mitsui and started her career as an analyst at Goldman Sachs. Throughout her career, she said she’s seen how “widely distributed ambition and talent are.”
“I want Evertrue to be known for the quality of the founders I back and the businesses they go on to build.”

====================================================================================================

Could a modern chess engine on a Pentium have defeated Kasparov in 1996?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
07:56:21 UTC
16:56:21 KST

by Nicholas Carlini 2026-11-01
On February 10th, 1996, a computer program (IBM's Deep Blue) defeated the reigning chess champion (Garry Kasparov) in a game of chess for the first time. Deep Blue would go on to lose the match, but a year later, would get its revenge in a 3.5-2.5 rematch.
In order to achieve this feat, IBM spent several million dollars on research and development building a special-purpose computer specifically for this purpose. Deep Blue itself (the victorious, upgraded version) was powered by 30 P2SC processors each running at roughly 120 MHz, and (most importantly) 480 custom VLSI chess co-processors used to perform chess-specific operations like move generation and position evaluation. When it was built, Deep Blue was the 259th most powerful supercomputer in the world, and could analyze over 200 million chess positions per second.
But was Deep Blue necessary to beat Kasparov? Or could we have gotten away with an off-the-shelf 1996 desktop running a smarter chess engine?
As it turns out, we could easily have beaten Kasparov without building Deep Blue, using only commodity hardware, and it (almost certainly) wouldn't have even been close. I know this because, as I write this very sentence, I have sitting next to me a 200 MHz Intel Pentium Pro (released exactly 31 years ago---November 1st, 1995) with 64MB of RAM running a custom port of Stockfish that plays at an estimated Elo of 3300 ± 87 at classical time controls---far beyond the strength of any human, or indeed Kasparov's Elo of just under 2800. But wait, there's more! I've hooked this machine up to the internet with a Realtek RTL8139C Ethernet card, so you can play it below!
There are three ways to get stronger at chess: you can analyze more moves, analyze better moves, or analyze moves better. Famously, humans improve by doing the latter two: the average chess grandmaster during a classical game will analyze roughly the same number of moves as an amateur player, but they analyze only the most critical moves and do a better job with that analysis. Chess engines like Stockfish, running on silicon instead of our human brains, dominate us at the first of these and analyze millions of positions per second. And because there's not that much room for micro-optimizations on modern engines that will let a given CPU process more positions per second, most progress in chess AI also tends to follow how humans improve and finds better methods for selecting moves and analyzing boards.
Skill in chess is measured by an Elo score. A difference of 200 Elo means that you should expect the stronger player to win three out of every four games, and multiplicatively from there: a 400 Elo gap corresponds to a 90% expected score and a 1000 Elo gap gives a 99.7% expected score. The best chess engines that you could run on commodity 1996 hardware were rated roughly 2300 Elo. Deep Blue was rated 2700. In contrast, modern Stockfish is rated closer to 3700.
This raises the question: how much of the advantage of a modern chess engine comes down to the fact that a modern desktop computer is extremely fast, and how much of the advantage comes down to the fact that we've just gotten better at programming chess engines? As it turns out, the majority of the Elo gains (+720 of the +1335 gained) can be attributed to better programming, and not better hardware.
To test this, I ran two experiments. The first experiment uses Crafty, a competitive engine from 1996 whose source code is now available. On my Pentium Pro, the 1996 build of Crafty version 11.13 has an Elo of 1999 ± 90 at 1 second per move, and 2383 ± 77 at 10 seconds per move. On modern hardware (a Ryzen 7) this engine's Elo jumps massively to 2614 ± 97 at the same 1 second per move. But if I take this engine and slowly start to add all the knowledge we have about how to design better chess engines---adding modern search algorithms like fail-soft, LMR, correction history; adding better quiescence methods; and adding neural-net evals (NNUE)---then you can increase the Elo up to 2718 ± 34 on the Pentium Pro at 1 second per move, and 2974 ± 74 at 10 seconds per move.
In the second experiment I go somewhat further: instead of trying to update Crafty, let's just try to get Stockfish---the best chess engine today---running on 1996 hardware and see how it does. This takes some work and is mainly a matter of rewriting the code so that it uses 32-bit arithmetic (a PPro is a 32-bit machine) and doesn't use more than 64MB of RAM. This naive port has an Elo of roughly 2496 ± 64 at 1 second per move (2940 ± 76 at 10 seconds per move). But we can do better! A modern Stockfish engine is designed to run on a modern CPU. By making a number of adjustments to the algorithm and tuning it to the Pentium, we can increase its Elo to 2846 ± 35 at 1 second per move, 3109 ± 79 at 10 seconds per move, and 3180 ± 80 at 30 seconds per move.
The most concise way to summarize all of the above is just to have them play a match of 100 games against each other. And when I do this, I find that a modern chess engine running on a 1996 Pentium Pro wins 78, draws 17, and loses 5 games against the 1996 Crafty engine running on modern hardware.
The remainder of this article provides the details.
There's not much to say about the hardware, to be honest. I bought a used ("vintage" said the listing; I feel old.) desktop PC with standard period-appropriate hardware. The desktop is running a 200 MHz Intel Pentium Pro connected to an Intel VS440FX motherboard with 64MB of SIMM RAM clocked at 66 MHz. The operating system boot off of a 1.44MB 3.5 inch floppy disk, and connect to the Internet via a Realtek RTL8139C. I also have a 1MB Trio64V+ graphics card connected over VGA to my monitor, with input coming from a PS/2 keyboard---but I don't much use either of those and instead communicate with it over a serial port connected to my modern desktop.
For those readers who may not be able to appropriately contextualize this hardware, a 200 MHz Pentium Pro sold for $1,325 at launch in November 1995, and four 16 MB SIMMs would have cost about $2,000. Add on the other peripherals, and this machine would likely have gone for about four or five thousand dollars. So it's a nice workstation, but by no means a supercomputer. However, given the rate of depreciation and the exponential rate of improvements to hardware, this same machine in 1997, when the second match occurred, would likely go for just a thousand dollars (because who would want to buy a Pentium Pro when the better-in-almost-every-way Pentium MMX was now available for just $400?)
The interface between the chess engine and the internet is a custom-built "operating system" whose sole job is to spin up the chess engine and connect it directly to the networking card. (Throughout this article, when I say "I wrote", what I really mean is "I asked an LLM to write". I used a combination of Fable 5.1, Opus 5.5, and GPT-6 to implement the code here. (This article, though, is human text.).) It's designed to be as compact as possible: the operating system (which, in fact, is more like a hypervisor, or something) is sitting on a bootable floppy disk. Its sole job is to boot the Pentium Pro into 32-bit protected mode, initialize the networking card, and await further instructions. I can then send it any arbitrary "userspace" guest program that I want into RAM and it'll boot that up and start running it. The chess program I wrote starts up a custom-built HTTP server which waits for incoming HTTP requests with a given position (as a FEN string) and maximum run time, and on each request runs the engine until the timer expires, and returns a response.
As mentioned earlier I (again: a language model) actually modified two engines. The stronger of the two is powered by Stockfish adapted to run efficiently on a thirty-year-old processor. It plays at an estimated Elo of 3300 at classical time controls Because I can't run nearly enough games at classical time controls on my single machine to get this number, I've extrapolated it from shorter time controls. I measured 3180 ± 80 at 30 seconds per move, and it gained +70 Elo per tripling of the time between 1 second to 10 seconds and 10 seconds to 30 seconds. Because a classical game gives about six minutes per move (including thinking on the opponent's turn) we should expect an Elo of 3350. Allowing for some diminishing returns, a safe estimate is an Elo of 3300. , high enough above Kasparov's 2800 Elo that we should expect Stockfish-1996 to come out ahead with a score of roughly 9/10. The (only slightly) weaker engine starts with the source code to Crafty 11.13, and is brought forward in time by adding all the lessons we've learned about how to write a better chess engine over the past thirty years.
The first engine I (read: my LLM) worked on was Crafty 11.13, its 1996 release. On this engine the LLM had to do the most work to backport ideas we've learned over the last 30 years. Below is a breakdown of where the Elo gains come from, when evaluating either at 1 second per move or 10 seconds per move. Sometimes you'll find the 1s vs 10s improvements disagree. Part of this clearly could be noise---I only ran a few hundred games which leaves a relatively large margin of error---but there is also quite a lot of signal here. Because the 1996 computer is so much slower, at 1s/move it has a very low search depth and so how fast the evaluation runs matters more than how good it is. But at larger search depths, once you stop making tactical blunders, the position evaluation becomes much more important.
On the second engine, as mentioned above, I did the inverse. I (again, read: my LLM) ported Stockfish to run on 1996 hardware, and then tweaked the parameters so that it would be better suited to lower search depth. The gains here are thus somewhat more modest, because there are no large algorithm wins that remain.
I think it's overwhelmingly likely to be the case, but it's actually a little bit more tricky of a situation than you might hope. You see, I've done a bit of sleight of hand throughout this article. The problem is that Elo scores are relative, and I've been comparing my engine's Elo to that of various Stockfish engines that have known strengths on the Computer Chess Rating Lists (CCRL). But because humans don't play against engines very frequently, there can be some small divergences between scores on the FIDE Elo ladder and the CCRL ladder, in the same way a player's FIDE Elo doesn't directly translate to their Elo on online chess. Fortunately, because I've gotten these engines to play at an Elo rating of 3200-3300, and Kasparov was rated just under 2800, there should be more than sufficient buffer to absorb any discrepancy.
But if you happen to know a 2700+ Super GM who wants to try and defeat a 2026 chess engine playing on 1996 hardware, I'd be happy to accept the challenge!
The first time we humans do anything, we rarely do it efficiently. Once we see a path that would clearly work if only we scaled it up, we do just that. Only later, after we've had the time to better understand the problem at hand, do we come to recognize that we could have achieved the same feat much more efficiently by being clever.
I mostly did this for fun, because whether I could have beaten Kasparov with commodity hardware sounded like a fun question to answer, but I also hoped it would provide a tiny bit of insight into the rate of algorithmic progress in "AI". Chess engines in 1997 were already quite advanced; after all, Alan Turing helped write the first chess program nearly fifty years earlier. So I suppose at the time it seemed plausible that maybe there weren't that many more ideas left to find. And with hindsight it's easy to see that's wrong.
Which leads to the question: just how much are we leaving on the table with modern-day "AI"---that is to say, language models. Will the advances we accrue over the next few years from software outpace the advances in hardware scale? Clearly this toy experiment doesn't answer that question definitively, but it does gesture at a potential future. (There's also probably some kind of meta-lesson about using LLMs---"AI"---to speed up our ability to answer weird research questions like this.)
(Also, uh, if anyone has any ideas for projects I could do with a Pentium Pro in a few weeks once I no longer need it to host this chess engine ... do let me know!)

====================================================================================================

Could a modern chess engine on a Pentium have defeated Kasparov in 1996?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
07:56:21 UTC
16:56:21 KST

by Nicholas Carlini 2026-11-01
On February 10th, 1996, a computer program (IBM's Deep Blue) defeated the reigning chess champion (Garry Kasparov) in a game of chess for the first time. Deep Blue would go on to lose the match, but a year later, would get its revenge in a 3.5-2.5 rematch.
In order to achieve this feat, IBM spent several million dollars on research and development building a special-purpose computer specifically for this purpose. Deep Blue itself (the victorious, upgraded version) was powered by 30 P2SC processors each running at roughly 120 MHz, and (most importantly) 480 custom VLSI chess co-processors used to perform chess-specific operations like move generation and position evaluation. When it was built, Deep Blue was the 259th most powerful supercomputer in the world, and could analyze over 200 million chess positions per second.
But was Deep Blue necessary to beat Kasparov? Or could we have gotten away with an off-the-shelf 1996 desktop running a smarter chess engine?
As it turns out, we could easily have beaten Kasparov without building Deep Blue, using only commodity hardware, and it (almost certainly) wouldn't have even been close. I know this because, as I write this very sentence, I have sitting next to me a 200 MHz Intel Pentium Pro (released exactly 31 years ago---November 1st, 1995) with 64MB of RAM running a custom port of Stockfish that plays at an estimated Elo of 3300 ± 87 at classical time controls---far beyond the strength of any human, or indeed Kasparov's Elo of just under 2800. But wait, there's more! I've hooked this machine up to the internet with a Realtek RTL8139C Ethernet card, so you can play it below!
There are three ways to get stronger at chess: you can analyze more moves, analyze better moves, or analyze moves better. Famously, humans improve by doing the latter two: the average chess grandmaster during a classical game will analyze roughly the same number of moves as an amateur player, but they analyze only the most critical moves and do a better job with that analysis. Chess engines like Stockfish, running on silicon instead of our human brains, dominate us at the first of these and analyze millions of positions per second. And because there's not that much room for micro-optimizations on modern engines that will let a given CPU process more positions per second, most progress in chess AI also tends to follow how humans improve and finds better methods for selecting moves and analyzing boards.
Skill in chess is measured by an Elo score. A difference of 200 Elo means that you should expect the stronger player to win three out of every four games, and multiplicatively from there: a 400 Elo gap corresponds to a 90% expected score and a 1000 Elo gap gives a 99.7% expected score. The best chess engines that you could run on commodity 1996 hardware were rated roughly 2300 Elo. Deep Blue was rated 2700. In contrast, modern Stockfish is rated closer to 3700.
This raises the question: how much of the advantage of a modern chess engine comes down to the fact that a modern desktop computer is extremely fast, and how much of the advantage comes down to the fact that we've just gotten better at programming chess engines? As it turns out, the majority of the Elo gains (+720 of the +1335 gained) can be attributed to better programming, and not better hardware.
To test this, I ran two experiments. The first experiment uses Crafty, a competitive engine from 1996 whose source code is now available. On my Pentium Pro, the 1996 build of Crafty version 11.13 has an Elo of 1999 ± 90 at 1 second per move, and 2383 ± 77 at 10 seconds per move. On modern hardware (a Ryzen 7) this engine's Elo jumps massively to 2614 ± 97 at the same 1 second per move. But if I take this engine and slowly start to add all the knowledge we have about how to design better chess engines---adding modern search algorithms like fail-soft, LMR, correction history; adding better quiescence methods; and adding neural-net evals (NNUE)---then you can increase the Elo up to 2718 ± 34 on the Pentium Pro at 1 second per move, and 2974 ± 74 at 10 seconds per move.
In the second experiment I go somewhat further: instead of trying to update Crafty, let's just try to get Stockfish---the best chess engine today---running on 1996 hardware and see how it does. This takes some work and is mainly a matter of rewriting the code so that it uses 32-bit arithmetic (a PPro is a 32-bit machine) and doesn't use more than 64MB of RAM. This naive port has an Elo of roughly 2496 ± 64 at 1 second per move (2940 ± 76 at 10 seconds per move). But we can do better! A modern Stockfish engine is designed to run on a modern CPU. By making a number of adjustments to the algorithm and tuning it to the Pentium, we can increase its Elo to 2846 ± 35 at 1 second per move, 3109 ± 79 at 10 seconds per move, and 3180 ± 80 at 30 seconds per move.
The most concise way to summarize all of the above is just to have them play a match of 100 games against each other. And when I do this, I find that a modern chess engine running on a 1996 Pentium Pro wins 78, draws 17, and loses 5 games against the 1996 Crafty engine running on modern hardware.
The remainder of this article provides the details.

uBlock Origin filter to block autoplay videos in TechCrunch
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
07:36:47 UTC
16:36:47 KST

Every TechCrunch article autoplays a video in the middle of the post. Hiding it isn’t enough, because the player keeps running in the background.
The video is a JW Player embed, and the page also loads Connatix’s floating ad player. Blocking their scripts stops the video from loading at all, and the cosmetic rules hide the empty box it leaves behind.
These work in uBlock Origin on Firefox (the full MV2 version, not uBO Lite). Paste them into Dashboard → My filters and click Apply changes:
! TechCrunch: stop the autoplaying JW Player video
||content.jwplatform.com/libraries/$script,domain=techcrunch.com
||ssl.p.jwpcdn.com^$script,domain=techcrunch.com
||connatix.com^$third-party,domain=techcrunch.com
techcrunch.com##.wp-block-techcrunch-jw-player-embed
techcrunch.com##.jw-player-inline-promo
If TechCrunch switches video vendors, the ## rules will probably keep hiding
the box, but you’ll need a new network rule to stop the audio.

Apple Watch’s AI features for rewinding and recapping conversations begin beta testing
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
07:20:00 UTC
16:20:00 KST

Apple’s AI-powered “Audio Intelligence” technology for the Apple Watch is rolling out into beta testing on Tuesday, initially for developers in the iOS 27.3 update, after first being announced at the company’s Surprise and Shine hardware event in September. The technology includes a set of four opt-in AI features that allow Apple Watch Series 12 wearers to detect important sounds, identify songs, catch up on conversations, and take notes.
The news was announced via a press release sent out via Apple in India, but the update has not yet rolled out in the U.S. market as of this morning.
The sound recognition feature will help deaf and hard-of-hearing users identify important sounds like sirens, alarms, or doorbells from their watch, while the song recognition feature taps into Apple’s Shazam app to detect music playing in the background and identify the song name and artist.
However, the more potentially controversial features are those that listen to human conversations: Live Rewind and Siri Recaps.
Given the backlash to AI recording devices, like Meta’s AI glasses and other AI wearables, Apple has attempted to carve out a space where the features can be useful to Apple Watch owners, while still respecting the privacy of those around them.
With Live Rewind, the idea is to let you go back in time to see what someone just said, in case you missed it. For instance, if someone was giving you verbal directions or reading a long list of specials at a restaurant, you could double-press on the Watch’s digital crown to see what was said in the last 15 seconds as a text snippet displayed on the watch face. You can view this snippet and then do nothing, and it will disappear permanently. Or you can save it to the stand-alone Siri app that shipped with iOS 27 for later access, which might be helpful if you’re trying to remember someone’s name or take a quick note from a fast-paced presentation.
Apple says that users will also be able to tap an “Ask Siri” option that appears below the text snippet if they have questions about something that was said — like a technical term they don’t understand, or perhaps some sauce option the waiter mentioned that they’re unfamiliar with, among other things.
When you double-press the digital crown, an audible chime appears and an animation displays on the watch. This is meant to signal to people through the combination of the gesture, sound, and visual display that you’ve recorded something that was said.
These snippets will be deleted automatically after 30 seconds if you don’t save them.
The other option, Siri Recaps, takes this ambient listening technology even further by allowing Apple Watch owners to take high-level notes of their conversations.
Importantly, Apple says it’s not identifying speakers, saving the raw audio, or providing word-for-word transcriptions. Instead, the feature summarizes a conversation with a short sentence and some bullet points that identify key points. For instance, depending on the topic, the bullet points might include things like important dates or deadlines, pricing or payment plan information, and other factual information.
Apple suggested the feature could be used in scenarios like work meetings, parent-teacher conferences, personal training sessions, or other places where you may need to remember what tasks you have to complete or things that were said. Essentially, it’s supposed to mimic a user’s ability to jot down their own notes — without adding emotion or tone.
This could be helpful particularly at times when you aren’t able to write up your own notes, either because your hands are full or you’re trying to concentrate on whatever’s being said at the time. The recaps won’t record conversations when you’re listening to music with your headphones or using their noise cancellation features.
At the end of the day, you’re prompted to review your saved Siri Recaps when the feature is enabled, and then choose which, if any, you want to save. These notes can be saved to your Notes app or even exported to your Journal app, if they’re more personal in nature. If you do nothing, the recaps will delete themselves within seven days.
This reminder to review the Siri Recaps you’ve recorded also appears when you first unlock your Apple Watch in the morning.
Users can opt to enable the Siri Recaps manually, or they can configure a schedule, such as only having the recapping take place while at work, or making sure that they never run at night. Even if they’re running on a schedule, you can still turn off the recapping feature at any time from the Control Center.
Apple notes that Siri Recaps has been designed to omit sensitive information like government-assigned identifiers, financial and authentication data, certain personal identifiers, and any language that would promote self-harm.
The Audio Intelligence features for the Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4 rely on a combination of Apple’s hardware, software, and services. This includes the S11 chip, built-in microphone, and the Apple Intelligence models that run on the iPhone, as well as Apple’s Private Cloud Compute.
The always-on processor includes a secure enclave — a hardware-protected memory space that can’t be accessed by the rest of the operating system — where a dedicated audio buffer processes the raw audio. This allows the audio to be processed without it actually being accessible to anyone, including Apple. The audio is only accessible to the paired iPhone through a secure pairing mechanism between it and the watch.
To work, lightweight AI models on the S11 chip on the Apple Watch detect if there’s a conversation happening that the wearer is a part of, which is then transferred to the secure enclave on the iPhone. On-device processing on the iPhone turns the audio into condensed text, meaning the text of the conversation with the filler words removed. This condensed text is then encrypted and sent to Apple’s Private Cloud Compute (PCC) to create the recap of the conversation that is sent back to the phone. (The recap and any summarizations used to create it are deleted from PCC immediately afterwards.)
The recaps you can then access on your iPhone are only accessible to you, not to Apple. And they’re encrypted when they’re synced between your multiple Apple devices.
Similarly, Apple Watch transfers audio to the iPhone for processing to create the 15-second snippets for Live Rewind.
Apple says none of these features should impact the battery life of your watch or phone. The features will later roll out to the public beta for broader testing before becoming publicly available to all.
Post updated to indicate that the updates have not yet rolled out in the U.S.

====================================================================================================

Nvidia – open-source auto-ontology
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
07:04:50 UTC
16:04:50 KST

Agents can retrieve documents and query databases, but reliability gates agentic analytics: a revenue metric answered faster is worth nothing if the number is wrong. Agents need a context layer that tells them where data lives, what it means, whether to use it, how to use it, where it came from, and what is similar. NVIDIA Auto Ontology is an open library that builds that layer from enterprise metadata, reconciles definitions across systems, and exposes it to any agent harness through MCP, CLI, and API. It complements the semantic models customers already own.
It enables developers to:
- Discover: generate enterprise ontologies from metadata; connect existing ontologies and semantic models; build AI-ready knowledge graphs.
- Ground: give agents trusted business meaning for metrics, entities, and relationships; reconcile inconsistent definitions across systems.
- Validate: check generated queries against certified definitions and lineage; return provenance with every answer.
- Expose: serve context to any agent harness through MCP, CLI, and API. Learn: fold certified corrections and usage back into the ontology, so shared context improves with every question.
To run a text-to-SQL benchmark such as BIRD, refer to the ontology SQL evaluation repository.
Licensing & contributions. Auto Ontology is distributed under the
Apache License 2.0. Third-party open-source components
bundled, linked, or otherwise used by this project are listed in
THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md. This project
is currently not accepting external contributions.
- Natural-language querying of structured data — questions are translated to SQL and executed against connected databases, powered by NVIDIA NIM.
- Semantic catalog / ontology layer over relational sources, stored in Postgres alongside the catalog it describes.
- Authentication & RBAC —using Better Auth.
- Web UI for chat, analysis, data catalog, analytics, and settings.
- Flexible deployment — Helm chart for Kubernetes or Docker Compose for a local stack.
Auto Ontology uses NVIDIA NIM endpoints for inference — either the hosted
NVIDIA API catalog (integrate.api.nvidia.com) or
self-hosted NIMs. The defaults in .env.example are:
- LLM: nvidia/nemotron-3-nano-30b-a3b
- Embeddings: nvidia/nemotron-3-embed-1b
- Reranking: nvidia/llama-nemotron-rerank-vl-1b-v2
The endpoints and models are configured via the DEFAULT_MODELS_ENDPOINT, DEFAULT_MODELS_MODEL,
EMBED_ENDPOINT, and EMBED_MODEL environment variables and require a
DEFAULT_MODELS_API_KEY.
Each model role is configured by a triplet of environment variables —
<PREFIX>_ENDPOINT (URL), <PREFIX>_API_KEY, and <PREFIX>_MODEL:
You can set a full triplet, only some of its fields, or none at all — any field
left unset falls back to the matching DEFAULT_MODELS_<FIELD>. So the simplest
setup is to fill in DEFAULT_MODELS_* and override per-triplet fields only where
they differ (e.g. EMBED_MODEL, NON_REASONING_MODEL). *_API_KEY also falls
back to the legacy NVIDIA_API_KEY for backward compatibility.
- Docker with Docker Compose v2 (e.g. Docker Desktop on macOS/Windows,
Docker Engine on Linux) for the local stack. For Kubernetes deployments see
DEPLOYMENT.md.
- An NVIDIA API key (nvapi-...) for NVIDIA NIM (chat and ingestion) from
the NVIDIA API catalog at https://build.nvidia.com.
- Connection details for the source database(s) you want to query (Databricks, Postgres, Snowflake, or DuckDB).
Kubernetes
To deploy Auto Ontology on a Kubernetes cluster, see DEPLOYMENT.md.
Local (Docker Compose)
- Clone the repository: git clone <repo-url> auto_ontology && cd auto_ontology
- Create your environment file (.env) from the template and fill in the values
(Postgres credentials, DEFAULT_MODELS_API_KEY, CONNECTION_STRINGS, etc.).
See .env.example for the full list of variables:
cp .env.example .env
# edit .env Note: 
  - AUTH_SECRET and APP_URL are required — without them every page
fails with a Better Auth "default secret" error.
  - POSTGRES_PORT only changes the host-side port mapping (set it if
5432 is already taken on your machine); containers always reach Postgres
on the internal network port.
- Build the images and start the stack: docker compose up -d --build This builds the backend (auto_ontology) and frontend (auto-ontology-frontend) images, brings
up Postgres and pgAdmin, runs the one-shot frontend-migrate job to
sync the database schema, and starts the app (backend, ingestion service,
and frontend).
- Open the UI at http://localhost:3000 (the backend API is on :3001,
pgAdmin on :5050).
Troubleshooting: if the UI loads without the left navigation panel
(or otherwise looks broken after a restart), your browser is holding a
stale session cookie — this happens when AUTH_SECRET changes or the
database is reset. Clear the site data for localhost:3000 (or delete
the better-auth.session_token cookie) and sign in again.
Auto Ontology resolves the source databases it connects to from two sources:
- Added through the UI — connections created from within the app. With this option the connection credentials are stored in plaintext in Postgres unless Vault is configured: 
  - Without Vault: the full connection object (including the password) is JSON-serialized and stored, unencrypted, on the catalog's database row.
  - With Vault: the credentials are written to Vault, keyed by the database name, and the catalog row stores no credentials (the database name is the lookup key — there is no separate reference field).
Vault is enabled only when all of VAULT_ADDR, VAULT_NAMESPACE, VAULT_ROLE_ID, and VAULT_SECRET_ID are set (VAULT_AUTH_MOUNT and VAULT_KV_MOUNT are optional overrides). It is off by default; a partial
configuration is ignored with a warning and falls back to plaintext storage.
- CONNECTION_STRINGS environment variable — a comma-separated list of
connection strings supplied at deploy time (e.g. the Helm --set connectionStrings=<CONNECTION-STRINGS> flag). This is a fallback: it
is used only when there are no UI-added connections.
Auto Ontology Supports SSO for authentication. The Redirect URI should be configured in the IdP as: APP_URL/api/auth/sso/callback
A browser signs in and gets a session cookie, which a script cannot obtain. For scripts, notebooks, and scheduled jobs, mint an API token instead:
- Open the user menu (top right) → API Tokens → New token.
- Name it, optionally pick an expiry (default: never), and copy the token. Auto Ontology stores only a SHA-256 hash of it, so it is shown exactly once. Losing it means minting a new one.
Send it as x-api-key on any /api/... call — Authorization: Bearer <token>
works too:
curl -H "x-api-key: $AUTO_ONTOLOGY_API_TOKEN" https://auto_ontology.example.com/api/termsimport os
import requests
session = requests.Session()
session.headers["x-api-key"] = os.environ["AUTO_ONTOLOGY_API_TOKEN"]
terms = session.get("https://auto_ontology.example.com/api/terms").json()
answer = session.post(
    "https://auto_ontology.example.com/api/chat/completions",
    json={"question": "How many orders shipped last week?"},
).json()
Things worth knowing:
- A token acts as its owner. It carries no permissions of its own — every call is authorized against the owner's role, exactly as it would be in the browser. A viewer's token cannot do admin things.
- The whole API surface accepts it. Authentication is resolved in one place
(frontend/auth/resolve-user.ts) for every route, so any endpoint in docs/openapi/auto-ontology-api.json that is not withPublic works with a token.
- Revocation is immediate. Delete the token in the UI, or delete/ban the owning user, and the next request with it gets a 401.
- Tokens cannot manage tokens. Creating and revoking requires a signed-in session, so a leaked token cannot issue itself successors.
Public clients call the authenticated Next.js gateway at
POST /api/chat/completions. The response is an SSE stream containing step,
result, or error events, followed by [DONE].
conversation_id is optional:
- Omit it for a stateless, one-shot request.
- Supply a client-generated UUID to create or continue a conversation.
- Reuse the same UUID for every turn in the thread. Wait for [DONE] before
sending the next turn; overlapping requests for one conversation return 409 Conversation in progress.
{
  "question": "What about last month?",
  "conversation_id": "d61d8aa3-d496-4fa7-97ce-4f831c162e7f"
}
For conversational requests, FastAPI loads up to the five most recent completed turns (bounded to 12,000 characters) and rewrites contextual input such as “What about August?” into a standalone question before running the existing text-to-SQL graph. Independent questions are passed through unchanged. Internal agent thoughts, chart payloads, errors, and raw SQL result sets are not reused as context.
Conversation history is scoped to the authenticated Auto Ontology user. Browser and
service callers should use the Next.js gateway, which resolves the user and
forwards the internal x-auto-ontology-user-id header to FastAPI. The FastAPI port is a
trusted internal service boundary: it must not be exposed publicly because that
header is not independently verified by FastAPI. A direct FastAPI request
without conversation_id remains stateless; a direct request with
conversation_id requires the trusted identity header.
The web UI manages the UUID automatically. An API-created conversation can be
opened in the UI at /chat?focus=<conversation_id> when it belongs to the
signed-in user.
Coding agents: start at AGENTS.md. Skills live in
skills/ (nvidia-ontology-setup, nvidia-ontology-management, nvidia-ontology-query).
Maintainers keep using CLAUDE.md.
Agent harnesses such as Cursor and Claude Desktop can query your data through
Auto Ontology's MCP server. See
mcp/ to run it and docs/mcp.md for the details.

Nvidia – open-source auto-ontology
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
07:04:50 UTC
16:04:50 KST

Agents can retrieve documents and query databases, but reliability gates agentic analytics: a revenue metric answered faster is worth nothing if the number is wrong. Agents need a context layer that tells them where data lives, what it means, whether to use it, how to use it, where it came from, and what is similar. NVIDIA Auto Ontology is an open library that builds that layer from enterprise metadata, reconciles definitions across systems, and exposes it to any agent harness through MCP, CLI, and API. It complements the semantic models customers already own.
It enables developers to:
- Discover: generate enterprise ontologies from metadata; connect existing ontologies and semantic models; build AI-ready knowledge graphs.
- Ground: give agents trusted business meaning for metrics, entities, and relationships; reconcile inconsistent definitions across systems.
- Validate: check generated queries against certified definitions and lineage; return provenance with every answer.
- Expose: serve context to any agent harness through MCP, CLI, and API. Learn: fold certified corrections and usage back into the ontology, so shared context improves with every question.
To run a text-to-SQL benchmark such as BIRD, refer to the ontology SQL evaluation repository.
Licensing & contributions. Auto Ontology is distributed under the
Apache License 2.0. Third-party open-source components
bundled, linked, or otherwise used by this project are listed in
THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md. This project
is currently not accepting external contributions.
- Natural-language querying of structured data — questions are translated to SQL and executed against connected databases, powered by NVIDIA NIM.
- Semantic catalog / ontology layer over relational sources, stored in Postgres alongside the catalog it describes.
- Authentication & RBAC —using Better Auth.
- Web UI for chat, analysis, data catalog, analytics, and settings.
- Flexible deployment — Helm chart for Kubernetes or Docker Compose for a local stack.
Auto Ontology uses NVIDIA NIM endpoints for inference — either the hosted
NVIDIA API catalog (integrate.api.nvidia.com) or
self-hosted NIMs. The defaults in .env.example are:
- LLM: nvidia/nemotron-3-nano-30b-a3b
- Embeddings: nvidia/nemotron-3-embed-1b
- Reranking: nvidia/llama-nemotron-rerank-vl-1b-v2

China Publicly Discloses Plan For a Lunar Landing Within Three Years
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

China Publicly Discloses Plan For a Lunar Landing Within Three Years (arstechnica.com) 41
			
		 	
				At the Seventh Manned Spaceflight Conference in Beijing, Chinese space officials publicly outlined the most detailed roadmap yet for landing astronauts on the Moon "before 2030." Ars Technica reports:  China's lunar landings will involve a lunar-orbit rendezvous similar to what NASA did in the 1960s and early 1970s with the Apollo program. However, lacking a rocket as powerful as the Saturn V vehicle, the crew and lunar lander will launch separately to the Moon. According to slides shared on social media, the current plan involves:
- An uncrewed test of the Long March 10A rocket (a single-stick variant, with no boosters) and the Mengzhou spacecraft to low-Earth orbit. Target: early 2027
- An uncrewed test of the larger Long March 10 rocket (a core stage with two boosters) and the Lanyue lunar lander, including descent/ascent from Moon's surface. Target: 2028
- A concurrent launch of two Long March 10 rockets, one carrying uncrewed Mengzhou and the other the Lanyue vehicle; they will dock in lunar orbit. Target: 2029
- Dual launch of Long March 10 rockets, this time with crew on the Mengzhou spacecraft, leading to crewed landing mission. Target: "Before 2030"
The public release of this new information provides a timeline for gauging China's technical progress in its efforts to land humans on the Moon before 2030 and beat NASA and the United States back to the lunar surface. Notably, the plan (for now, at least) omits a mission similar to Apollo 8 or Artemis II, which involved a crew flying out to the Moon and either entering orbit or passing close by before returning to Earth. The Mengzhou vehicle should still get a workout closer to Earth with humans on board as part of missions carrying astronauts to Tiangong, China's space station in low-Earth orbit.

Google takes Dreambeans to India in bid to bring AI-generated personal stories to the masses
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

Google has launched its experimental AI app Dreambeans in India, its first market outside the U.S., as the company looks to tap the world’s second-largest internet market to bring personalized visual stories drawn from users’ digital activity to a broader audience.
On Monday, Google expanded Dreambeans, available on Android and iOS, to India with support for 13 local languages, including Hindi, Punjabi, Tamil, Telugu, and Bengali, alongside English. The launch comes less than two months after Google made the app available to all eligible U.S. users aged 18 and older.
Introduced in June, Google’s Dreambeans uses information from connected Google services, including Calendar, Gemini, Gmail, Photos, Search, and YouTube, to proactively generate personalized, illustrated stories about users’ interests and everyday lives, without requiring them to enter a prompt. The app initially featured static, AI-generated illustrations, but Google recently added a feature that uses its Veo video-generation model to even animate images in select stories.
Google said it chose India as Dreambeans’ first international market because of strong interest from Indian users seeking access to the app and the widespread use of its services in the country. India stood out for its high overlap of users across Gemini, Gmail, and Google Photos, Google product manager Gozde Oznur said during a press briefing.
Dreambeans has recorded nearly 55,000 downloads in the U.S. since its June debut, according to estimates from market intelligence firm Sensor Tower shared with TechCrunch. About 63% of those downloads came in September, when Google opened the app to all eligible U.S. users aged 18 and older. However, downloads dropped 84% month over month to about 5,500 in October.
In the first 30 days after Google opened Dreambeans to all eligible U.S. users, the app recorded an estimated 29,000 downloads, compared with 7.4 million for Meta’s Muse, 2.8 million for ChatGPT, 362,000 for Grok Bot, and 155,000 for Claude during their respective first 30 days after launch, Sensor Tower estimates show. However, unlike those general-purpose AI apps, Dreambeans focuses on generating personalized stories instead of responding to a broad range of user queries.
Even though Dreambeans supports many widely spoken Indian languages, users can select only one language at a time for their generated stories. The app can draw on information across Google services in multiple languages, but it cannot currently generate stories that mix languages, Oznur said during the briefing.
That could limit Dreambeans’ appeal in India, where many people regularly switch between languages, including Hindi and English, often mixing the two in what is commonly known as Hinglish.
Dreambeans’ dependence on information from users’ Google accounts also raises questions about privacy and data access. The app uses Google’s Personal Intelligence technology to draw on connected services and personalize its AI-generated stories. Those services can contain sensitive personal information that some users may not want to share with an AI app just to receive personalized content.
Users can choose which Google services to connect to Dreambeans and disconnect them at any time, Oznur said. They can also delete their Dreambeans data from within the app. Moreover, Oznur asserted that information deleted from the underlying connected services is also removed from Dreambeans’ personalization system.
Users, Oznur said, can tell Dreambeans to avoid certain subjects, including business-related information, and the app will try to adjust its stories accordingly. She added that Dreambeans is available only to personal Gmail account holders, not enterprise users, and that generated stories are visible only to the account holder.
Dreambeans’ proactive approach also carries the risk of surfacing memories that users may find upsetting. Oznur acknowledged that Dreambeans could generate stories involving people who have died, for instance. She said users can tell the app to stop showing certain subjects, comparing the feature to controls in Google Photos that let users limit which memories appear.
Oznur told reporters that Google is exploring ways to make Dreambeans more actionable, moving beyond personalized stories and recommendations to help users act on the app’s suggestions.

BYD Sold More EVs Last Month Than the US Did All Quarter
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

BYD Sold More EVs Last Month Than the US Did All Quarter (electrek.co) 156
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from Electrek: The world's leading EV maker is showing no signs of slowing down. BYD sold 490,168 new energy vehicles (NEVs) in October, up 11% from the prior year and the company's highest monthly total in 2026. BYD's battery electric vehicles drove the growth, with a record 305,743 units sold, up 37% year over year and marking the first time its EV sales have crossed the 300,000 mark. To put that into perspective, just over 262,500 EVs were sold in the US in the entire third quarter combined, according to Kelley Blue Book estimates. By the end of the year, BYD expects overseas shipments to reach about 2 million vehicles. "In 2027, that number is expected to rise to more than 2.5 million vehicles," adds Electrek.

Median net worth of U.S. households under 35 fell 23% from 2022 to 2025 (real)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
03:28:26 UTC
12:28:26 KST

Net worth for people aged 30–33 in the U.S.
Total assets minus total debts — bank accounts, investments, retirement accounts and home equity, less mortgages, loans and cards.
Half of people aged 30–33 in the U.S. have a net worth above $68,000. Reaching the upper quarter takes about $235,000, and the top 10% starts at $440,000 — roughly 6.5× the median. The lower quarter sits below $7,100, and the bottom 10% is negative — more owed than owned.
The full distribution
Get this cohort's numbers when they move.

You'll get the current figures right away, then monthly. Keeping Up Inc, New York. Privacy.

Rivian spinoff Also wants to reinvent the wheel — but first, it has to ship
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
03:24:56 UTC
12:24:56 KST

When Rivian quietly spun off its micromobility brand, Also, a lot of observers wondered if the luxury EV maker had bitten off more than it can chew. Chris Yu insists that’s not the case.
Rivian spinoff Also wants to reinvent the wheel — but first, it has to ship
Also President Chris Yu opens up about surviving the AI server component crunch, rewriting UL battery standards, and why the company’s modular e-bike is just the first step.
Rivian spinoff Also wants to reinvent the wheel — but first, it has to ship
Also President Chris Yu opens up about surviving the AI server component crunch, rewriting UL battery standards, and why the company’s modular e-bike is just the first step.
Yu, a Stanford aeronautics PhD and former CTO at Specialized, runs the startup as an independent business that counts Rivian as a minority shareholder. Under his lead, Also rolled out the TM-B — a quirky, modular e-bike with a raft of unique features, including a swappable top frame, USB-C chargeable battery, a built-in screen, a series hybrid pedal-by-wire system, and proprietary “Dream Ride” software. There’s also the TM-Q, an electric four-wheeler aimed at commercial deliveries.
But it hasn’t been completely smooth sailing. After dealing with supply chain headaches, confusing regulations, and some communication stumbles, Also is finally shipping its early Launch Edition bikes to customers while also getting ready to roll out the standard and performance versions. Meanwhile, customers have been letting the company have it on Reddit over the delayed deliveries, and the few people who have received their bikes have already experienced some minor software problems.
The Verge met up with Yu to chat about scaling production, surviving parts shortages, dealing with the mess of US e-bike laws, and why the company is betting big on autonomous delivery.
This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity.
What is the current status of the bike and production ramp?
On the bike, the main thing is the production ramp. It’s one thing to create a product as a traditional OE where you work with several Tier 1 suppliers, get them to deliver on time, and assemble it. With us, we’ve built and designed everything down to individual components and ICs. Over the last year, every imaginable supply chain learning has come across.
We’ve been in the trenches competing with AI server demands for components like ICs, compute, memory, and raw PCBA [printed circuit board assembly] materials. Our inverter board is designed entirely in-house for high performance, and the thermals require a sophisticated board material. It turns out AI server farms have similar thermal requirements, triggering a global shortage, so we had to requalify and resource. But all that is behind us, and we are really starting to flow to customers and ramp up. A huge draw to come to Also was shipping a product that people will enjoy and use on the road every day, and reaching that milestone now has been huge for the team.
How many bikes have you delivered so far? Are you still in the Founder’s Edition phase?
That’s right. We’re quickly ramping up. We have a substantial number out there now, and you can see a lot of volume and traffic online of people taking their first rides. Deliveries are quickly increasing along the classic manufacturing ramp where you start steadily and then add volume.
In hindsight, was targeting a spring launch too optimistic?
At the time, based on everything we knew, spring was very much on plan. But as with Rivian on the automotive side or us earlier this year, a lot of unknown unknowns happened. Certainly I didn’t foresee the intense urgency around AI server hardware, nor the fact that those supply chains are so shared with light electric vehicles.
“Certainly I didn’t foresee the intense urgency around AI server hardware.”
— Chris Yu, president of Also
Was there direct overlap between Also’s supply chain and Rivian’s?
I look at it as a three-way Venn diagram between us — vertically integrated small EVs — and Rivian — full-size automotive EVs — and broader AI hardware like robotics and servers. There is a healthy overlap right in the middle, alongside direct overlap between us and Rivian. Where we overlap with Rivian there is a clear benefit, but it wasn’t easy for Rivian either.
[Rivian CEO] RJ [Scaringe] and I talk all the time. Half the time we’re discussing how to handle a specific part or supply chain constraint. You either fight for the part and lean on suppliers, find an alternative supplier with the identical component, or — as both we and Rivian have had to do — completely redesign and requalify the system around a replacement part.
Can you get specific on what components had to be redesigned or requalified?
It sounds relatively boring on paper, but it’s critical. One example was IC components like high-performance capacitors. Swapping them requires the work of going through full validation testing to make sure the alternative hits our durability and lifespan standards, because those components dictate the reliability of PCBAs.
Another was raw PCBA board material. Only certain base materials meet our flammability, thermal cycle, and structural rigidity requirements. When supplies ran short, we had to qualify third and fourth alternatives. There is no parallelization of verification testing — components must spend weeks inside thermal chambers and on shock and vibration rigs to prove they can handle real-world conditions.
In a letter to customers, [head of product] Saul Leiken mentioned there were regulatory hurdles around power output as well. What happened with UL certification?
It wasn’t so much an impediment as proof of how nascent the regulatory space is relative to what we’re building. Our battery combines several uncommon attributes: exceptionally high power density, an automotive-style software-defined battery management system with two-way flow for regenerative braking, USB-C 3.1 charging at up to 240 watts, and a removable pack.
Those things combined don’t exist in standard e-bike battery guidelines. Certification bodies like UL write standards assuming a typical e-bike pack without USB data connections or such high bidirectional power throughput. When we presented our architecture, we identified undefined areas in the certification rules. Rather than treating it as a gray area and self-certifying, we partnered directly with UL to develop a clarification standard tailored to this type of battery architecture. We went back and forth, completed unit testing on both sides, agreed on the protocol, and we are now fully UL certified.
Do you anticipate any thermal runaway problems, given how battery fires have plagued the e-bike sector?
With any lithium-ion battery, you can never say never — whether you’re Tesla, Rivian, or an e-bike maker. Even from top-tier cell manufacturers like LG, Samsung, or Panasonic, there is an inherent cell fallout rate. Across thousands of vehicles and millions of cells, math dictates a non-zero number. That part is inescapable basic physics.
Where we are super confident is our pack architecture. We took an exceptionally robust and conservative approach from both a mechanical and software protection standpoint, incorporating passive and active propagation defenses learned from generations of automotive EV battery design. The cell architecture, array welding, and management systems all benefit directly from that engineering.
When do you anticipate starting deliveries of the Standard and Performance trims?
Super soon. We have started opening reservation conversions, and anticipation for the Performance trim has been huge. We are focused on fulfilling orders for the supporters who lined up first and waited the longest, and the Performance line will follow right around the corner.
Early reservation holders on Reddit voiced frustration about communication gaps during the delays. What did you learn from that?
I forget sometimes how much of a blessing it is for customers to care that much. It’s easy to make a product where nobody cares and there are zero Reddit comments. The responsibility is on us to be transparent. Our team’s default mentality was heads-down execution: chase suppliers, run engineering, and pass tests. We didn’t come up for air to keep everyone posted.
That was a mistake on our part — not providing regular, transparent updates for the cohort that backed us early. We’ve changed that over the last couple of months, establishing regular communication with our preorder holders about what’s happening in production and what’s coming next.
“That was a mistake on our part — not providing regular, transparent updates for the cohort that backed us early.”
Did the delays trigger many order cancellations?
Very few. Most people backed the project sight unseen, and since then almost everyone has attended a test event to try it out, which led them to double down. The overwhelming sentiment has just been: “I want it. When is it coming?”
Between pedal-by-wire, swappable frames, and custom software, did you overcomplicate the bike when an off-the-shelf design could have shipped much faster?
I don’t look at it that way at all. If we were to revisit the design, I would do the exact same thing. The largest untapped opportunity isn’t cyclists who already tolerate the current bike experience. It’s the massive demographic of people intrigued by leaving the car behind who haven’t opted in yet. Their hesitations are consistent: fear of theft, avoiding maintenance every two months, wanting seamless navigation and audio, and needing to carry a child without having to buy three different bikes.
Solving those pain points requires a baseline of technology. Just like in automotive EVs, once you use software to personalize the ride and eliminate friction, people opt in. The majority of people attending our test rides haven’t ridden a bicycle in 10 or 15 years. Our goal wasn’t to go overboard; it was determining the minimum amount of innovation needed to create an experience as frictionless as stepping into a Tesla Model Y or Rivian R2.
With cities and states cracking down on overpowered bikes and mopeds, how does Also fit into the shifting regulatory landscape?
We are well-positioned by design to navigate a patchwork regulatory system. Current backlash is driven by bad actors selling products labeled as e-bikes that are actually mini-motorcycles, or bikes where clipping a wire uncaps speeds to 45 mph without vehicle safety standards.
The primary issue is a lack of regulatory resolution between genuine Class 1, 2, and 3 e-bikes and unregulated vehicles, leading to blunt blanket bans like we’ve seen in New Jersey. Our philosophy is that the opportunity within legitimate Class 1, 2, and 3 categories is massive, and we remain definitively within that framework.
We engineered the bike with immediate low-end torque so accelerating to 20 or 28 mph is responsive and fun without needing dangerous motorcycle speeds. Furthermore, because the bike is software-defined, the identical hardware can be configured to local rules — whether that’s 20 mph, 28 mph, or European 25 km/h limits. We are also introducing over-the-air parental controls so families can set conservative speed limits for younger riders, which is the exact opposite of the cut-the-wire approach.
Were you concerned about delicate electronics or firmware falling out of calibration during shipping?
Physical package handling was an early challenge. FedEx did not handle the initial deliveries well, so we paused and switched carriers, which resolved the issue.
On firmware and calibration, it is a solved problem. Nobody picks up an electric car wondering if the drive-by-wire calibration got jostled in transit. Mechanically, the bike has very few moving parts. The drivetrain is fully sealed and pedal-by-wire, so traditional failure points like exposed derailleurs and shifters simply don’t exist. The down-tube computer, display unit, and hand controls are robust, solid-state systems rather than fragile instruments.
“FedEx did not handle the initial deliveries well, so we paused and switched carriers, which resolved the issue.”
What is the out-of-the-box assembly process like for non-cyclists?
Very minimal. I pushed the team to make it feel as turnkey as unboxing an Apple Watch. You release a few clips, slide off the outer carton, and find three core elements: the main frame, the top frame, and the cockpit. The cockpit handlebar contains all integrated controls and wiring with a blind internal connector.
Assembly entails mounting the front wheel, powering on the bike from shipping mode, connecting the mobile app, snapping on the modular top frame, and securing the stem base plate with two bolts. Threading the pedals is the final step, which is guided by short 20-second video walkthroughs in the app alongside a provided shock pump to set suspension pressure based on rider weight.
How will service and repairs work for riders who don’t live near an Also brand hub?
Many issues can be resolved over the air. When physical service is required, our onboard diagnostics pinpoint the exact issue immediately through data logs rather than requiring guesswork at a shop. If hardware replacement is necessary, we can dispatch parts proactively.
Our repair network combines our own facilities with authorized local bike shops. We currently have roughly 150 vetted partners, expanding beyond 200 soon. We provide these shops with complete technical documentation, dedicated software tools, and direct parts inventory. Because every component board is digitally serialized to the bike for anti-theft security, authorized partners use our proprietary software to link new parts directly to the frame.
Micromobility saw several high-profile company collapses after the pandemic boom. How does Also protect itself from those pitfalls?
We didn’t start Also solely to be an e-bike company. The bike is our flagship and our handshake with the world, but our broader mission is building purpose-built small vehicles. The vast majority of urban trips are short, low-payload journeys — moving a single passenger or delivering takeout. Designing an urban transportation network from scratch would never rely exclusively on multi-ton cars.
The bike serves as the architectural foundation for a broader vehicle roadmap that is software-defined, vertically integrated, and autonomy-first. Following the bike launch, we are heavily focused on autonomous delivery. Earlier this year, DoorDash joined as a strategic partner to develop and deploy autonomous delivery platforms. A light vehicle platform that carries payload across town at 28 mph offers significant cost-per-mile efficiencies, and our autonomous platform shares direct architectural commonality with that system.
Will that DoorDash collaboration result in a low-speed sidewalk robot?
Sidewalk robots represent a completely different product class — closer to an industrial automated robot than an L4 autonomous road vehicle. By nature, sidewalk robots travel at 1 to 2 mph. While that works within localized, high-density environments like university campuses, purpose-built light electric vehicles operate at vehicle speeds across entire metro areas.
I’m sure you’re aware there is some excitement going on around low-speed vehicles like golf carts.
One hundred percent. And it does feel like this kind of fits into that. There’s been a ton of interest there. And that’s one of the more common questions I get is like when can we get one of these. But I think what you just said is actually – that’s the point that I want to focus on, which is – It’s less about the specific form factor of like a TM-Q or otherwise. It’s more the notion that there’s this huge gap and a huge demand for people to say, hey, the average household has something like two point something vehicles. And nearly everyone agrees that you don’t need two Escalades. You need like one big capable vehicle. And we’ve seen that a lot of people have started picking up things like golf carts and LSVs. And I would argue that it’s in spite of the deficiencies in the category.
Because if you just think big picture, the problem that we’re trying to solve collectively is how do I have something that costs much, much less than an average-priced $50,000 car today? But at the same time, why does that have to be a crappy experience? I want that to be a premium experience and potentially be able to be L4 autonomous as well. And so for us, that’s a huge opportunity. So let’s take the notion of small purpose-built for getting around and doing the everyday trips that everyone does, but have it feel like a magical experience that potentially could be fully autonomous as well. That is a super interesting opportunity.

Own Your HackerTube
Quality input, filtered by AI and tuned to your taste. Follow YouTube channels, write your standards, and an AI picks only the videos worth your time, then learns from what you like and don't, all on your own YouTube-style site.
19 registered users Build your own HackerTube.
How it works
Say what you care about
Write your standards in plain words and follow the YouTube channels you trust. Videos you add yourself, with a reason, teach it your taste.
The AI watches, so you don't have to
Every day it checks new videos from your channels and scores them against your standards. Each pick comes with why it is worth your time and the key moments to jump to.
Your feedback trains it
Picks go live as soon as they pass the filter. You can still tell it which videos you liked and which you did not, and why. The AI remembers your judgment and adds it to its criteria, so over time you get quality plus your own taste.
Not another AI summary
Summaries try to replace watching. Filtering decides what is worth watching in the first place.
Better input, not a shorter summary.
Most AI video tools summarize a video so you never watch it. This one filters instead: it checks every new video against your standards, so what reaches you is worth your time. You still watch it yourself, and remember it.

- • AI picks are labelled as AI; your own words stay yours
- • Tell it what you like and don't, and why; the AI remembers, and you can change your standards any time

A YouTube-style site
Home grid, watch page, search, and Liked, History and Watch later kept in the reader's own browser.
Key moments
Jump straight to the part worth watching, using the creator's own chapters.

No prompts to write and no tasks to schedule. New videos from your channels are judged once a day.
It learns your taste
Mark a pick as great, or as not a fit and say why. The AI remembers your judgment and uses it in every future check.
Your own address
Your site lives at ownhackertube.cc/s/yourname, is readable by search engines, and has an RSS feed.

Free to start, no card needed. Your first 14 days give you 3 channels and 60 AI checks a month; after that the Free plan keeps 1 channel and 30 checks. Pro follows up to 20 channels.
Who it's for
Anyone who learns on purpose and guards their attention
In the AI era, good output starts with good input. And a lot of what gets written with AI these days is, frankly, filler. If you care what goes into your head, let a filter that knows your standards do the first pass.
Founders and builders
Keep up with the interviews and talks that matter in your field without scrubbing through hours of footage.

Questions
Is it free?+
Yes. Your first 14 days include 3 followed channels and 60 AI checks a month. After that the free plan keeps your site and everything on it, with 1 followed channel, 30 AI checks a month and up to 50 videos. Pro follows up to 20 channels with 600 checks a month.
Does the AI summarize the videos?+
No. It decides whether a video is worth your time and tells you why in a sentence or two, then points you to the key moments. You still watch the video.
Where do the key moments come from?+
From the chapters the creator wrote in the video description. The AI only chooses among them, so a timestamp is never invented. Videos without chapters still get a pick and a reason, just no key moments.
Which videos and channels work?+
YouTube videos and channels, followed automatically every day. Bilibili is supported too (beta, still being tested): add a video link, or sync an uploader with one click from your own browser, because Bilibili only shows video lists to real browsers. Videos play in each platform's own player and stay there.
What if the AI picks something I don't want?+
Mark it as not a fit and pick a reason, such as too basic, off topic or clickbait. It leaves your site, and the AI remembers your judgment for future picks. You can also rewrite your standards at any time.
Do my readers need an account?+
No. Anyone can open your site. Their Liked, History and Watch later lists stay in their own browser.
How does it decide what is worth watching?+
You describe what you care about in plain words. The AI scores each new video from 0 to 10 against that and publishes the ones that score 7 or more.
Can I use my own domain?+
Custom domains are rolling out for Pro. Until then your site lives at ownhackertube.cc/s/yourname.

Show HN: Rustable is an open source database client built in Rust

Is it written in Rust?
Of course it is. We also use mise, and mise is written in Rust. That’s how you know it’s good. Please don’t ask any follow-up questions about this methodology.
It’s Rust, compiled to WASM, running in your browser. Not a JavaScript rewrite. Just because we can. It’s Rust, dude. Try it.
The actual Rustable workspace · real SQLite + an in-memory Redis demo · disposable sample data. Use Connections (the plug icon) to switch between Studio demo and Redis demo. The Redis demo runs in Rust/WASM and supports a subset of Redis commands; run INFO for the list. Browse, query, edit, import, export. Reload resets both demos; saved queries stay in this browser. Open SQLite files as copies with ⌘O / Ctrl+O. Your originals stay untouched. Server connections need the desktop app.
Yes, we support your silly OS as well. Windows and Linux users, you’re welcome.
v0.4.9 · Early software. macOS is signed and notarized. Release notes & checksums ↗
Built for people who want to get their work done. Not clowns. 🤡 (Clowns may build from source.)
Five databases. One familiar workspace.
SQL databases: browse, query, import, export, and review row edits before saving. MySQL 8.4 and MariaDB 11.4 are tested. Standalone Redis 7.4 is now supported: key browsing, commands, string edits and TTL controls. Real Redis server connections are desktop-only; Cluster and Sentinel are not included.
Support details & current limits ↗
The app code, yes. Even GitHub says so. Check the sauce for yourself ↗
Because PostgreSQL is the only correct choice for a server-side SQL database, and SQLite is obviously, objectively awesome. These are completely unbiased engineering conclusions.
MySQL and MariaDB are now available alongside PostgreSQL and SQLite. Standalone Redis is now available too. We can have opinions and still support your toy databases.
Other database apps are built for pros. Rustable is built for bros. Same SQL. Less ceremony.
Every line, by skilled artisans. Our artisan is called Astra and works suspiciously quickly.
Fine. It’s vibe coded. You can read the source.
Obviously, it's written in Rust and uses mise and egui
Native Windows and Linux builds, too. Yes, actual executables for your inferior operating system. Unlike certain database explorers that cost money and are closed sauce. Very generous of us.
Yes. Download v0.4.9 above. This is early software; we’re using it, finding rough edges, and working on it.
macOS: Apple Silicon, with automatic updates. Windows and Linux: x64 portable packages, manual updates for now. Linux needs Ubuntu 24.04 or compatible newer libraries. Built-in SSH tunnels are currently macOS/Linux only; Windows can use a direct connection or an external tunnel.
Having issues with the app? Sounds like a PICNIC problem.

Open-Source 5G Ran Platforms: A Dual Perspective on Performance and Capabilities
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
02:58:44 UTC
11:58:44 KST

Computer Science > Networking and Internet Architecture
  [Submitted on 26 Aug 2026]
Title:Open-Source 5G RAN Platforms: A Dual Perspective on Performance and Capabilities
View PDF
            Abstract:Open-source implementations of the fifth generation (5G) Radio Access Network (RAN), such as OpenAirInterface (OAI) and srsRAN, have become increasingly relevant for industry and academic research, rapid prototyping, and low-cost private 5G deployments. The state of the art presents different evaluation scenarios for both platforms; however, these are often limited to individual testbed evaluations, end-to-end studies, or partial comparisons between them. In particular, there is a lack of evaluations considering different Software-Defined Radios (SDRs), RAN configurations, user applications, and numbers of real UEs. To address this gap, this paper presents a comparative evaluation of OAI and srsRAN. The study includes a qualitative assessment of supported features and deployment flexibility, followed by a quantitative performance analysis covering radio resource control setup procedures, compliance with theoretical data rates, and real application workloads with diverse quality of service requirements. Specifically, we consider Video on Demand (VoD) for data rate evaluation, Live Streaming (LS) and Cloud Gaming (CG) as latency-sensitive applications. These workloads provide a comprehensive view of each platform capability.

Open-Source 5G Ran Platforms: A Dual Perspective on Performance and Capabilities
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
02:58:44 UTC
11:58:44 KST

Computer Science > Networking and Internet Architecture
  [Submitted on 26 Aug 2026]
Title:Open-Source 5G RAN Platforms: A Dual Perspective on Performance and Capabilities
View PDF
            Abstract:Open-source implementations of the fifth generation (5G) Radio Access Network (RAN), such as OpenAirInterface (OAI) and srsRAN, have become increasingly relevant for industry and academic research, rapid prototyping, and low-cost private 5G deployments. The state of the art presents different evaluation scenarios for both platforms; however, these are often limited to individual testbed evaluations, end-to-end studies, or partial comparisons between them. In particular, there is a lack of evaluations considering different Software-Defined Radios (SDRs), RAN configurations, user applications, and numbers of real UEs. To address this gap, this paper presents a comparative evaluation of OAI and srsRAN. The study includes a qualitative assessment of supported features and deployment flexibility, followed by a quantitative performance analysis covering radio resource control setup procedures, compliance with theoretical data rates, and real application workloads with diverse quality of service requirements. Specifically, we consider Video on Demand (VoD) for data rate evaluation, Live Streaming (LS) and Cloud Gaming (CG) as latency-sensitive applications. These workloads provide a comprehensive view of each platform capability.
