The 2026 iPad Mini is a terrific tiny tablet — for a big price
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
02:55:45 UTC
11:55:45 KST

The iPad Mini sometimes feels like a trap. It is, in some ways and for some people, the perfect iPad: meaningfully larger than my phone’s screen but still small and light enough to use for reading in bed or gaming on the subway. As most of the rest of the iPad line has tilted ever closer to laptop territory, the Mini might be the most iPad-y iPad left. I’ve taken a Mini everywhere with me since the very first one. But Apple only updates the Mini sporadically, and those updates sometimes feel like someone inside Apple Park picked its specs by closing their eyes and clicking randomly in an Excel sheet. The Mini is always a little underpowered, a little overpriced, and a little afield in Apple’s tablet lineup. We, the iPad Mini People, sometimes feel a little forgotten.
The 2026 iPad Mini is a terrific tiny tablet — for a big price
With better performance and a better screen, this is a Mini to be reckoned with. But it’s not perfect, and it sure isn’t cheap.
The 2026 iPad Mini is a terrific tiny tablet — for a big price
With better performance and a better screen, this is a Mini to be reckoned with. But it’s not perfect, and it sure isn’t cheap.
This year’s model, though, is an upgrade through and through. Apple made the screen larger and better, while making the iPad itself smaller and lighter. It upgraded the processor in a big way. It made the whole package more durable, so you can take it to more places and use it for more things. It actually feels like a tablet made for a reason, not just another occasional bone thrown to the small-tablet fans.
For the past week, I’ve been testing the midnight iPad Mini with cell service, 512GB of storage, and the nano-texture matte display coating. I really like it. It’s the best iPad Mini yet, and a huge leap over the previous model. The one in my hands also costs $1,249, and I cannot in good conscience tell you to buy it. Even the very cheapest version — Wi-Fi only, 128GB of storage, regular OLED display — starts at $699, by a wide margin the highest starting price ever for a Mini.
If you don’t already know the iPad Mini is your one true love, there’s probably a better iPad out there for you. But if you’re an iPad Mini Person and you can stomach the price? You’re getting the best Mini in years.
iPad Mini (2026)
The Good
- OLED display looks great
- Waterproof and dustproof
- A20 Pro chip is a big bump up
The Bad
- $$$$$
- No 120Hz display
- Still no FaceID
The Mini’s new display is its most important and most controversial upgrade. It now measures 8.5 inches instead of 8.3, and it’s now a 2316 x 1512 OLED panel instead of an LCD. The OLED gives the screen a little more dynamic range than the previous model, with brighter bright spots and darker dark spots. Its peak brightness is noticeably brighter than before, and its minimum brightness is low enough to be totally pleasant for reading in the dark (as long as you’re in dark mode). In general, it’s just a little more crisp and contrast-y, in a nice way; the old Mini’s display was the least of my worries, but the OLED is a solid improvement. The tablet I tested also has Apple’s nano-texture matte finish, which seems to dull and darken the screen slightly; on a particularly white background, the whole thing seems to shimmer. I suspect I’d like the OLED panel even more without it.
The nano-texture does, however, do an excellent job of reducing glare. The effect is actually really cool: The textured coating doesn’t extend to the bezels, so you can see a source of light or glare come in from the edge of the screen and then simply disappear as it hits the screen. So what you lose in pure screen fidelity, you gain in the ability to read on the iPad in a coffee shop without it reflecting the overhead lights like a mirror. The nano-texture option costs an extra $100, though, and requires you to upgrade to 256GB of storage, so you’re now looking at a $900 tablet. Spend the extra money on the cellular version or, better yet, don’t spend it at all.
Refresh rates matter!
For some reason, Apple has never deigned to put its ProMotion display tech on the iPad Mini, which has a refresh rate capped at 60Hz rather than the 120Hz you’ll get on most other recent Apple devices. This is such an odd spec choice that it has launched a bunch of conspiracy theories. Maybe it’s to make more people buy an iPhone Duo instead? Maybe it’s a 120Hz panel downgraded to 60Hz by software just to make people upgrade again next time? Apple’s answer, to Chris Lawley and others, has been something to the effect of “120Hz is irrelevant, OLED’s faster pixel response solves the problems.” I don’t agree. I don’t think the lack of ProMotion is an immediate and total dealbreaker, but refresh rates matter! To games and high-touch creative apps, of course, but you notice the difference even with basic swiping and zooming. The Apple Pencil feels more juddery moving across my screen. On a 120Hz screen, I can scroll a browser page and still clearly read the text as it’s moving. With 60Hz, it gets blurry. In all these cases, even if you don’t explicitly see the difference, you really do feel it.
The most meaningful upgrade in the new Mini, at least for me and how I use my iPad, is that it is designed to be more or less waterproof and dustproof. Its IP67 rating means that the Mini got the highest possible score for protection from “solid foreign objects,” which for our purposes mostly just means dust won’t get inside, and is also “protected against the effects of temporary immersion in water.” It’s not quite as rugged as, say, the iPhone 18 Pro, and I don’t recommend using it as an underwater camera or anything, but your tablet should be fine as long as you don’t drop it in the tub and leave it for a while.
This increased ruggedness immediately changed my iPad usage in a couple of ways. I became less worried about handing the Mini to my toddler, whose general stickiness and penchant for spilling things no longer feel like threats. I also started using the Mini more in the kitchen — I love to cook with an iPad nearby for recipes and Community episodes, and it was nice not to be constantly guarding against flour explosions and water spills. At one point, the iPad Mini even came with me in the shower during a particularly close football game. It’s still kicking.
This is, for lack of a better word, the Field iPad
The Mini’s IP rating is also the feature that finally made me see who Apple sees as the ideal iPad Mini user. This is, for lack of a better word, the Field iPad. We hear all the time about pilots who use Minis instead of sheaves of paperwork, but Apple also sees them in use on construction sites, oil rigs, doctor’s offices, and all kinds of other places. (It’s a fun rabbit hole, actually — can I interest you in an “explosion proof” last-gen Mini for $4,500 or so?) Apple’s goal with the Mini is not really to maximize performance, satisfy the pixel-peepers, or enhance creator workflows. It’s durability and usability. Body and screen.
Apple made a couple of changes to the Mini to receive its IP rating, like ditching the speaker holes and opting for what it calls an Acoustic Display. It means the sound fires out of the front panel rather than from speaker holes. (Some TVs currently work the same way.) It doesn’t sound any better than before, maybe a tiny bit bassier, but it also doesn’t sound worse, and if anything it’s slightly better to have the sound actually pointed at you instead of out the sides of the tablet. Mostly it’s a wash in service of the ruggedization, which is good enough for me.
The rest of the hardware is just wins all the way around. The A20 Pro chip, the chipset also on the iPhone 18 Pro (and the Duo), benchmarks about 50 percent faster than the 2024 Mini for standard computing tasks, and about three times as fast for graphics-intensive ones. (The chip in my review unit has one more CPU core than the A20 Pro in the cheaper Minis; those will still be drastic improvements, but maybe a hair less so.) I notice the difference, even side by side with the old model, in how fast apps load and games play. Games like Assassin’s Creed Mirage, which were all but unplayable on the 2024 Mini, work nicely on this one. The body is about a tenth of an inch thinner and about an ounce and a half lighter. Perhaps as a result, the battery is a tiny bit smaller, but battery life remains steadfastly somewhere around nine hours of screen time.
One last hardware thing I should mention: Apple moved the front-facing camera from the portrait side of the Mini to the landscape side. On every other iPad, all of which seem to want to be used horizontally, that’s clearly the right decision. On this one, the only iPad I actually hold vertically for long periods, I’m torn. And in either case, that camera still doesn’t enable FaceID — it’s still TouchID or nothing on the Mini.
I thought the last iPad Mini was a disappointment, a product made from spare parts without any particular reason for existence. This year’s Mini feels mostly like a return to form. It’s powerful enough to be useful for a long time. It’s lighter and thinner than ever. The 60Hz refresh rate is a bummer, but I don’t think it’s a dealbreaker for most people. And it’s really the only downside. Though at this price, should you have to deal with any downsides?
This year’s Mini feels mostly like a return to form
After a run of new models and price increases from Apple, it’s worth level-setting a bit on iPad prices. The cheapest model you can buy is now the base iPad at $449, with an A16 chip (the one from the iPhone 14 Pro) and 128GB of storage. The cheapest iPad Air, with an M4 processor and 128GB of storage, is $749. The Pro has an M5 and 256GB for $1,199. iPads are expensive!
In this context, the new Mini’s $699 starting price feels at least $100 too high — this model has always been more expensive than the base iPad, but unless you really want or need the small screen, $50 more gets you a much better and more versatile iPad Air. As ever, the iPad Mini is really only for iPad Mini People (and pilots and oil riggers, apparently). Someday, Apple will give us the tiny flagship we’ve been waiting for, and I’ll gladly pay Apple’s top dollar for it. This is a very good iPad Mini, but it’s not quite the Mini in its final form.

====================================================================================================

A November 3 critique argues that the reported Qwen math gains relied on Python code and repeated model runs, unlike raw-weight-only tests on much smaller networks. It credits the engineering result while disputing the implication that a single technique explains all the experiments.
An MIT CSAIL team reports that Astra improved Qwen’s math scores by editing weights using Python and feedback from practice runs. In the test allowing 50 forward passes, the authors report MATH-500 rising from 7.9% to 48.1%. These preliminary results are separate from the team’s raw-weight-only experiments on smaller networks.

The linked source was unavailable during retrieval. This entry retains the original Hacker News title; the underlying claim has not been verified from the article.
Subvocal’s founders say their prototype glasses use radio-frequency sensing to decode silently mouthed words. In their Hacker News introduction, they describe ongoing development and a planned first shipment in early 2027.
Researcher veritas describes finding alarming function names and embedded prompts in a browser-fingerprinting script, apparently designed to trigger AI safety refusals during analysis. The October 28 post presents this as an obfuscation tactic, not evidence that the script creates biological weapons.
Mistral says it has released the weights of its trillion-parameter multimodal Large 4 model after further training on coding and defensive security tasks. The company reports benchmark gains and adds three reasoning modes to balance capability and inference cost.
DeepSeek’s DeepInfer repository describes an open-source framework supporting DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash on NVIDIA and Huawei hardware. The release includes offline inference and optimized execution features, but says a complete serving system still needs cache management, disaggregation and load balancing.
Robert Haas writes that a surge in bugs found with language models has stretched PostgreSQL's human reviewers and contributed to late feature withdrawals. His October 30 account expected version 19 in November or later, framing the delay as a quality trade-off while questioning how maintainers can keep pace.
In an October 28 report, Dennis Jackson says the Git, curl and Python tools shipped with macOS still accepted affected eMudhra certificates after other Apple networking components had been protected. He also reports that a macOS update reverted a manual workaround, raising concerns for developer and build-system connections.
The source returned a 403 access response, so its contents could not be reviewed. The original Hacker News submission title is retained; no article summary or claims have been inferred from it.

The November 2026 feature follows his efforts to make bread from it and situates the crop within Italy's interest in traditional grains and farming practices.

WIRED reports that Senators Ron Wyden and Adam Schiff accuse the administration of withholding a FISA court opinion beyond its disclosure deadline as Congress considers renewing Section 702. Their letter seeks answers by November 6. The article describes a dispute over access to the ruling and oversight of searches involving Americans' communications.
In an October 22 statement, eMudhra said it would leave the global SSL/TLS certificate market following a certificate incident, citing costs and limited revenue. The company promised replacement certificates through partners and continued status services during the transition, while saying its other trust and enterprise services would continue operating.

He uses estimated ratings to argue it could have surpassed Kasparov, a counterfactual rather than an actual match.

Apple may try to increase revenue by doing…something with third-party AI apps
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
01:49:52 UTC
10:49:52 KST

Apple’s services business remains at record levels, but it’s facing growing pressure. The central part of that business, Apple’s App Store, has faced regulatory pressure and a slump in mobile gaming, which have hurt services growth.
However, on Monday’s earnings call, Apple signaled that it may look to boost services revenue through third-party apps. What does that mean, exactly? Unfortunately, it’s not entirely clear.
When discussing how AI could potentially “contribute to revenue growth over time,” Kevan Parekh, Apple’s chief financial officer, said: “On the product side, you know, we feel AI offers a compelling reason for customers to upgrade and switch in order to get the latest capabilities, and we also feel there’s opportunities across third-party apps and partnerships as well as first-party opportunities.”
Parekh did not elaborate on what sorts of opportunities might be available via third-party apps. However, Reuters reported Monday that investors are wondering whether Apple could profit by distributing third-party AI agents through the App Store and taking a cut of the revenue they generate.
Regulatory and legal changes have cut into App Store commission revenue in some markets, even as Apple’s services business as a whole continues to grow. Third-party estimates in August, for example, suggested the company had seen an 18% drop in U.S. commission revenue, with declines also in Brazil and Japan after those countries introduced new regulations. Apple has traditionally controlled payments within its app marketplace tightly, taking a cut of all App Store transactions. But a lawsuit by Epic Games ultimately forced the company to allow apps to redirect users to outside payment systems. Other legal challenges throughout the world have further hampered the business.
During the earnings call, Parekh referenced Apple’s App Store woes and returned to the idea that the company’s service business could expand into new territory.
“We’ve also seen some factors that impacted the performance of the App Store, and during the September quarter, the performance on the App Store was similar to the prior year, and we had a record number of paid subscriptions with double-digit growth for third-party apps on the App Store,” Parekh said. “At the same time, we did see some headwinds in mobile gaming that we’ve talked about before, and expect to see those continue into the summer quarter.”
Parekh added: “As we move ahead, we expect services growth to be between 8 to 9% [in the December quarter]. This outlook contemplates our best estimates on sequential headwinds, primarily from third-party licensing and the additional half percentage point of foreign exchange I just mentioned.”
TechCrunch has reached out to Apple for more information and will update this story if it responds.

DiscImageStudio: burn visible artwork onto CDs, DVDs and Blu-ray discs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
01:33:43 UTC
10:33:43 KST

Disc Image Studio 是一个多平台的光盘盘面图像生成与刻录工具，帮助您使用任何刻录光驱在任何 DVD / CD / 单层蓝光 光盘上刻蚀出肉眼可见的图像。
Disc Image Studio 可将一张图片转换成符合 CD、DVD 与单层 BD-R 光盘物理层约束的数据，刻录后将使光盘盘面呈现肉眼可见的图案。
Windows 下载 win-x64.exe 后可直接运行。Linux 或 macOS 请下载与 CPU 匹配的实验性 CLI 压缩包，解压后运行 ./disc-image-studio --help。这些发布包均自带 .NET 运行时。 GitHub Releases
在主页选择 CD、DVD 或蓝光，指定输出位置后创建工程。
在设计页导入图片，用图层完成排版，并在右栏「影调」模块调整抖动、伽马、亮度与灰阶档位。
在预览页核对盘面投影，在导出页生成 ISO / RAW / WAV+CUE，或使用即时刻录功能直接写入空白盘片。
即时刻录为实验性功能，请只使用可报废的测试介质。
- CD 支持：支持任何 CD 。您可导出带格式头的 WAV 音轨及配套 CUE，兼容 cdrecord, ImgBurn等刻录软件；也允许导出 RAW 音轨。详见 CD 刻录兼容说明。
- DVD 支持：支持任何 DVD。您可导出 ISO 镜像。
- 蓝光支持：支持任何单层 BD-R 25 GB，支持纯绘图与 UDF 2.01 文件。详见 ADDING_BLURAY.md。
- 随盘文件：CD／DVD／蓝光可在设计页直接选择要保存的文件夹及内圈／外圈位置，也可在导出页调整；设置随工程保存。
- 文件区域预览：混合盘的设计投影和成品读回用黄色标出文件区（含文件系统）。
- 介质灰度方案：CD 与 DVD 均使用黑白二阶，CD 使用 0D / AA，DVD 扰码域使用 A5 / 92。详见 PHYSICAL_MODEL_AUDIT.md。
- 灰度抖动工具：独立程序也可将原图处理为 0/255 黑白二阶 PNG；支持打开或拖入原图、并排预览及无损 PNG 导出。详见使用与构建说明。
- 盘片预设：内置数个品牌的光盘参数，覆盖 CD／DVD／BD。您也可将自己标定好的数据贡献给我们，详见预设维护说明。
Disc Image Studio 提供了一套智能且精准的辅助标定流程，相较人工肉眼识别标定，辅助标定算法将可确定的盘面物理数据精确度从 10-6 提升到了 10-14。校准前采用恒定轨距，十字线的径向线条出现明显弯曲；使用辅助标定得到的三次轨距参数重新生成并刻录后，线条更接近径向直线。
流程与参数说明见辅助标定说明。
- 镜像文件输出：您可以导出完整 ISO / RAW 文件并使用合适的刻录软件手动刻录。
- 即时流式刻录（实验性）：CD、DVD 与 BD-R 边生成边刻录，不保存完整镜像；BD-R 走 MMC 顺序写入。
- 不要使用非空白介质！
- 桌面版需要 Windows 10 2004（2004/20H1，19041）及以上，或 Windows 11。
- 构建：.NET 10 SDK（global.json 锁定 10.0.x）；自行构建的非自包含 GUI 需要 .NET 10 Windows 桌面运行时。Release EXE 已包含运行时，无需另行安装 .NET。
- “即时刻录”功能需要一台 Windows 可识别的 CD / DVD / BD 刻录机与空白盘片；BD-R 需要支持顺序写入的单层空白盘。
dotnet build DiscImageStudio.slnx --configuration Release
dotnet run --project src/DiscImageStudio.App --configuration Release
构建回归检查：
dotnet run --project src/DvdImageSolver --configuration Release -- selftest
dotnet run --project tests/DiscImageStudio.ArchitectureTests --configuration Release
版本规则、CI 生成 EXE、Release 草稿检查、MSIX 打包与签名流程见发布指南。
Linux（glibc）可选 linux-x64 或 linux-arm64。Apple Silicon Mac 选 osx-arm64，Intel Mac 选 osx-x64。CLI 在本地生成镜像，实际写盘可交给支持相应镜像或音轨的刻录软件。
使用 .NET 10 SDK 从源码运行 CLI
dotnet run --project src/DiscImageStudio.Cli -c Release -- --help
CLI 可读取 PNG、JPEG、BMP 和 GIF，使用动画图片的第一帧。下面的例子用两扇区音轨验证安装，同时生成配套 CUE。
./disc-image-studio cd-generate --input picture.png --output sample.wav --sectors 2
各命令的完整参数见对应文档。
src/DiscImageStudio.Cli/     Linux 和 macOS 离线命令行入口
src/DiscImageStudio.ImageIO/ WIC 和跨平台图像编解码
src/DiscImageStudio.Core/    介质无关的模块契约、工程/预设模型、任务与命令目录
src/DiscImageStudio.Imaging/ CD/DVD/BD 共用的图片预处理与抖动辅助
src/DiscImageStudio.Burning/ Windows IMAPI2 设备枚举、流缓冲与安全刻录层
src/DiscImageStudio.Cd/      CD-DA 生成核心与 CD 模块
src/DiscImageStudio.Dvd/     DVD 引擎适配模块
src/DiscImageStudio.BluRay/  BD-R 几何、17PP/ECC、3T/6T 映射、流式生成与 ISO
src/DiscImageStudio.Iso/     UDF 2.01 / ISO9660 写入器
src/DiscImageStudio.App/     WPF GUI、统一命令行与商店资源
src/DvdImageSolver/          保持隔离的 DVD 编码/求解引擎
tests/                       无第三方测试框架的架构回归测试
packaging/                   MSIX 清单与打包脚本
tools/                       独立辅助工具与资产生成脚本
docs/                        架构、引擎、预设、发布等文档
Disc Image Studio 的实现与物理模型主要参考以下规范：
CD 公共编码部分还参考了 ECMA-130，第 2 版（CD-ROM，1996）。
- Disc Image Studio 不安装驱动或服务；“即时刻录”功能使用了 Windows 自带的 IMAPI2，拒绝非空白介质。
- “即时刻录”功能已有部分实盘反馈，对不同设备、介质与刻录入口的验证覆盖仍有限，属于实验性功能，请只使用可报废的测试介质。刻录中断、断电或生成速度不足仍可能使盘片报废！
- 实际可见效果取决于盘片、刻录机、固件与写入策略。
- 混合文件夹会在开始刻录前扫描目录并固定布局；期间源文件大小改变会安全终止任务，已开始写入的盘片仍可能报废。
- BD-R 顺序写入不提供备用区重映射；中断后同一盘片通常不能继续写入。
欢迎报告问题与提交代码。开发环境搭建、模块边界规则、测试要求与 Pull Request 流程见贡献指南。
项目采用 GNU GPL v3.0 or later（GPL-3.0-or-later）。CD 交织实现包含从 CDImage 移植并修改的代码，整体分发沿用兼容的 GPL 授权；分发二进制或修改版时须遵守对应源代码等要求。第三方字体与组件遵循各自许可证，详见 授权说明 和 第三方声明；应用"关于"页也可离线查看完整文本。
感谢 arduinocelentano 的 CDImage 项目，它展示了通过生成 CD 音轨在盘面形成可见图像的方法。本项目的 CD 交织实现由其相关代码移植并继续修改，CD 盘面绘图思路也受其启发。CDImage 原作者及贡献者的工作为本项目提供了重要基础。

DiscImageStudio: burn visible artwork onto CDs, DVDs and Blu-ray discs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
01:33:43 UTC
10:33:43 KST

Disc Image Studio 是一个多平台的光盘盘面图像生成与刻录工具，帮助您使用任何刻录光驱在任何 DVD / CD / 单层蓝光 光盘上刻蚀出肉眼可见的图像。
Disc Image Studio 可将一张图片转换成符合 CD、DVD 与单层 BD-R 光盘物理层约束的数据，刻录后将使光盘盘面呈现肉眼可见的图案。
Windows 下载 win-x64.exe 后可直接运行。Linux 或 macOS 请下载与 CPU 匹配的实验性 CLI 压缩包，解压后运行 ./disc-image-studio --help。这些发布包均自带 .NET 运行时。 GitHub Releases
在主页选择 CD、DVD 或蓝光，指定输出位置后创建工程。
在设计页导入图片，用图层完成排版，并在右栏「影调」模块调整抖动、伽马、亮度与灰阶档位。
在预览页核对盘面投影，在导出页生成 ISO / RAW / WAV+CUE，或使用即时刻录功能直接写入空白盘片。
即时刻录为实验性功能，请只使用可报废的测试介质。
- CD 支持：支持任何 CD 。您可导出带格式头的 WAV 音轨及配套 CUE，兼容 cdrecord, ImgBurn等刻录软件；也允许导出 RAW 音轨。详见 CD 刻录兼容说明。
- DVD 支持：支持任何 DVD。您可导出 ISO 镜像。
- 蓝光支持：支持任何单层 BD-R 25 GB，支持纯绘图与 UDF 2.01 文件。详见 ADDING_BLURAY.md。
- 随盘文件：CD／DVD／蓝光可在设计页直接选择要保存的文件夹及内圈／外圈位置，也可在导出页调整；设置随工程保存。
- 文件区域预览：混合盘的设计投影和成品读回用黄色标出文件区（含文件系统）。
- 介质灰度方案：CD 与 DVD 均使用黑白二阶，CD 使用 0D / AA，DVD 扰码域使用 A5 / 92。详见 PHYSICAL_MODEL_AUDIT.md。
- 灰度抖动工具：独立程序也可将原图处理为 0/255 黑白二阶 PNG；支持打开或拖入原图、并排预览及无损 PNG 导出。详见使用与构建说明。
- 盘片预设：内置数个品牌的光盘参数，覆盖 CD／DVD／BD。您也可将自己标定好的数据贡献给我们，详见预设维护说明。
Disc Image Studio 提供了一套智能且精准的辅助标定流程，相较人工肉眼识别标定，辅助标定算法将可确定的盘面物理数据精确度从 10-6 提升到了 10-14。校准前采用恒定轨距，十字线的径向线条出现明显弯曲；使用辅助标定得到的三次轨距参数重新生成并刻录后，线条更接近径向直线。
流程与参数说明见辅助标定说明。
- 镜像文件输出：您可以导出完整 ISO / RAW 文件并使用合适的刻录软件手动刻录。
- 即时流式刻录（实验性）：CD、DVD 与 BD-R 边生成边刻录，不保存完整镜像；BD-R 走 MMC 顺序写入。
- 不要使用非空白介质！
- 桌面版需要 Windows 10 2004（2004/20H1，19041）及以上，或 Windows 11。
- 构建：.NET 10 SDK（global.json 锁定 10.0.x）；自行构建的非自包含 GUI 需要 .NET 10 Windows 桌面运行时。Release EXE 已包含运行时，无需另行安装 .NET。
- “即时刻录”功能需要一台 Windows 可识别的 CD / DVD / BD 刻录机与空白盘片；BD-R 需要支持顺序写入的单层空白盘。
dotnet build DiscImageStudio.slnx --configuration Release
dotnet run --project src/DiscImageStudio.App --configuration Release
构建回归检查：
dotnet run --project src/DvdImageSolver --configuration Release -- selftest
dotnet run --project tests/DiscImageStudio.ArchitectureTests --configuration Release
版本规则、CI 生成 EXE、Release 草稿检查、MSIX 打包与签名流程见发布指南。
Linux（glibc）可选 linux-x64 或 linux-arm64。Apple Silicon Mac 选 osx-arm64，Intel Mac 选 osx-x64。CLI 在本地生成镜像，实际写盘可交给支持相应镜像或音轨的刻录软件。
使用 .NET 10 SDK 从源码运行 CLI
dotnet run --project src/DiscImageStudio.Cli -c Release -- --help
CLI 可读取 PNG、JPEG、BMP 和 GIF，使用动画图片的第一帧。下面的例子用两扇区音轨验证安装，同时生成配套 CUE。
./disc-image-studio cd-generate --input picture.png --output sample.wav --sectors 2
各命令的完整参数见对应文档。

Ukraine’s new pocket-sized gadget turns Russian drones’ cameras against them
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
01:12:55 UTC
10:12:55 KST

Ukrainian drone teams have a new way to spy on the drones that spy on them. A Kyiv equipment startup called CODE380 has been gaining attention for a pocket-sized receiver, the D·Vision SDR, that picks up live camera video from many Russian drones and shows it on a screen.
Those drones send their video over the air without any scrambling, so anyone with the right receiver can watch along. A Ukrainian unit can see where a drone is heading and what its pilot is aiming at before it hits.
According to Defense News, the device is light, at about 10 ounces, which is less than half the weight of competing Ukrainian models, one of which weighs a couple of pounds.
It also listens on the radio channels Russia’s newer Molniya drones started using for video in June. Serhii “Flash” Beskrestnov, a radio specialist who advises Ukraine’s president, said in a June Facebook post cited by Defense News that “most detectors don’t see these frequencies, or see them poorly.” He warned that putting too much trust in a detector could prove fatal.
Beskrestnov has also warned that digital video could one day replace analog, which would blind receivers like this one. The New York Times reported in August that some Molniyas are AI-guided, using Nvidia chips and emitting no signals.

Y Combinator’s Garry Tan uses AI agents so much, he spends up to $200K a year on tokens
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
00:35:52 UTC
09:35:52 KST

Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan has, in the past, jokingly admitted that he’s suffered from “cyber psychosis” and sometimes barely sleeps because he’s so excited to be working with his AI agents. On a new episode of Walter Isaacson’s podcast The Builders, set to publish on Tuesday, Tan put a price tag on his passion.
“I spend about maybe $100[,000] to $200,000 a year on tokens,” he told Isaacson. He uses agents for countless work projects and coding tasks and has freely shared his set of 23 Claude Code skills, called gstack, on GitHub — an effort to help teach others what agents can do, he said.
On the one hand, he uses his agent the way many people use AI: to help him research Isaacson’s podcast and prep for the kinds of questions he might be asked. On the other, his agent also helps him run his life.
“It’s read all of my email and all of my Dropbox. It’s read, like, all of my Apple Notes, even my therapy notes going back 20 years,” he said.
He describes using AI agents as getting help with “almost anything that you do in your day-to-day life, and for it to do it somewhat more autonomously. Think of it as a Jiminy Cricket on your shoulder at all times. Like, almost anything you want to do, it knows you, and it’s there to help. And it will actually sort of run ahead and say, ‘What about this?’ Or it’ll help you see around corners.”
As an early OpenClaw adopter who is now a power user, Tan says one thing he’s learned is not to treat AI agents like traditional software. Some people are so worried about “repeatability and safety that they actually trade off all of the beautiful parts of working with an AI agent, which basically can be so similar to a human,” he said.
For instance, Y Combinator is also using an agent that is “rebuilding the way we operate at YC,” he said. “We now have an internal AI agent, for instance, named Stray Light that kind of has a personality, and then it has a company brain. Like it actually has the total context of every founder we’ve ever backed, anyone who’s ever emailed us, like all the applications.”
Tan, who is, naturally, an avid lover of technology (as anyone running Silicon Valley’s famed startup accelerator would be), believes personal AI agents will eventually become mainstream, if one major problem is solved: Costs have got to come down.
“It’s too expensive right now,” he said, adding that he knows the amount of money he spends on AI agents is “a crazy privilege that not everyone can do.”
But freebie agents controlled by Big Tech maybe aren’t the answer, either. When discussing the future he’d like tech to build, and how Meta as a social media company has impacted us, he said, “This is a huge company that controls the algorithm, and we all wish that they didn’t at some level,” he said. “So you could imagine a personal AI that you control, that you program.”

Hinge tests a cheaper plan as users become more fed up paying for dating apps
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
00:14:51 UTC
09:14:51 KST

Hinge is testing a cheaper subscription plan as its parent company Match Group looks for new ways to get users to pay for dating apps at a time when people are increasingly tired of subscriptions and, in some cases, dating apps altogether.
During its third-quarter 2026 earnings call on Monday, Match Group said the new Hinge tier will include features such as additional filters and custom location boundaries, giving users more control over who they see and where they meet people.
“It’s designed for women in particular, but also for price-sensitive users,” said Steve Bailey, chief financial officer of Match Group. “Remember that Hinge is one of the highest-priced products we have. The revenue per payer, for example, at Hinge is almost twice that of Tinder, around $30. It also has one of the lower payer penetrations. Its payer penetration is only slightly more than half that of Tinder.”
Match Group didn’t share how much the new subscription will cost. TechCrunch has reached out to the company for more details.
The move comes as dating apps continue to struggle to convince people to pay for services they’re growing tired of using. Between subscription fatigue, endless swiping, and frustration with the overall dating experience, users are increasingly questioning whether premium dating features are worth the money.
Interestingly, Hinge is making this move despite being one of Match Group’s strongest performers. The app had a much better quarter than Tinder, with global monthly active users growing 12% year over year, direct revenue increasing 19%, and paying users rising 23% to 2.3 million. The app generated $221 million in direct revenue and had 2.3 million paid subscribers as of the end of the quarter. Direct revenue is money earned from subscriptions and in-app purchases.
By comparison, Tinder brought in $480 million in direct revenue but experienced a slight 2% decline year-over-year, although it has 8.9 million paid users, a 4% decline from a year earlier.
The lower-priced subscription could help Hinge build on that momentum by converting more users into paying customers without requiring them to spend as much upfront.
The move also marks a shift in Hinge’s subscription strategy. In 2023, the company experimented with a more expensive tier aimed at users willing to pay extra for features designed to help them get more out of the app. Now, it’s testing whether a more affordable option could appeal to a broader audience.
Still, cheaper subscriptions alone are unlikely to solve the bigger problem facing dating apps: user fatigue. Match Group says it’s seeing some signs of improvement, partly thanks to efforts to make its platforms feel less like traditional dating apps.
Recent initiatives include Group Hangouts and Events, which offer users alternative ways to connect beyond the usual one-on-one matching experience. Match Group plans to ramp up those efforts in the fourth quarter.
Another initiative on the horizon is real-time adaptive recommendations, which the company plans to begin testing in the fourth quarter. Announced during its CEO Connection presentation in September, the feature will allow Tinder to respond to user actions within seconds rather than hours, with the goal of making recommendations more relevant and personalized.

Intel stock collapse worsens as $3.5T chip rout spreads
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-03
00:08:53 UTC
09:08:53 KST

Wall Street mostly swept up last week's tech wreckage Monday. Chip stocks kept tracking mud back through the house.
About 7 in 10 S&P 500 stocks rose Monday, yet nearly every chip stock fell, and the group shed more than $400 billion in market value in a single session, by Yahoo Finance calculations.
Intel (INTC) led the way down again.
The chipmaker slid about 9% after its worst day since 1974 on Oct. 30, when a disappointing forecast knocked nearly 29% off the stock. Monday's close left Intel worth less than half of what it was when September ended.
And the damage didn't stick to Intel.
Micron (MU), Sandisk (SNDK), Seagate (STX), and Western Digital (WDC) — the memory and storage names that led the chip boom this year — all got clobbered, falling roughly 6% to 8%. Marvell (MRVL) fell even harder, a drop that came as it agreed to buy Lumentum (LITE) in a $115 billion deal. Nvidia (NVDA) held up better than most, but it's still down about 16% since Oct. 6.
Zoom out and the mess gets bigger. Chip and memory stocks have lost about $3.7 trillion in market value since Oct. 6, by Yahoo Finance calculations. Nvidia lost the most in dollars, but Intel's drop of nearly half is the steepest among the giants.
Not everything tied to AI bled. SpaceX (SPCX) rose, though it's still well below where it started October. Software was mixed, apart from cybersecurity stocks like Fortinet (FTNT) and CrowdStrike (CRWD), which fell with chips.
Adam Johnson, founder of the Bullseye Brief and portfolio manager of the American Ingenuity Fund, thinks last week's sell-off already priced in the worst.
"The market kitchen-sinked it," Johnson told Yahoo Finance on Monday. "They discounted everything that could go wrong. And when you do that, typically that's when you see a bottom."
John Chambers, founder and CEO of JC2 Ventures and Cisco's former CEO, expects the gap between winners and losers to keep widening.
"There will be major train wrecks," Chambers told Yahoo Finance, also on Monday. "And rising tide does not raise all boats. I would not bet against Nvidia."
Stock Trader's Almanac editor Jeff Hirsch, who coined the term "Octoberphobia," bought the sell-off on Oct. 30. History is on his side. Chips have climbed in all eight midterm cycles since 1994, measured from the last October close through the following June, by Yahoo Finance calculations. But in 2002 and 2018, they first fell about another 11%.
Next up is AMD (AMD), which reports third quarter results Tuesday after the bell, the same day as the midterm vote.

Snap Reaches Settlement In Social Media Addiction Lawsuit Ahead of Trial

Snap Reaches Settlement In Social Media Addiction Lawsuit Ahead of Trial (cbsnews.com) 5
			
		 	
				Snap has settled a lawsuit brought by two teenagers who alleged Snapchat was deliberately designed to be addictive and contributed to their mental health problems, avoiding a trial that was scheduled to begin this week. Snap was the last defendant to settle after Meta, Alphabet and TikTok resolved claims in the same case. CBS News reports:  The teenage plaintiffs, identified in court documents as S.J. and K.D.B., had alleged in the suit that Snapchat was designed to be addictive and that using the platform from a young age contributed to their mental health issues. "We are glad to reach resolution for our clients," Mark Lanier, founder of The Lanier Law Firm and lead counsel for the plaintiffs, said in a statement to CBS News. "We believe this jury would have found the companies' conduct reprehensible."
Snap was the last social media company named in the lawsuit to settle the case, which was set for opening arguments this week. Meta resolved claims last week, while Alphabet and TikTok, the other defendants, each also resolved the allegations. Terms of the Snap settlement were undisclosed [...].  
Earlier this year, a jury found Meta and Google-owned YouTube liable in a landmark social media addiction lawsuit, awarding $6 million in damages to a young woman who alleged the platforms' addictive features harmed her mental health. In August, Meta also agreed to pay up to $18 billion in settlements and introduce new child-safety measures to resolve claims from dozens of U.S. states that it deliberately designed Facebook and Instagram to addict children while misleading the public about their risks.

Scaling Git at OpenAI
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-02
23:47:56 UTC
08:47:56 KST

Scaling Git at OpenAI
Building agentic-scale source control while investing in the Git ecosystem.
Over the past year, the size and demands on our monorepo have increased at a staggering pace. It was clear that we needed to revamp our source control system to scale to the level of concurrent work that Codex makes possible.
We built Pando, a new backend for Git repositories, without interrupting any engineering work or changing how our team uses Git. Pando now powers all development in OpenAI’s monorepo. Pando is designed to meet two ambitious goals: first, horizontally scalable writes, optimized for concurrent pushes to many branches. And second, maximize read throughput, so clients can download data fast enough to fully saturate their available network bandwidth.
We met these goals while retaining compatibility with the Git protocol and client, enabling both our engineers and agents to seamlessly adopt Pando into their existing workflows. In production, Pando cut median push latency by nearly 5x. It now delivers nearly a petabyte of Git data per day.
Here, we’ll cover our motivations for building a new source-control system, along with why we chose to retain compatibility with Git. We’ll compare our system with common approaches to scaling Git, describing why Pando is uniquely capable of meeting OpenAI’s scalability needs. We also discuss what’s next, and how we’ll continue contributing to Git to support agent-scale work.
Codex has changed the scale and pace of development at OpenAI, enabling engineers to do far more work in parallel. That means more branches and pushes, more merges, and more fetches and clones from development environments, research jobs, and CI.
These demands compound. One engineer now has several changes ready for CI at once, and each push can set several jobs fetching the same commit.
Starting around the end of last year, the systems supporting our monorepo were struggling to keep up. We suffered slow fetches and pushes along with failed merges, with reliability at times falling below 90%. Had we reached a limit of Git itself, or of the way we served our monorepo? Git grew out of a community that exchanged patches to the kernel via email. For more than two decades, developers have adapted their tools around Git as the way they build software has changed. When we started to feel friction in our own monorepo, it mattered to us that we could keep building on that work.
We saw a way to keep that familiar workflow while rebuilding the backend to handle pushes to different branches in parallel and serve the same history to many readers concurrently. Building on Git as the foundation of our source-control workflow meant that improvements to the client could be useful beyond OpenAI. We wanted to contribute those improvements from our copy of Git(opens in a new window) to upstream, sharing them with the broader community.
Scaling a source control system has two essential dimensions: many writers pushing to different branches, and many readers fetching the same history. Pando optimizes for both.
First, Pando coordinates updates to each branch independently. Updates to unrelated branches can proceed in parallel, and a reader fetching a branch gets the history needed for that revision. The tradeoff is that a reader looking at several branches can see them at different points of time.
Second, Pando reuses packfiles (Git’s format for compressing multiple objects together) across fetches instead of generating an individual packfile for each client. A client may download some objects it already has, but the server can serve the same bytes to many clients with little computation.
These choices let us scale writes across branches and serve reads from caches. To see how they fit together, let’s start with how Git stores and transfers history.
Git must be able to keep two parts of a repository in sync: objects and references.
A Git repository records snapshots of files and the history connecting them as a graph of objects. An object may be either a blob, tree, commit, or tag. Blobs hold the content of an individual file, and trees associate names with blobs or other trees to form a directory structure. Commits point to a root tree and zero or more parent commits, forming the history of your repository.
Objects are identified by a hash of their type and contents. Objects are also immutable: recording a changed file creates a new blob, while unchanged files and directories can reuse existing objects.
Branches and tags are references, or “refs”, that point to commits. Committing on a branch creates a new commit and updates the branch’s ref to point to it.
Git stores objects in either a “loose” or “packed” state. Loose objects are stored in individual files identified by their object ID. Packed objects are stored together in a packfile, enabling similar objects to be stored as deltas against one another.
In Git, an object is reachable from a commit if Git can find it by following links from that commit through its parents, trees, and blobs. During a fetch, the client tells the server which commits it wants and which it already has. The server finds the objects reachable from the wanted commits, excludes those it knows the client already has, and sends the remainder as a packfile in the same format Git uses on disk. A full clone starts without any objects, so it needs all the objects reachable from the history being cloned.
A push transfers objects in the other direction by asking the server to update one or more refs. The client uses the server’s set of advertised refs to avoid sending objects the server already has. The pack being sent can therefore depend on existing objects: receiving the pack alone is not enough to reconstruct the new history.
Replicating a Git repository means keeping both objects and references available across multiple servers. Before a replica can serve a reference’s new value, it must be able to supply the objects reachable from that value. The service also needs to coordinate competing reference updates, i.e., pushes that try to move the same branch from the same old tip to different new commits. Systems that impose a single order on all writes to a repository can make pushes to unrelated branches wait on one another.
While designing Pando, we evaluated conventional approaches for scaling source control using Git: one that coordinates ref updates across repository replicas using three-phase commit (3PC, a protocol for coordinating a commit across multiple nodes), and one that orders updates through a shared write-ahead log (WAL, a record of changes written before they’re applied) in blob storage.
In the three-phase commit design we modeled, the Git service keeps a complete copy of the repository on several replicas. When any server receives a push, the incoming packfile is copied and sent to all replicas in parallel. To publish the push, a majority of replicas must accept the ref update. The server managing this is the coordinator, and it requires multiple rounds of communication between the coordinator and each other replica.
To accept a reference update, each replica locks its copy of the ref and checks that it still points to the commit the client expects. The coordinator then sends a pre-commit message to the replicas that accept. Once a majority acknowledges the pre-commit message, the coordinator tells them to perform the update. Any up-to-date replica can then advertise the new branch tip from its local copy of the repository.
Keeping complete replicas makes it possible to serve reads locally, and guarantees that clients get a consistent view of all references no matter which replica they pick. As a consequence, references are updated one at a time, and each push requires multiple round trips to coordinate the ref update among replicas.
Waiting for a quorum puts replica disk performance and communication latency on the push path. As write volume grows, the replicas must keep up with incoming packs as well as read traffic.
In the WAL-based design we modeled, the Git service uses a shared manifest in blob storage to order updates across the repository. When a server receives a push, it uploads the incoming packfile to a blob store and prepares the ref update in its local copy of the repository. To publish the push, it adds an entry to a shared manifest stored in the blob store that records the ref update and points to the packfile. The Git server then updates the manifest using a compare-and-swap operation. This atomically replaces the manifest, or otherwise rejects the change. If another writer has updated it in the meantime, the server reads the intervening entries, checks that its ref update is still valid, and retries using the packfile it already uploaded.
Writers can upload their packfiles in parallel, but even pushes to unrelated branches require updating the same manifest. The round-trip time for a successful compare-and-swap limits how often the repository can publish a new manifest. A low-latency blob store makes this step fast. Batching lets one manifest update several refs at the same time, at the cost of queueing updates and increasing latency.
The blob store can receive packfiles in parallel, but updates to the shared manifest still compete with one another. Increasing the blob store’s capacity alone doesn’t resolve that bottleneck. Each replica still needs to read and index packs from the blob store before it can serve them locally. If incoming writes outpace that work, replicas fall behind and clients wait longer to fetch newly pushed commits.
That brings us to Pando. Pando is a new architecture for serving Git repositories with the goal of scaling to many clients pushing simultaneously. Here’s how it works:
Prior to developing Pando, we had a hunch that we could meet our requirements without compromising on Git, but we did need to compromise on some of the things that most Git systems assume to be requirements:
- Repository-wide consistency: Providing a consistent, point-in-time snapshot of all refs requires coordination across the repository. In a system that orders all ref updates together, pushes to unrelated branches contend on a single coordination point.
- Unique packfiles for every client: If two clients with different local histories fetch the same branch, the server may need to generate a different pack for each to avoid sending objects they already have. Creating these responses costs CPU and disk I/O, making it difficult to scale to high volumes of readers.
Instead, Pando provides a relaxed consistency model (causal consistency) to scale writes, and reuses packfiles to scale reads.
When a client pushes to a branch, Pando stores the incoming packfile as an immutable blob in a blob store. Each time the branch moves, Pando records a ref version containing the new tip, the pack that supplied it, and any earlier ref versions the pack depends on in a metadata store. Both the blob store and metadata store are horizontally scalable.
Fetching a commit or reference from Pando requires replaying a set of ref versions (packfiles) to the client, reconstructing the state of the client that created the ref version being fetched. This provides a weaker form of consistency across the entire repository, while providing serializability for updates to individual refs. Clients only conflict when pushing to the same ref simultaneously. A client reading several branches cannot assume those reads describe a single point in time across the repository.
This is a form of causal consistency. In order to fetch a reference, the reader observes the history of the writer that produced it. During a push, Pando controls what shared history the client and server agree on, ensuring clients produce more easily shared packfiles.
As a consequence, Pando doesn’t provide a point-in-time state of all references during Git operations or an ordering of ref updates within a clone or fetch. Concretely, suppose that branches foo and bar are both updated during the negotiation of a full clone. During the negotiation, one client may observe foo at version 1 and bar at version 2, while another client observes foo at version 2 and bar at version 1. In practice, users and agents are agnostic to the global ordering of ref updates, and instead depend on the order of updates to a single reference.
In this simulation of the three consistency models we configure 1,000 clients to each select a distinct branch and then repeatedly push unique commits, measuring total wall clock duration and push to fetch latency. The latter is an important metric for workloads, as it measures round trip time for users and agents to collaborate or to use other systems which rely on version control. The simulation models hardware ranging from commodity to premium virtual machines and blob storage systems. In this simulation, we’ve relaxed repository-wide consistency for three-phase commit.
The modeled three-phase commit system shows significantly greater write latency under load as their disks saturate; meanwhile, write-ahead log systems show significantly greater read latency for the same reason, as servers struggle to keep up with reading from the journal. In both real world testing on our monorepo and in this simulation, Pando supports many thousands of clients able to push concurrently without impacting performance under load.
On handling a push, Pando validates the incoming packfile while streaming it to blob storage, without unpacking it into a local repository. When a client begins a fetch Pando selects the stored packs the reader needs, and the reader downloads them. No server needs to apply packfiles to a local repository or perform costly unpack and packing operations. Because these servers primarily perform negotiation and packfile selection, Pando servers can run on commodity hardware.
This allows Pando to scale to the limits of clients, the network between clients and Pando, and the blob storage service. With enough capacity, clients working on independent branches can each transfer packs as fast as their own connection allows. To serve more clients at that rate, we can add Pando servers and scale the metadata store and blob store alongside them.
Pushing and Fetching a Branch in Pando
Pando stores the writer’s packfile in blob storage and records the ref version in metadata service. On fetch, Pando selects the packs the reader needs, and Express serves them.
An agent edits files and creates a commit locally and runs git push, which creates a packfile of those changes to update a branch. Pando receives the pack and streams it to blob storage. After validating the incoming push with a single pass decode, Pando uses a multi-key transaction to record a ref version in our metadata store and atomically update the ref pointer. This enables Pando to provide “read-your-own-write” consistency. This entire write operation requires \(O(1)\) metadata reads and writes.
When another client fetches the branch, Pando selects a set of stored packs using the history the client already has. This operation requires \(O(\log \Delta)\) metadata reads, \(\Delta\) being the shortest distance in ref versions between what the client has and what the client wants. The server returns packfile URIs, and the client downloads them through a service, Express, that stores packfiles in regional caches.
This design relies on the three central services of Pando’s architecture: pushes, fetches, and compaction.
When a client pushes, a conventional Git server is like a poker player showing all of its cards, revealing to the client every branch it has. The client then gathers the commits, trees, and blobs to create a packfile that excludes objects it knows the server already has.
Advertising more refs can make the push smaller. It can also make the pack harder to reuse, because another client may be missing objects the writer left out.
Pando takes a different approach. Instead of showing its full hand, it only advertises a select set of branches to the client. This is done for two reasons:
- Providing a consistent view of every ref is expensive. With millions of branches, the ref advertisement alone can exceed 100 MiB. Providing this requires each server to either maintain a current mapping, requiring significant coordination overhead, or a database query that scales with the number of refs.
- By providing only a subset of the refs to the client, the client will generate a pack that depends only on what the server explicitly allows the client to exclude. Tracking the dependencies allows Pando to reuse the packfile a client generates in one push by serving it as the response to a subsequent fetch.
As a Git server advertises more refs, clients pushing have more sources from which to exclude objects, enabling them to produce smaller packs. However, reusing these packfiles requires the server to be able to prove the clients fetching have each of the dependencies. Otherwise, the packfile may omit objects the client needs.
On the other hand, if a Git server advertises none at all, the client would generate a packfile containing the repository’s full history. That packfile can be used by any other client, but at the significant cost of making fetching any branch effectively a full clone.
By default, when a client pushes a new feature branch, Pando advertises only a designated trunk branch as a base. This keeps the advertisement the same size as the repository gains branches, but comes with tradeoffs. When an engineer either pushes to a single branch many times, or stacks one feature branch on top of another the client has to resend everything outside of the trunk.
This causes successive branch pushes to be larger, containing potentially-redundant objects, and uses additional client bandwidth. In our monorepo, the median packfile produced this way was under 20 KiB, and the 99th percentile was under 3 MiB. For us, this was a sensible trade-off because a reader that already has the advertised trunk history can fetch the branch with that one packfile.
After choosing the set of refs to advertise to the client, Pando accepts the push and updates its metadata store. Pando splits the metadata update into synchronous and asynchronous portions. The synchronous metadata update ensures clients can immediately fetch the newly updated commit. This writes a new ref version and updates the ref pointer mapping to that new version storing that new tip, the dependencies used to negotiate the packfile, and a link to the packfile that was pushed.
At the same time, asynchronous work is recorded in the same transaction, such as scheduling compaction. For very large pushes, only a subset of commits are written synchronously. This allows us to require only a constant number of reads and writes to the metadata store, regardless of the size of the push.
Importantly, Pando servers can accept pushes to unrelated refs independently. The only contended key is the ref pointer, which we guard using an atomic compare-and-swap-like operation. We can scale up write throughput by adding Pando servers, metadata store scaling units, or blob stores.
When a client performs a fetch, it provides the server with two sets of commit IDs, known in the protocol as haves and wants. In a conventional Git server, the server computes the set difference among the objects reachable by the haves and those reachable by the wants, compresses them, and sends the result to the client. While the open-source Git server has a variety of indexes locally to accelerate this operation, these packfiles can be expensive to compute and are generally resistant to caching.
Instead, Pando uses the commit mappings recorded on pushes to look up the client’s haves and wants, resolving them to a set of ref versions that contain those commits. Each of those ref versions links to a packfile previously received by Pando. The server uses this to solve for what the client needs, selecting the packfiles that cover the set of objects the client needs.
Planning fetches in Pando
Pando compares what a client has with what it wants, then selects the stored packfiles needed—including any missing branch dependencies.
Fetch 27 trunk packs.
Select a branch to include its trunk dependencies.
The Git protocol only allows a single packfile to be sent in the HTTP response on a clone or fetch. However, an under-appreciated feature of the Git protocol, packfile-uri, allows a server to return links to download additional packs separately. Pando uses this exclusively, and transmits an empty packfile inline. To support this feature, OpenAI contributed a number of improvements to packfile-uri upstream.
OpenAI systems operate in many geographic regions, so we maintain Pando clusters with local caches in each. A pack delivery service, Express, caches our blob store, and a separate service caches the metadata store. These caches enable Pando to serve requests for a commit ID or a special read of a ref entirely from the cached state in that cluster. We use consistent hashing to route requests for a packfile to Express servers, with cache hit rates of over 99%.
This system of caches provides extremely high throughput, capable of saturating the network limit between clients and Pando. Using these packfile URIs and local caches, we’ve validated that using only standard cloud provider virtual machines, we see clone and fetch operations saturate 10 Gbps network links.
For most Git servers, repacking and garbage collection are necessary to maintain the performance of a growing repository. Both of these steps reclaim space, but are compute- and I/O-intensive.
In a fast-moving repository like OpenAI’s monorepo, it is infeasible to send an individual packfile URI for every push that has occurred since the last fetch. Each day, a client would need to download tens of thousands of packfiles to catch up their trunk.
Instead, Pando combines packs in the background to reduce this overhead, adopting a design similar to compaction in log-structured merge-trees. Each push produces what Pando refers to as an “L0” packfile, representing the smallest unit of data Pando can transmit. Over time, background workers collect these packfiles into covers spanning a sequence of ref versions, scaling geometrically from L0. Today, our monorepo uses up to L4 packfile covers.
These covers serve a dual purpose. After a cover is sufficiently full, a compaction worker will claim it and produce a single packfile. This improves the compression ratio by reorganizing the objects inside the packfile. But it also improves fetch negotiation performance by acting as skip list over ref versions. By scanning through indexes at each level simultaneously, Pando can select a set of packs to send to a client in logarithmic time. These packfile covers, repacked or not, enable a more efficient solution to fetch planning, reducing both throughput and latency requirements for Pando.
Compaction in Pando
Small packfiles are grouped into larger covers across levels, improving compression and speeding up fetches.
Unlike many Git servers, packfiles are kept at every level and not discarded or garbage collected. Packs at every level are used to serve clients continuously, based on the haves and wants they request in a fetch. And for clients using Pando as an API, reading an arbitrary object from a packfile tends to require reading less data from an L0 than an L4, because it’s less likely to be compressed.
Compaction in Pando significantly reduces both the total transfer size sent to clients and enables pack planning to scale sub-linearly in how far behind the client is. This is especially important for large and quickly changing repositories, which we expect to be a new normal in software development.
We built Pando with Codex, moving from kick-off to production, in just 10 weeks. The core of Pando was designed by a single engineer, implemented by teams of agents, and deployed by a handful of engineers. Pando began serving production traffic in April of this year, and is one part of a larger system of source control management tools used at OpenAI.
We employed a wide variety of approaches when validating Pando’s design. We applied our principles for building more secure software foundations. As a greenfield project, we chose to implement Pando in Rust and without legacy dependencies. We wrote a conformance test suite so agents could test their changes against standard Git behavior. We also developed deterministic simulation testing harnesses allowing us to check invariants and properties of the system, enabling us to find and mitigate subtle race conditions and regressions in negotiation and pack planning.
Many models contributed to this system; as new models became available, we used them to improve Pando’s performance and reliability. Using GPT‑6 Astra, agents wrote Lean and TLA+ to formalize a “theory of Pando” that we used to find and fix bugs in the implementation, and prove properties of the system. Multiple continuous sessions ran for hundreds of hours finding and fixing edge cases in Pando. We also run agents around the clock to red-team Git and Pando using Daybreak-capable models.
Our initial deployment of Pando was as a read- and write-through proxy to our existing source control system. This allowed us to keep Pando and the previous system in sync, so we could safely move traffic onto Pando without downtime.
As part of its caching design, Pando can also deduplicate and combine requests before proxying. This allowed us to keep our existing tools healthy as we moved systems to Pando one at a time. As we migrated more workloads to Pando, their pushes populated Pando’s cache and improved the reliability of our previous backend.
In order to optimize performance when using Pando, we began shipping our public distribution of Git to developer machines and within the ChatGPT desktop app. To migrate clients to Pando, we used an internal wrapper around the Git CLI to migrate clients in phases. We created many other tools for handling change requests, code review, and APIs for automations. We migrated hundreds of integrations that depended on legacy APIs, webhooks, and repository downloads alongside Git traffic.
Codex itself helped developers and teams make the move. We created skills for preparing existing checkouts, and for guiding agents to use the new system. As a result, developers could simply ask Codex to migrate their development environments and services.
Switching to Pando improved merge reliability and reduced the latency of the median push by nearly 80%.
Pando can deliver a petabyte of Git data per day with just a handful of CPU cores. Pando has served more than 57 terabytes of Git packs in a single hour, with more than 99% of those bytes from local disk cache.
Reusing packs lets many clients download the same repository history with little computation on the serving nodes. This example shows a single repository, but Pando is designed to support many more. As demand grows, we can scale data delivery and Git negotiation independently.
Building Pando on the Git protocol and client helped us identify bugs and opportunities to improve Git’s own performance.
For example, Pando serves reusable packs directly to clients, so downloading and indexing those packs became a central part of making the system fast and reliable. That work surfaced edge cases in concurrent downloads, cleanup after interrupted transfers, and resuming downloads that had already completed. We fixed those paths, added validations when accepting an existing pack for a fetch, and contributed that work back upstream.
We also needed to see where time and bandwidth were going, so we added tracing for packfile-uri downloads and byte counts for pack generation, making it easier to tell whether a slow fetch was spending its time generating packs or transferring them.
In OpenAI’s Git build(opens in a new window), the client can download and index independent packs in parallel. We added the concept of “explicit haves” so a server can advertise a branch’s current tip separately from the history a pushing client may leave out of its pack. On Pando, this enables clients to use --force-with-lease to check that a feature branch hasn’t moved and build its pack against trunk alone. Anyone with that trunk history can then fetch the branch using the same pack.
Several of these improvements are already in upstream Git, and many others in our public repository. We’re continuing to work with the Git community to make these improvements useful beyond Pando. You can follow that work in our public patch tracker(opens in a new window).
By rethinking conventional wisdom on how to scale Git repositories, Pando enables us to scale our monorepo without changing the Git workflows our engineers and agents already use. In our monorepo, Pando has unlocked a greater degree of parallelism, improved merge reliability, and reduced the time it takes to publish code changes. Beyond our monorepo, we’re also exploring other uses for Pando within OpenAI, including workloads where large numbers of agents need to collaborate on a shared Git repository.
As engineers and agents produce more software, more teams beyond OpenAI will encounter the limits we hit in source control. We’re currently exploring ways to share the technology that makes Pando possible with our users.
Pando builds on two decades of experience from users and operators of Git repositories. We’ll continue to develop Pando while working with the open-source Git community to share any improvements that benefit Git users more broadly.
